

===== README.md =====

# moeme — edge sparsity feasibility for Qwen3.8-27B

A reproducible study of whether Qwen3.8-27B-class quality can be delivered on an
NVIDIA RTX 4060 Laptop (8 GB VRAM) / i7-13620H / 15.4 GiB RAM / 1 TB NVMe
machine, using no paid compute.

Everything in this repository was measured or computed on that machine. Results
are committed under `results/` so the claims can be checked against raw data.

---

## Headline findings

1. **Qwen3.8-27B is dense, not Mixture-of-Experts.** Its non-FFN text backbone
   alone is **9,783,238,144 params**, active on every token. A "9B active per
   token" target is therefore **arithmetically unreachable** from this model, no
   matter how good the router is. This is an executable test, not an opinion.
2. **Post-hoc FFN sparsification has no usable operating point.** At 12.5%
   active compute, a *perfect oracle* router still loses **42–81%** of the layer
   output. The router is not the bottleneck (oracle-vs-learned gap ≤ 0.033), so
   better routing cannot fix it.
3. **The target model runs at 0.561 tok/s today** (Q4_K_M), and prefill at
   1.6 tok/s. Independently predicted from hardware constants at 0.48 tok/s.
4. **The bottleneck is storage streaming, not compute.** The SSD measures
   **0.8–1.9 GB/s** against a ~6.6 GB/s datasheet figure, and 2.2 GB/token has
   to come off it at Q4_K_M. *This is the whole problem.*
5. **The fix is aggressive quantisation to full residency, not sparsity.**
   Below ~3.4 bpw the model fits entirely in VRAM+RAM, which predicts
   4.9 tok/s at Q3_K_M and 6.4 tok/s at Q2_K — an ~9x gain with **no training
   and no architecture change**.

6. **Converting the 27B into a MoE does not rescue the goal, and training does
   not rescue the conversion.** Training only beat the frozen sparsity ceiling at
   the *mildest* ratio tested (E=8, 12.5% active, by 0.006). At the ratios a
   conversion actually needs it makes the reconstruction **worse**: E=32 (3.1%
   active) 0.9188 → 0.9355 and E=64 (1.6%) 0.9277 → 0.9549, reproducible across
   seeds. The trained 0.8B candidate is still **5.6–10.4x** worse than dense in
   perplexity. Measured in E14 (locally, 4 layers) and E15 (24 layers, free
   Kaggle T4s), not argued.

Read `docs/feasibility_report.md` then `docs/decision_memo.md`. For the MoE
question specifically, `docs/upcycling_design.md` (the plan) and
`docs/prior_work.md` (what the field actually achieved, and why E15's negative
result is about budget and configuration rather than feasibility).

## Repository layout

```
src/moeme/ffn.py            dense-equivalent FFN partitioning, partition plans,
                            oracle + learned-router diagnostics, concentration
src/moeme/accounting.py     exact parameter accounting, memory-tier throughput model
src/moeme/branch.py         the *branching* alternative: complete experts (tile or
                            replica init), shared experts, forced-expert mode for
                            independent training, residual_report diagnostics
tests/                      243 tests: exactness, shapes, plans, oracle bounds,
                            accounting, upcycling (dense limit, gradients, aux loss,
                            half precision, CUDA autocast), branching (exact and
                            narrow limits, shared experts, forced-expert training),
                            PoC driver budget and error resilience
tools/verify_model_geometry.py   exact param accounting from HF safetensors headers
tools/hf_tensor.py               fetch single tensors via HTTP range requests
tools/bench_hardware.py          non-destructive hardware characterisation
tools/bench_inference.py         end-to-end quantised inference benchmark (ollama)
tools/residency_table.py         what quantisation fits resident, and the tok/s that follows
tools/summarise_results.py       re-print every headline number from results/*.json
experiments/verify_real_ffn.py   partitioning verified on REAL 27B weights
experiments/pilot_sparsity.py    sparsity pilot with real activations
experiments/upcycle_poc.py       upcycling PoC: does TRAINING beat the frozen ceiling?
experiments/branch_poc.py        branching vs slicing, measured head-to-head, plus
                                 branch-train-miX (experts trained apart, then mixed)
experiments/eval_gate.py         evaluation set with EXECUTABLE code checks
kaggle/upcycle_poc.ipynb         the same PoC, run on free Kaggle GPUs (E15);
kaggle/README.md, kaggle_run.log  how to run it, and the raw session log
configs/tiers.json               measured constants, with provenance and limitations
docs/                            source validation, feasibility report, decision memo,
                                 upcycling design, branching-vs-slicing, expert
                                 offloading, quality-vs-speed (MoE vs dense on a
                                 fixed task set, plus engine placement),
                                 prior-work review of the MoE route
results/                         raw evidence: JSON + printed tables
```

## Quickstart

```bash
make venv        # .venv reusing system torch, plus pytest
make test        # 243 tests, offline, ~8 s
make doctor      # one-command local diagnostics (~90 s, writes results/hardware.json)
make bench-inference
make verify-sources      # re-derive parameter counts from the real artefacts
```

Optional, requires network and (for `pilot`) a GPU:

```bash
make verify-real-ffn   # 535 MB ranged download; exactness on real 27B weights
make pilot             # ~1.8 GB download; real activations
make upcycle-selftest  # upcycling PoC pipeline check, offline, ~2 s (no download)
make upcycle           # upcycling PoC on a real model (GPU; or use kaggle/)
make branch-selftest   # branching-vs-slicing check, offline, CPU, ~2 s (no download)
make branch            # branching vs slicing on a real model (GPU)
make eval MODEL=qwen3:4b NUM_PREDICT=1536   # 30-task gate, executable code checks

# A model too slow for the full gate: same tasks, fixed settings, resumable.
make eval MODEL=qwen3.8:latest NUM_PREDICT=192 \
  EVAL_EXTRA="--think off --tasks r_lily,f_json_nulls,i_only_word --resume"
```

`--think off` is what makes a thinking model evaluable at a small `num_predict`,
and the gate checkpoints after every task, so an interrupted multi-hour run keeps
what it has and continues with `--resume`.

### The MoE question: does *training* change the answer? — answered, and the answer is no

**Measured (E14 + E15): training buys a small win only at the mildest sparsity,
and loses at the ratio a 27B conversion actually needs.** On real Qwen3.5-0.8B
with `combine=sum`, `plan=random`, k=1:

| E | active | frozen pilot oracle | oracle *trained* | router *trained* | beats frozen |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|:--|
| 8 | 12.5% | 0.8624 | **0.8560** | 0.8951 | yes, by 0.006 |
| 32 | 3.13% | 0.9188 | **0.9355** | 0.9653 | **no** (+0.017) |
| 64 | 1.56% | 0.9277 | **0.9549** | 0.9806 | **no** (+0.027) |

(The residual is the relative L2 error of the k-expert reconstruction against the
true dense FFN output — 0 is exact, 1 is as bad as predicting zero. `beats
frozen` uses only the four layers the pilot measured.)

So the deficit **grows with E**: the sparser the ratio, the more training hurts.
Training is not creating extractable sparsity, it is *adapting the function* — the
residual against the original dense FFN actually **rises** under training
(0.9324 → 0.9480 at E=8; 0.9919 → 0.9974 at E=64) while perplexity falls three
orders of magnitude. Re-purposing experts away from the split they were
initialised to be is exactly what costs the model its oracle ceiling.
E15 ran all 24 layers (not 4), on free Kaggle T4s, and a second seed reproduces
the E=64 inversion to three decimals (0.9551 / 0.9802).

**So the retrofit did not beat its frozen ceiling.** `docs/upcycling_design.md` §9
said to abandon the route if trained `oracle_topk` does not fall below the frozen
oracle at the same (E, k). At E=32 and E=64 it does not — it rises.
→ `results/kaggle_upcycle_poc.json`, `results/kaggle_upcycle_poc_seed1.json`,
`kaggle/kaggle_run.log`, `docs/experiment_log.md` E14 and E15.

**Read that as a result about an *initialisation*, not a verdict on upcycling —
which demonstrably works, and which E15 did not fairly test.** LLaMA-MoE partitions
the FFN exactly as E15 does and needed **200B tokens on 112 A100s**, stating that
15-20B are required just to *compare* partition methods; E15 trained **614k**. E15
also used the gating and the partition axis the literature identifies as wrong, and
omitted the output-rescaling fix both LLaMA-MoE and NVIDIA call essential. The
9.78B-backbone arithmetic below is untouched by any of this. Full review with
sources: `docs/prior_work.md`.

The experiment behind those numbers:

`pilot_sparsity.py` measured the ceiling for a **frozen** partition (it updates no
weights). `experiments/upcycle_poc.py` implements the other half: it upcycles the
FFN into a real top-k MoE (`src/moeme/upcycle.py`) and trains the experts and the
router, then compares the top-k-vs-all-experts residual on the **trained** weights
against the pilot's frozen number. If trained residual < frozen oracle, training
created structure a frozen partition never had.

### Was the partition the problem? — yes. Complete modules are lossless (E16)

**Measured: the objection that ended E15 was correct, and it is now answered.**
Real MoE experts are *complete* feed-forward networks, not disjoint slices. Built
that way on the same model, the same activations and the same metric
(`experiments/branch_poc.py`, `src/moeme/branch.py`):

| active width | stored | best-1 tile | best-1 replica | ppl tile | ppl replica |
|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| 12.5% | 1.00x / 0.50x | 0.7104 | 0.7307 | 54.00 | 33.88 |
| 25% | 1.00x | 0.6339 | 0.6530 | 45.63 | 24.93 |
| 50% | 1.00x / 2.00x | 0.4975 | 0.5134 | 26.02 | 20.11 |
| **100%** | **1.00x / 4.00x** | 0.0000 | **0.0000** | 15.48 | **15.49** |

(dense val ppl **15.4803**; each cell is the best single expert's residual
against the *original dense* layer, which is the only reference that means the
same thing for both constructions.)

1. **Completeness is what buys losslessness.** A full-width replica at k=1
   reproduces dense to within the bf16 floor — ppl 15.49 against 15.48. Slicing
   cannot, at any E unless k=E. E15's degradation was structural.
2. **Width, not tiling, sets the deficit.** Best-tile and best-replica agree to
   ~0.02 at every width, so "slicing hurts" is really "reading 25% hurts".
3. **The big ppl gap at equal width is the router, not the architecture.** An
   untrained tile router picks a random block (0.8878 against a best of 0.6339);
   a replica has one block and nothing to choose. Training the tile router
   recovers most of it (45.63 → 23.19).
4. **Dense residual cannot arbitrate a trained model.** In every trained row the
   residual *rises* while ppl *improves*.
5. **Giving each module its own domain helps where a division of labour exists.**
   Experts pre-trained apart on code/math/chat/wiki (`--btx`) reach ppl **21.64**
   at 1.00x storage and 25% active width, against **23.19** for the same
   architecture trained on mixed data for the same steps — 1.55 ppl better. At
   full width the effect vanishes, because an exact copy has nothing to specialise
   toward.

**And the wall is unchanged: the lossless case costs 4.00x the dense FFN.** This
does not make a model smaller than dense; it makes the per-token read smaller. On
8 GB the binding constraint is storage, so quantisation remains the route to a
27B here.
→ `docs/branching_vs_slicing.md`, `results/branch_poc_08b.json`,
`docs/experiment_log.md` E16.

### Can we offload the cold experts to RAM and keep the hot ones in VRAM? — yes, but not for this model

**Measured (E17), same machine and runtime, on the 14.3 GB of usable fast memory:**

| model | arch | total / active | quant | file | resident? | decode |
|---|---|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| `qwen3.8:latest` | **dense** | 27.8B / 27.8B | Q4_K_M | 17.0 GB | no | 0.561 tok/s |
| `ornith-1.5-35b-a3b:q2_k` | MoE 256×8 | 35.5B / ~3B | Q2_K | 13.0 GB | **yes** | **19.356 tok/s** |
| `ornith-1.5:35b` | MoE 256×8 | 35.5B / ~3B | Q4_K_M | 22.0 GB | no | 6.450 tok/s |

The two `qwen35moe` rows are one architecture at two quantisations, which
separates the two effects: **sparsity is worth 11.5×** (a 35.5B MoE at Q4_K_M
beats a 27.8B *dense* at the same quant) and **residency is worth 3.0× on its own**
(the same MoE at 13.0 GB vs 22.0 GB). Every row fits one equation — decode time =
bytes read per token / the bandwidth of the tiers holding them: 17.0 GB / 1783 ms,
~1.83 GB / 155 ms, ~1.05 GB / 52 ms.

**So why not for the 27B?** Offloading chooses *where each byte comes from*, never
*which bytes are needed* — and in a dense model that set is the entire model.
There is no cold subset to push out; moving layers to RAM re-labels 17.0 GB per
token instead of reducing it, and 17.0 GB does not fit in 14.3 GB at any
placement. Worth noting the idea is not missing from the runtime: these runs used
ollama's default `num_gpu = -1` ("offload what fits") and peaked at 6.7-6.9 GB of
VRAM in every case. **The idea is missing from the model, not the engine.**
The lever that does work remains quantisation — Q3_K_M at 11.8 GB is fully
resident, predicted 4.85 tok/s against the measured 0.561.
→ `docs/expert_offloading.md`, `results/inference_moe*.json`, `docs/experiment_log.md` E17.

### Is the fast MoE as good as the slow dense 27B? — yes, and it is not close on time (E18)

**Measured on this repo's own gate, both models, identical prompts, temperature 0,
fixed seed, identical token cap, all 4 reasoning answers matching exactly:**

| model | reasoning @768 | wall (4 tasks) | 19-task score @768 |
|---|---:|---:|---:|
| `ornith-1.5-35b-a3b:q2_k` (13 GB MoE) | **4/4** | **41 s** | **17/19 = 89.5%** |
| `qwen3.8:latest` (17 GB dense) | **4/4** | 3517 s | not run (0.34 tok/s) |

Both answered `0.05`, `3`, `47`, `42`. **The MoE matches the dense 27B on this
task set while finishing the reasoning work in ~1/86th of the wall time** — and
the two failures it does have (`5 words` asked, 6 given; a forbidden letter) are
the same two the dense model fails at every cap.

**Read the small-cap numbers with care, because the first version of them was
wrong.** At `num_predict=192` the dense model scored 0/4 on reasoning against the
MoE's 2/4. All four of its failures were **truncations** — it was reasoning
correctly and got cut off — and the harness recorded the last number it saw as
the answer. Two grader defects compounded it: `r_bat_ball` expected `0.10` for a
ball that costs `0.05` (failing a correct answer) and `r_modular` expected `1`
where `7**20 % 13 = 3` (passing a wrong one). All three are fixed and pinned by
tests that re-derive the expectations and run a reference solution against every
coding case; `--rescore` re-scored the completed runs instead of re-running them.

**Is the architecture itself the compromise? No — measured at equal quantisation.**
`ornith-1.5:35b` is the *same* `qwen35moe` architecture at Q4_K_M (22 GB), so
against the dense 27B at Q4_K_M (17 GB) it differs in architecture only. Same
runtime, same engine, same 19 tasks: reasoning **ties at 4/4**, the non-reasoning
half differs by **one** brittle task (12/15 vs 13/15), and the sparse model
decodes **6.14 tok/s against 0.32 — ~19×**. Sparsity costs nothing measurable here.
What it does not do is make a model *fit*: at equal bits the 22 GB MoE is 7× slower
than the 13 GB Q2_K one because it misses the fast tiers too — it just reads 8 of
256 experts per token instead of the whole file. On an 8 GB card the 13 GB MoE
stays the one to run, and the reason is memory, not quality.

**And the engine question has a measured answer.** The same 19 tasks through
llama.cpp at surplus's planner placement (`-ngl 42 -ncmoe 24`) scored **17/19 —
identical to the ollama tag, same two failures** — at ×1.12 decode, ×1.21 prompt
processing and ×1.75 wall time. A placement changes where bytes come from, never
which weights the model needs, and that is now measured rather than assumed. Two
findings from getting there: a raw `/completion` request applies no chat template,
so the model's `<think>` block landed in the answer and the *same* run scored
13/19 (a 21-point difference from a request setting), and the earlier "×2.59 / ×10"
placements gains compared against ollama's **cold 32-token** baseline — warm, on
the same tasks, ollama runs 43.1 tok/s and the real gain is ~10-20%.
surplus's own `bench` segfaults on the engine it pins, its `shim` returns HTTP 200
with an empty `response` (so it cannot be scored at all), its auto-plan in the
shim chose `-ncmoe 36` for 3.41 tok/s against 48-50 for its own best plan, and its
default 4 GiB RAM cage is smaller than this MoE needs. Its **planner** is the good
part: adopt the placement, verify it with `llama-bench` upstream.
→ `docs/quality_vs_speed.md`, `results/eval_*_reason768.json`,
`results/eval_llama_ngl42_ncmoe24.json`, `docs/experiment_log.md` E18.

Memory safety was sampled, not assumed: over 59 minutes of dense generation RAM
never fell below 5.79 GiB available and peak VRAM was 6811 of 8188 MiB.

`make upcycle-selftest` runs the whole pipeline on a tiny random model with no
download -- do that first, because it is what caught a device bug that would have
failed every CUDA run. The real run is set up for **free Kaggle GPUs** in
`kaggle/upcycle_poc.ipynb` and was executed there: 24 layers at E=8/32/64, plus a
seed-1 replicate, in one 3.25 h session. `kaggle/README.md` records the measured
step cost and the environment gotchas; `docs/upcycling_design.md` is the plan and
the arithmetic for doing it at 27B scale.

`make eval` runs the gate that `docs/decision_memo.md` Path A depends on:
coding tasks are **executed** against fixed cases, not string-matched. Thinking
models need a large `NUM_PREDICT`; rows the cap cut off are labelled `TRUNCATED`,
counted separately, and reported alongside a score that excludes them, rather than
being scored as capability failures. `make eval-llama` runs the same tasks through
llama.cpp at an explicit expert placement (`LLAMA_PLACE`, default surplus's
`-ngl 42 -ncmoe 24`), refusing to start if free VRAM/RAM are below threshold;
`make eval-surplus` does the same through surplus's Ollama-compatible shim.

`make help` lists everything. `make clean-results` removes regenerable outputs.

### One-command diagnostics

If you only run one thing, run this — it is non-destructive, needs no downloads,
and self-deletes its temp files:

```bash
python3 tools/bench_hardware.py --out results/hardware.json
```

It reports usable VRAM/RAM, CPU and GPU bandwidth, and NVMe sequential plus
random throughput at 4K/64K/256K/1M. **It deliberately writes a temp file larger
than RAM**, because otherwise the page cache serves the reads and the numbers
describe DRAM rather than the SSD. It also drains writeback and drops clean
pages with `posix_fadvise` before timing — without that, sequential read is
understated badly. (During development this distinction mattered: a first
attempt reported the 1 MiB cold mmap read at 15.9 GB/s, which was pure cache.
The corrected figure, 0.76 GB/s, agrees with the independent random-1M
measurement.)

## Measurement summary (this machine)

| Quantity | Measured |
|---|---:|
| Usable VRAM (desktop holds ~2.9 GiB) | ~3.8 GiB |
| Usable RAM for weights | ~10.2 GB (9.5 GiB) |
| GPU memory bandwidth | 246.8 GB/s |
| CPU RAM bandwidth (memcpy) | 40.7 GB/s |
| NVMe cold read | 0.82–1.9 GB/s |
| `qwen3:4b` decode | 60.7 tok/s |
| `ornith-1.5:9b` decode | 24.6 tok/s |
| **`qwen3.8:latest` (27B, Q4_K_M) decode** | **0.561 tok/s** |
| **same, prefill** | **1.6 tok/s** |

## Reproducing each claim

| Claim | Command |
|---|---|
| Exact parameter split of Qwen3.8-27B | `make verify-sources` |
| 9.78B irreducible backbone | `make test` (`test_no_ffn_routing_can_reach_9b_active`) |
| Partitioning is exactly dense-equivalent | `make verify-real-ffn` |
| Partitioning is not free | `make verify-real-ffn` (throughput section) |
| No latent expert structure | `make pilot` + `make report` |
| Training does *not* beat the frozen ceiling at the target ratio | `make report` (`results/kaggle_upcycle_poc.json`, E15) |
| Training beats it only at the mildest ratio, by 0.006 | `results/upcycle_poc.json` (E14) |
| The E=64 inversion is not seed noise | `results/kaggle_upcycle_poc_seed1.json` |
| The upcycling pipeline is sound before spending a GPU | `make upcycle-selftest` |
| 0.561 tok/s and the tier model | `make bench-inference`, `python3 tools/residency_table.py` |
| Evaluation scorer is correct | `make test` (`tests/test_eval_gate.py`, 37 tests) |

## Scope and limitations

Stated up front, because overclaiming here would be worse than the negative
results: both MoE experiments run at 0.8B scale (that is what fits the free
VRAM, and the qwen3_5 Gated DeltaNet path cannot run on CPU here because the
installed `causal_conv1d` is CUDA-only); the real-weight experiment uses synthetic
inputs, so it probes weight structure rather than end-to-end quality; no benchmark
suite or contamination analysis was run; and the memory-tier model is an
additive ceiling estimator validated within ~2x, not a forecast. Workstream B's
runtime exploration (llama.cpp/GGUF, `io_uring`, prefetch policy, native-MTP
speculative decoding) is **designed but not executed**. Full list in
`docs/feasibility_report.md` §9.

Web search was unavailable throughout, so **all** source verification used
primary artefacts (Hugging Face API, `config.json`, model cards, safetensors
headers). Free Kaggle GPU compute *was* used for E15 — one 3.25 h session on 2x
Tesla T4, inside the weekly allowance as observed — and the published quota
numbers themselves were never confirmed from a primary source, so nothing here
is sized against them. Lightning AI was not used.

## Licence and provenance

Study code: do what you like with it. Model artefacts remain under their own
licences (Qwen3.8-27B is Apache-2.0, verified from the model card). No model
weights are redistributed here; `tools/hf_tensor.py` fetches them from the
original repositories on demand.


===== docs/branching_vs_slicing.md =====

# Branching vs slicing: are MoE experts *complete modules* or *slices*?

E14/E15 upcycled a dense FFN by **slicing** its intermediate axis into E disjoint
experts and keeping k. The standing objection to that whole line of work is:

> Real MoE models do not slice anything. Mixtral, Switch and BTX build their
> experts from **complete** feed-forward networks. Activating one performs a
> whole layer's maths, so there is no partial-sum deficit to begin with. The
> negative result may be an artefact of the construction, not a fact about MoE.

That objection is correct, and this document reports the experiment that tests
it. Two constructions are built from the *same* dense layer, evaluated on the
*same* activations with the *same* metric, and compared at *equal active width*
— which is what separates two claims that are easy to conflate:

| claim | what it is about |
|---|---|
| "slicing hurts" | tiles vs replicas |
| "reading less hurts" | width |

## What was built

`src/moeme/branch.py` — `BranchMoEFFN`, a top-k FFN whose experts can be
initialised two ways, from the same dense weights, with the same router, losses
and diagnostics as `moeme.upcycle.MoEFFN`:

* `init="tile"` — expert *j* is the *j*-th disjoint block of intermediate units.
  At `expert_units = I/E` this **is** the E14/E15 construction, and summing all
  experts reproduces the dense layer. Storage stays at 1x the dense FFN.
* `init="replica"` — every expert is initialised from the *same* block. At
  `expert_units = I` every expert is a complete copy, so `top_k = 1` reproduces
  the dense layer **exactly** — at E x the storage.

Three mechanisms were added to make the "cut it into sub-models and give each one
its own data" idea testable rather than merely arguable:

1. **`n_shared` / `shared_units`** — always-on experts added to every token, so
   the units that are active for most inputs can be moved out of the router's
   remit (the DeepSeek-MoE / CMoE fix for router collapse).
2. **`set_force_expert(e)`** — bypasses the router and sends every token to one
   expert at weight 1.0, with no balancing loss. This is what lets an expert be
   trained **alone, on its own data, before any router exists**.
3. **`combine`** — `sum` (E14/E15's default), `mean`, or `softmax`.

`experiments/branch_poc.py` — the driver. `--btx` adds the branch-train-miX
configurations: stage 2 trains each expert independently on a *different domain*
(code, math, chat, wiki), stage 3 trains the router and experts jointly on the
mixed stream. `--self-test` checks the whole pipeline offline in ~2 s.

## Method

Qwen3.5-0.8B, `bf16`, layers `[0, 3, 8, 23]` of 24 replaced, 3M characters of
streamed `fineweb-edu` (661,966 tokens; 4 held-out val blocks), seq 512, effective
batch 2048, 80 cooperation steps, 40 steps per expert in stage 2, lr 1e-4,
`aux_loss_coef` 1e-2. TF32 is **disabled**, so an identity that should be exact
reads as exact and a small difference is architectural rather than arithmetic.
BF16 still leaves a ~4e-3 relative floor on residuals; the exactness identities
are verified to 1e-5 in fp32 by `--self-test` and by `tests/test_branch.py`.

All residuals are against the **original dense** layer, which is the only
reference that means the same thing for a tile and a replica. (`oracle_topk` in
`moeme.ffn` compares against the sum of all experts — the dense layer for a tile,
E x the dense layer for a replica — so it is not usable here.)

## Results

dense val ppl **15.4803**. `residual_*` are mean relative L2 errors over the four
upcycled layers; `ppl` is the whole model on the held-out blocks.

| config | active width | stored | best-1 | oracle top-k | router | all-sum | ppl |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| `tile_E8_k1_12pct` | 0.125 | 1.00x | 0.7104 | 0.7104 | 0.9231 | 0.005 | 54.00 |
| `tile_E4_k1_25pct` | 0.250 | 1.00x | 0.6339 | 0.6339 | 0.8878 | 0.005 | 45.63 |
| `tile_E2_k1_50pct` | 0.500 | 1.00x | 0.4975 | 0.4975 | 0.6656 | 0.004 | 26.02 |
| `tile_E1_k1_100pct` | 1.000 | 1.00x | 0.0000 | 0.0000 | 0.0000 | 0.000 | 15.48 |
| `tile_E4_k2_50pct` | 0.500 | 1.00x | 0.6339 | 0.4820 | 0.7392 | 0.005 | 34.34 |
| `tile_E4_k4_100pct` | 1.000 | 1.00x | 0.6339 | 0.0051 | 0.0058 | 0.005 | 15.47 |
| `replica_E4_k1_12pct` | 0.125 | 0.50x | 0.7307 | 0.7307 | 0.7307 | 1.831 | 33.88 |
| `replica_E4_k1_25pct` | 0.250 | 1.00x | 0.6530 | 0.6530 | 0.6530 | 2.117 | 24.93 |
| `replica_E4_k1_50pct` | 0.500 | 2.00x | 0.5134 | 0.5134 | 0.5134 | 2.493 | 20.11 |
| **`replica_E4_k1_full`** | **1.000** | **4.00x** | **0.0000** | **0.0000** | **0.0038** | 3.000 | **15.49** |
| `trained_tile_E4_k1_25pct` | 0.250 | 1.00x | 0.6339 | 0.6339 | 0.8852→0.9303 | 0.005 | 23.19 |
| `trained_replica_E4_k1_full` | 1.000 | 4.00x | 0.0000 | 0.0000 | 0.0044→0.5491 | 3.000 | 19.11 |
| `trained_replica_E4_k1_full_shared` | 1.000 | 4.00x | 0.0000 | 0.0000 | 0.0035→0.5142 | 3.000 | 22.78 |
| `btx_replica_k1_full` | 1.000 | 4.00x | 0.0000 | 0.0000 | 0.0044→0.6296 | 3.000 | 19.14 |
| `btx_replica_k1_half` | 0.500 | 2.00x | 0.5134 | 0.5134 | 0.5134→0.7336 | 2.493 | 19.80 |
| `btx_tile_k1_25pct` | 0.250 | 1.00x | 0.6339 | 0.6339 | 0.8477→0.9237 | 0.005 | **21.64** |

## Findings

**1. The objection is right: complete modules are lossless, slices are not.**
`replica_E4_k1_full` reproduces the dense model at k=1 — ppl 15.49 against the
dense 15.48, residual 0.0038 (the bf16 floor; exactly 0 at full width with one
expert, and 1e-6 in fp32). No slicing configuration can do this unless k = E
(`tile_E4_k4_100pct`, which is just the dense layer with extra bookkeeping). So
"experts must be complete modules" is confirmed, and the reason partition-based
upcycling degrades is structural, not a training failure.

**2. Width, not tiling, is what the deficit tracks.** At equal active width the
best-single-expert residual of a tile and a replica agree to ~0.02:

| active width | best tile | best replica |
|---|---:|---:|
| 12.5% | 0.7104 | 0.7307 |
| 25% | 0.6339 | 0.6530 |
| 50% | 0.4975 | 0.5134 |

Reading 25% of a layer costs you ~0.64 of that layer's output whether the 25% is
contiguous (a tile) or repeated (a replica). The oracle comparison at 50%
(`tile_E4_k2_50pct` 0.4820 vs `replica_E4_k1_50pct` 0.5134) even favours tiling,
which is expected: a tile can choose the *best* of E blocks, a replica has only
one block and the router has nothing to choose — visible in the table as
`best1 == oracle == router` for every replica row.

**3. The huge ppl gap between tiles and replicas at equal width is the router,
not the architecture.** At 25% active: `tile_E4_k1_25pct` ppl 45.63 versus
`replica_E4_k1_25pct` 24.93, with nearly identical single-expert residuals
(0.6339 vs 0.6530). The difference is that an *untrained* tile router picks a
random block (`router` 0.8878 against `best1` 0.6339), while a replica's "choice"
is vacuous. Training the tile router recovers most of it (45.63 → 23.19). Any
comparison of these two constructions that reports only end-to-end ppl is
measuring router initialisation.

**4. Residual against the dense function is the wrong objective once anything is
trained.** In every trained row the router's residual *rises* while ppl
*improves*: `trained_tile_E4_k1_25pct` 0.8852 → 0.9303 with ppl 45.63 →
23.19; `trained_replica_E4_k1_full` 0.0044 → 0.5491 with ppl 15.49 → 19.11. A MoE that is being
finetuned on a corpus is no longer trying to reproduce the dense function, and
drift away from it is what training *does*. This is the metric E14/E15 used as
the decisive gate, and it cannot arbitrate a trained model.

**5. Training a *lossless* configuration can only make it worse, and 80 steps
shows exactly that.** `replica_E4_k1_full` starts at dense quality by
construction. After 80 steps: 19.11 (joint) / 19.14 (domain-specialised) /
22.78 (with a shared expert). Specialisation did not recover the loss — it
matched joint training to two decimal places. At this budget the honest statement
is: *the untrained exact replica is the best sparse configuration measured*, and
everything that trains moves away from it.

**6. Giving each module its own domain does help — exactly where a division of
labour exists.** `btx_tile_k1_25pct` (stage 2 on four domains, then stage 3)
reaches ppl **21.64** at 1.00x storage and 25% active width, against **23.19**
for the identical architecture trained on mixed data for the same 80 stage-3
steps — 1.55 ppl better. At full width the effect vanishes (19.14 vs 19.11),
which is coherent: a full-width replica starts as an exact copy of the dense
function, so there is nothing for specialisation to add, while a 25%-wide tile
has to *earn* the missing 75% and pre-specialised experts give the router
something to route between.

**7. The routed experts do not collapse.** Post-training usage on the validation
batch is spread — layer 0 `[0.26, 0.25, 0.22, 0.27]`, layer 3
`[0.20, 0.18, 0.39, 0.23]`, layer 23 `[0.28, 0.17, 0.07, 0.48]` — so the
`eff_experts = 33.2/64` failure of E15 does not reproduce here. That is a
consequence of construction, not of tuning: E15's collapse came from
always-active neurons scattered across disjoint slices, which is precisely what
the tile-with-shared-expert and replica constructions avoid.

**8. The wall is unchanged, and it is made of storage.** The lossless case costs
**4.00x** the dense FFN. Nothing here makes a sparse model smaller than the dense
model it came from; it makes the *per-token read* smaller. On an 8 GB machine
that is the constraint that binds, which is why the residency table and the
quantisation ladder remain the route to a 27B on this laptop.

## What would make it better (next experiments, in order)

1. **A shared expert holding the *dense* FFN, with routed specialists added on
   top, and the router output zero-initialised.** This is the one configuration
   that is *guaranteed* to start at dense quality and can only improve: at
   initialisation the routed contribution is zero, so the layer is exactly dense,
   and specialists add capacity without ever being able to subtract it. The
   `_shared` run here did not test this (it gave the shared expert a *full* extra
   FFN, 2x width, and took the result to 22.78) — the correct version is one
   dense-width shared expert plus narrow routed experts with a zeroed router.
   This is the concrete way to "keep the 9B active slice at full quality".
2. **Train long enough to beat dense, or do not train.** Every trained
   configuration here is worse than the untrained exact replica. 80 steps on
   662k tokens is 3-4 orders of magnitude below what BTX used. Either scale the
   corpus by ~1000x, or restrict training to the router.
3. **Judge trained MoE by task metrics, not by dense residual.** Finding 4 is the
   methodological lesson; the gate set in `experiments/eval_gate.py` exists and
   should be the arbiter.
4. **Re-test `combine="softmax"` against `sum` with the router actually in the
   output path.** `sum` gives the router ~5 orders of magnitude less gradient
   from the language-model objective than `softmax` (measured in
   `src/moeme/branch.py`); the tile-router recovery in finding 3 was obtained
   with `softmax` and would not have been possible with `sum`.
5. **Only then re-run at 27B.** Nothing in this experiment changes the arithmetic
   in `docs/upcycling_design.md`: at 9B active of 27B total the selectable part
   is 2.9% of the FFN, so the wall is reached before the architecture is.

## Limitations

* One model (Qwen3.5-0.8B), 4 of 24 layers upcycled, one seed. Whole-model ppl
  moves only through those four layers, so ppl differences are *understated*
  relative to a full conversion.
* 80 cooperation steps and 40 expert steps is a pipeline check, not a training
  budget; findings 5 and 6 are statements about *this* budget.
* The stage-2 domains are 800k characters each with 2-block held-out sets, so
  `domain_ppl` is noisy and is reported as directional only.
* bf16 leaves a ~4e-3 residual floor. Identities that should be exact are
  verified in fp32 by `experiments/branch_poc.py --self-test` (18 checks) and
  `tests/test_branch.py` (38 tests).
* `stored_width_fraction` counts FFN width only. It excludes the router and does
  not include the embedding/attention backbone, which every token reads
  regardless of routing — see `docs/feasibility_report.md` §3 for why that
  backbone is what makes "9B active of 27B" unreachable by routing alone.

Raw evidence: `results/branch_poc_08b.json`, `results/branch_poc_08b_table.txt`.


===== docs/decision_memo.md =====

# Decision Memo

**Date:** 2026-10-10 · **Hardware:** RTX 4060 Laptop 8 GB / i7-13620H / 15.4 GiB /
Micron 3400 1 TB NVMe, Ubuntu 24.04.4
**Spend to date:** £0.00 · **Compute used:** the target laptop, plus one 3.25 h
session of free Kaggle T4 GPU for the trained-MoE experiment (E15).

---

## 1. Recommendation in one paragraph

**Do not convert Qwen3.8-27B into a sparse MoE.** It is proven impossible to hit
9B active that way (the untouched backbone is already 9.78B params), and it is
separately proven that post-hoc FFN sparsification of this dense model has no
usable operating point (a *perfect oracle* router loses 42–81% of the layer
output at 12.5% active). **Instead, run the existing dense model at aggressive
quantisation so it fits fully resident**, which requires no training, no
architecture change and no money, and should move decode from the measured
0.561 tok/s to roughly 4–6 tok/s. Then measure whether the quality holds up.
That is one download and one afternoon.

## 2. The evidence behind that

| Finding | Number | Where |
|---|---|---|
| 27B backbone (non-FFN) is already larger than the 9B-active target | 9,783,238,144 params | `docs/source_validation.md`, executable test |
| FFN share of the model | 61.6% (17,112,760,320) | safetensors headers |
| Post-hoc sparsification operating point | oracle residual 0.42–0.81 at 12.5% active | `results/pilot_curve.txt` |
| Is the router the bottleneck? No | oracle vs learned gap <= 0.033 | same |
| Does **training** the MoE reach the ceiling? No | at E=64/k=1 the residual *rises*: 0.9277 frozen → 0.9549 trained | `results/kaggle_upcycle_poc.json` (E15) |
| ...and it is not seed noise | seed 1 gives 0.9551 / router 0.9802 | `results/kaggle_upcycle_poc_seed1.json` |
| Trained MoE at the target ratio is 10.4x dense ppl | 202.5 vs 19.6 | same |
| Does latent expert structure exist? No | participation ratio ≈ E (127 of 128) | `results/real_ffn.json` |
| Partitioning is exact on real weights | worst rel. L2 error 5.1e-07 | `results/real_ffn.json` |
| Partitioning is *free* however? No | 2.1-2.4x (E=32) to 2.9-3.9x (E=128) slower at batch 1 across two runs; top-2 of 32 costs the same as all-32 | `results/real_ffn_rerun.json` |
| Target model today | **0.561 tok/s**, prefill 1.6 tok/s | `results/inference_27b.json` |
| Usable fast memory | 3.8 GiB VRAM + 10.2 GB RAM = 14.3 GB | `configs/tiers.json` |
| ...predicted independently from hardware constants | 0.48 tok/s | `tools/residency_table.py` |
| Residency makes it viable | Q3_K_M 11.8 GB resident -> ~4.9 tok/s predicted | `results/residency_table.txt` |

## 3. Three paths, ranked

### Path A — aggressive quantisation to full residency (**recommended**)

*What:* download Qwen3.8-27B at Q3_K_M / IQ3 / Q2_K; measure decode rate, peak
VRAM/RAM, and held-out task scores against the existing Q4_K_M baseline.

*Why it is the right first move:* the measured bottleneck is **storage
streaming, not compute**. At Q4_K_M the model is 17.0 GB against ~14.3 GB of
usable fast memory (3.8 GiB VRAM + 10.2 GB RAM), so 2.74 GB/token comes off an
SSD that delivers 1.5 GB/s. Below ~3.4 bpw the model fits entirely in VRAM+RAM
and that cost disappears.

*Cost:* £0, ~10–15 GB download, a few hours of work. *Expected outcome:*
~4–6 tok/s if the tier model is right, at some quality cost that must be
measured rather than assumed.

*Gate (decide with this, don't hope):* >= 4 tok/s **and** >= 95% of the Q4_K_M
baseline on coding and reasoning held-out tasks. Fail either and go to Path C.

*Risk:* Q3/Q2 can hurt coding and logic disproportionately. This is the real
risk of the whole project and the reason step 3 below (build the eval set) is a
prerequisite, not a nice-to-have.

### Path B — post-hoc MoE conversion of Qwen3.8-27B (**declined, on evidence**)

*Why declined:* two independent blockers, both measured.
1. **Arithmetic:** 9B active is unreachable because the non-FFN backbone is
   9.78B active params. Reaching it means retraining attention, Gated DeltaNet
   and the embedding/head — i.e. a new model, not a conversion.
2. **Quality:** the oracle ceiling for post-hoc FFN top-k is bad, and the
   router is *not* the limiting factor (gap <= 0.033), so training a better
   router cannot rescue it.
   Additionally, the one plan that looked promising (k-means weight clustering)
   is degenerate: for E=128 one cluster absorbs 52% of the units.
3. **And training does not create the missing structure** — this was the open
   objection to blocker 2, and it is now measured rather than assumed. On real
   Qwen3.5-0.8B over all 24 layers (E15, free Kaggle T4s), training the experts
   and router beat the frozen partition only at the mildest ratio tested (E=8,
   12.5% active, by 0.006) and **made the reconstruction worse** at the ratios a
   conversion needs: E=32 (3.1% active) 0.9188 → 0.9355, E=64 (1.6%) 0.9277 →
   0.9549. The deficit grows with E, and a second seed reproduces it. Training
   re-purposes experts away from the split they were partitioned from — the
   residual against the original dense FFN actually rises while perplexity falls
   three orders of magnitude. The learned router loses to the oracle at every
   ratio, so better routing is not the fix either.

   *Scope, after a literature review (`docs/prior_work.md`):* E15 trained **614k
tokens**; LLaMA-MoE, partitioning the FFN the same way, used **200B on 112 A100s**
   and states 15-20B are needed just to compare partition methods. E15 also omitted
   the output rescaling both LLaMA-MoE (N/k) and NVIDIA (∛(E·G²/T)) call essential,
   and partitioned by weights where CMoE shows the axis should be the activation
   pattern with a shared expert. So the blocker is **budget, not feasibility**:
   upcycling demonstrably works at ~50% of original pretraining compute (Sparse
   Upcycling, +10-60%), which is 10⁴-10⁵ GPU-hours — four orders of magnitude beyond
   a free tier. The arithmetic in blocker 1 is unaffected by any of this.

*This is exactly the checkpoint the handoff asked for:* "Stop or adjust
architecture if aggressive sparsity fails oracle recovery." It failed — in both
forms. The frozen partition has no operating point (§4 of the report), and the
trained partition is worse than the frozen one where it matters (E14/E15).
Stopping.

### Path C — train a small MoE from scratch (**blocked on compute; not now**)

*What the goal actually requires:* a model with ~9B active *and* near-27B
quality. Section 4 of the feasibility report shows the active-parameter side of
that is speed-feasible — a fully resident 9B-active model is predicted at
~19 tok/s here. The blocker is that it needs far more than 9B *total*, and the
total must fit in ~14.3 GB, which forces exceptionally aggressive quantisation
of a model whose experts were never trained to tolerate it.

*Why it is blocked:* training a ~20B-A9B MoE is a pretraining-scale project
(10^21–10^22 FLOPs order). Free tiers are hours of a single mid-range GPU. That
is several orders of magnitude short, and no scheduling of free tiers closes
that gap. **The handoff's own framing is right: this is a major retraining
problem, not an FFN routing tweak.** With a £20 ceiling and no ongoing budget,
it is not startable.

## 4. Verdict on "9–12B active with near-27B quality"

- **Reachable by converting Qwen3.8-27B? No.** 9B active is below the 9.78B
  irreducible backbone; 12B active is arithmetically possible (2.2B active FFN)
  but only with *trained* experts, and the pilot shows post-hoc splits have no
  operating point.
- **Reachable as a design point on this hardware? Yes, in principle.** A
  fully resident ~12B-active model is predicted at ~10 tok/s here
  (bandwidth-bound on RAM), and a 9B-active one at ~19 tok/s. The hardware is
  not the obstacle.
- **Reachable within this budget? No.** It requires pretraining compute that
  free tiers cannot supply by orders of magnitude.

So: the hardware can host such a model; the budget cannot produce one. Path A is
how you get the best available assistant on this machine in the meantime.

## 5. Status of each deliverable

| # | Deliverable | Status |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | Feasibility report | **Done** — `docs/feasibility_report.md` |
| 2 | Project repository | **Done** — this repo, README, tests, config, results; git-committed locally. Not pushed to GitHub (no authorisation requested) |
| 3 | Dense-equivalent FFN partitioning + verification | **Done and verified** — real 27B weights, 5.1e-07 worst error, 243 tests pass |
| 4 | Pilot sparsity study | **Done** — `results/pilot_sparsity.json`; quality-vs-active-compute curve with oracle and learned routers |
| 4b | Trained-MoE PoC (the "does training change the answer?" gate) | **Done** — `results/upcycle_poc.json` (E14, local), `results/kaggle_upcycle_poc.json` + `_seed1.json` (E15, 24 layers on free Kaggle T4s). Answer: no; worse than frozen at E=32/64 |
| 5 | Edge runtime experiment | **Partly done** — hardware characterised and three quantised baselines measured end-to-end on the target machine; residency table computed. **Not done:** llama.cpp/GGUF build, `io_uring` vs mmap comparison, prefetch policy, pinned buffers, speculative decoding / native MTP experiments |
| 6 | Decision memo | **Done** — this document |
| 4c | Branching vs slicing (E16): are experts complete modules or slices? | **Done** — `results/branch_poc_08b.json`. Answer: complete modules *are* lossless (ppl 15.49 vs dense 15.48) but cost 4.00x the FFN; width, not tiling, sets the deficit; domain-specialised experts help only where a division of labour exists |

**Executed:** all hardware measurements; three end-to-end inference baselines;
exact parameter accounting from real weight headers; FFN partitioning verified
against real 27B weights; sparsity pilot on real activations; the trained-MoE
PoC at the target ratio over all 24 layers on free Kaggle GPUs, with a second
seed; a branching-vs-slicing experiment separating "slicing hurts" from "reading
less hurts", with independent per-domain expert training; a validated memory-tier
throughput model; 243 passing unit tests.

**Designed but not executed:** the evaluation harness (30/30/20/20 task sets
with executable checks); the quantisation quality ladder; all of workstream B's
async-I/O and prefetch work; speculative decoding against the native MTP head.

**Blocked:** any pretraining-scale work (no budget); CPU-only runs of the
qwen3_5 Gated DeltaNet path (the installed `causal_conv1d` is CUDA-only, which
is a fixable environment issue and worth fixing before CPU-offload design).

**Not attempted, by choice:** SSH or any access to machines other than the one
this session runs on; no paid resources; no irreversible changes. Free Kaggle GPU
compute *was* used for E15 and is not a paid resource.

## 6. Next three actions

1. **Build the held-out evaluation set first** (coding / reasoning / instruction
   / chat, with executable checks). Path A's gate is meaningless without it, and
   it is the only way to avoid "it feels fine" replacing evidence.
2. **Run the quantisation ladder** (Q3_K_M, IQ3, Q2_K) and apply the gate.
3. **Check the SSD** — `lspci -vv` link width and drive health. A 4x error in the
   storage number is the difference between "streaming is hopeless" and
   "streaming is a real design option", and it is a 10-minute check.


===== docs/experiment_log.md =====

# Experiment Log

Every experiment actually executed during this study, in order, with the command
used and the raw outcome. Numbers here are the source for the reports; if a
figure is quoted in `docs/`, it should trace to a line in this file.

**Environment:** `cleanerbox-Stealth-15`, Ubuntu 24.04.4, i7-13620H (10c/16t),
RTX 4060 Laptop 8188 MiB, 15.4 GiB RAM, Micron 3400 1 TB NVMe.
**Session date:** 2026-10-10. **Spend:** £0.00. **Cloud compute used:** none.

---

## E1 — Source verification (model geometry)

**Command:** `python3 tools/verify_model_geometry.py Qwen/Qwen3.8-27B`

**Method:** range-read the 1199 safetensors tensor headers (no weights
downloaded, a few hundred KB total).

**Outcome:** total 27,781,427,952 params. FFN 17,112,760,320. Gated DeltaNet
5,562,044,928. Attention 1,677,721,600. Embeddings + LM head 1,271,398,400
each. Vision tower 460,730,096. MTP head 424,699,392. Text tower
26,895,998,464. → `results/qwen3.8-27b-geometry.json`

**Finding:** the model is **dense**; there is no `num_experts` field. The
handoff's inherited "17.1B FFN / 9.8B other" split is confirmed exactly.

## E2 — Source verification (the family's MoE model)

**Command:** `python3 tools/verify_model_geometry.py Qwen/Qwen3.8-Flash-Next`

**Outcome:** 179,999,981,459 params (180.0B) — 121.4B MoE experts + 51.2B n-gram
memory + 2.6B MTP. → `results/qwen3.8-flash-next-geometry.json`

**Finding:** the family's MoE variant is 180B, not 27B. It cannot run on this
hardware in any quantisation.

## E3 — Hardware characterisation

**Command:** `python3 tools/bench_hardware.py --out results/hardware.json --disk-gb 20`

**Outcome:** GPU 246.8 GB/s (read), 219.9 GB/s (copy). RAM 40.7 GB/s copy.
NVMe sequential read 1.72 GB/s (buffered) / 1.47 GB/s (4 MiB `O_DIRECT`) /
0.82 GB/s (1 MiB `O_DIRECT`). Random 4K 11,419 IOPS. mmap cold 1 MiB: 1.38 ms,
0.76 GB/s. → `results/hardware.json`

**Two measurement bugs found and fixed during this experiment** (worth recording,
because both produced plausible-looking wrong numbers):

1. The first mmap cold-read reported **15.9 GB/s** — impossible for a drive that
   reads sequentially at 1.7 GB/s. Cause: `MADV_DONTNEED` on a `MAP_SHARED`
   mapping only drops the process page tables; the next fault re-attaches the
   same cached page. Fix: `posix_fadvise(POSIX_FADV_DONTNEED)` on the file,
   which actually discards clean file pages. Corrected value 0.76 GB/s, which
   then agreed with the independent random-1M measurement (0.82 GB/s).
2. Sequential read was initially understated because it was timed while our own
   20 GB of dirty pages were still flushing. Fix: `fsync`, then a settle delay,
   then measure. Value moved from 1.68 → 1.72–1.93 GB/s.

**Anomaly, unresolved:** the drive is a Micron 3400 1TB (datasheet ~6.6 GB/s
sequential read) but tops out near 1.5 GB/s across three independent methods
(buffered read, `O_DIRECT` `dd`, and a real 8.6 GB model blob). Cause not
established. Candidates recorded in `configs/tiers.json` — PCIe link width /
power state, thermal throttling, or sustained-state fallback. **This is the
single most decision-relevant unresolved number in the study.**

## E4 — End-to-end inference baselines

**Command:** `python3 tools/bench_inference.py --spec <model>:-1:<n>`

| Model | Size | Decode | Prefill | Load | Peak VRAM |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| `qwen3:4b` | 2.5 GB | **60.668 tok/s** | 560.2 tok/s | 6.7 s | 6798 MiB |
| `ornith-1.5:9b` | 6.6 GB | **24.629 tok/s** | 138.9 tok/s | 15.4 s | 6884 MiB |
| `qwen3.8:latest` (27B Q4_K_M) | 17.0 GB | **0.561 tok/s** | **1.609 tok/s** | 46.8 s | 6884 MiB |

→ `results/inference_safe.json`, `results/inference_27b.json`

**Finding:** the 27B is ~44x slower than a 9B here, and prefill of 1.6 tok/s
makes even prompt-filling impractical. Also measured: `MemAvailable` ~10.23 GB
during runs, and ollama obtained ~3906 MiB of model VRAM (6884 − 2978 MiB
desktop baseline) — these two numbers became the tier capacities.

## E5 — Dense-equivalent FFN partitioning (unit level)

**Command:** `.venv/bin/python -m pytest tests -q`

**Outcome:** 87 passed. Covers exactness for 4 plans × 7 expert counts × 2 input
distributions, ragged (non-dividing) splits, plan validity, bf16 exactness,
oracle monotonicity, oracle-vs-random-router ordering, concentration bounds, and
clustering correctness against planted structure.

**Two real library bugs found and fixed by these tests:**
1. `PartitionedFFN.topk_forward` mis-indexed flattened expert outputs,
   returning scalars instead of hidden vectors.
2. `clustered_plan` hardcoded a CPU `torch.Generator` and a CPU `assign` tensor,
   so it raised on CUDA inputs (`Expected a 'cuda' device type for generator`).
   Fixed in the library, and `PartitionedFFN` now moves index tensors to the
   weight device.

## E6 — Partitioning verified on REAL Qwen3.8-27B weights

**Command:** `python3 experiments/verify_real_ffn.py --out results/real_ffn.json`

**Method:** ranged download of layer 1's `gate/up/down` (0.535 GB of the 55.6 GB
checkpoint; all 64 layers share identical FFN geometry).

**Outcome:**
- **Exactness: worst relative L2 error 5.081e-07** across 11 plan/experts combos
  × 2 input distributions — contiguous, interleaved and random at E ∈ {8,32,128},
  clustered at E ∈ {8,32} only (the k-means plan is the expensive one). The
  partitioning is exactly dense-equivalent on the real artefact.
- **Throughput is NOT free:** dense 2.443 ms; E=32 all-active 5.916 ms
  (**2.42x**); E=128 all-active 9.497 ms (**3.89x**). Small-GEMM overhead at
  batch 1.
- **But that ratio is noisy, so quote a range.** A second run on the same cached
  weights (`results/real_ffn_rerun.json`, 2026-10-11) measured dense 2.555 ms,
  E=32 5.431 ms and E=128 7.391 ms -- i.e. **2.13x** and **2.89x**. Single-shot
  batch-1 timings on a daily-driver GPU vary by ~35%; the range is
  **2.1-2.4x (E=32) and 2.9-3.9x (E=128)**. The direction never changes:
  partitioning costs more than dense.
- **And top-k does not buy it back.** E=32 top-2 was 5.931 ms against 5.916 ms
  all-active on the first run, and 5.359 against 5.431 on the second -- the same
  within noise. Keeping 2 of 32 experts saves ~0-1.3%, not the 16x the active
  parameter count implies: dispatch and small-GEMM overhead consume the sparsity
  win. This is the strongest form of the result, and it is what makes MoE a
  large-batch technique rather than a single-stream one.
- **No natural structure:** participation ratio 31.57 of 32 and 126.14–127.82 of
  128. Energy is uniformly spread; the oracle residual only approaches 0 as k → E.
- **Clustering degenerates:** for E=128 one cluster holds 9,014 of 17,408 units
  (52%); top-3 experts hold 91% of energy. The attractive-looking oracle residual
  (0.019) is an artefact of that imbalance, not evidence of sparsity.

## E7 — Pilot sparsity study (real activations)

**Command:** `python3 experiments/pilot_sparsity.py --out results/pilot_sparsity.json`

**Model:** `Qwen/Qwen3.5-0.8B` — same `qwen3_5_text` architecture as the 27B,
same 3:1 DeltaNet:attention pattern, FFN expansion 3.50 vs the 27B's 3.40.
512 tokens of real calibration text; real weights; real forward-pass activations
captured via a pre-hook on each `mlp` module.

**Setup obstacle worth recording:** the installed `causal_conv1d` is CUDA-only
(`Expected x.is_cuda() to be true`), so the qwen3_5 Gated DeltaNet path cannot
run on CPU through `transformers`. The pilot had to run on the GPU, which is why
a 0.8B model was chosen (the 2B sibling's 4.55 GB does not fit the ~3.8 GiB of
obtainable VRAM).

**Outcome — the decisive result.** At 12.5% active compute, best oracle residual
per layer, excluding the degenerate clustered plan:

| Layer | Best oracle residual @ 12.5% active | Learned router |
|---|---:|---:|
| 0 | 0.681 | 0.807 |
| 3 | 0.802 | 0.872 |
| 8 | 0.811 | 0.857 |
| 23 | 0.417 | 0.449 |

Oracle-vs-learned gap: **0.0001–0.033**.
→ `results/pilot_sparsity.json`, `results/pilot_curve.txt`

**Findings:**
1. Even a perfect oracle loses 42–81% of the layer output at 12.5% active.
2. The partition, not the router, sets the ceiling — so training a better router
   cannot fix this. This is the checkpoint the handoff asked for before spending
   training compute.
3. Participation ratio ≈ E throughout: no latent expert structure exists.

## E8 — Memory-tier model + residency table

**Command:** `python3 tools/residency_table.py`

**Outcome (corrected units):** with 3.8 GiB VRAM + 10.2 GB RAM usable and
measured bandwidths only (GPU 247, RAM 40.7, disk 1.5 GB/s):

| Quant | bpw | Size | Streamed/token | Predicted |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| Q2_K | 2.8 | 9.7 GB | 0.00 | 6.44 tok/s |
| Q3_K_M | 3.4 | 11.8 GB | 0.00 | 4.85 tok/s |
| Q4_K_S | 4.4 | 15.3 GB | 1.00 GB | 1.07 tok/s |
| Q4_K_M | 4.9 | 17.0 GB | 2.74 GB | 0.48 tok/s |

**Validation:** predicted 0.48 vs measured **0.561** for Q4_K_M (ratio 0.86);
the other two configurations land at 1.63x and 0.49x. The model brackets reality
within ~2x using only independently measured constants.

**Unit bug found and fixed:** the RAM tier was originally specified as
`10.2 GiB`, but the source measurement is `MemAvailable` in bytes (10.23e9 =
9.5 GiB). Mixing GB and GiB overstated the tier by ~7%. Corrected, with the key
renamed `ram_usable_gb` so the units are unambiguous. The 27B prediction moved
from 0.57 → 0.48 tok/s; all conclusions were unaffected.

**Finding:** below ~3.4 bpw the 27B becomes fully resident, removing all
per-token storage traffic. This is the actionable path.

## E9 — Evaluation scorer unit tests

**Command:** `.venv/bin/python -m pytest tests -q`

**Outcome:** 124 passed — the original 87 plus 37 covering the evaluation
harness's extraction and scoring logic (`tests/test_eval_gate.py`).

**Why this exists:** the harness decides pass/fail, so a buggy scorer would
silently invalidate every quality number. Offline and millisecond-fast, so
`make test` covers it without a model or GPU.

**Three real bugs it caught, all fixed in the library rather than worked around:**
1. **`run_code` produced `IndentationError` on every correct solution.** The
   candidate source was interpolated into an indented template and then
   `textwrap.dedent`-ed, which collapsed the function body to column 0. This had
   already polluted an earlier eval run: coding tasks reported "unexpected
   indent" and it looked like the model's fault. The candidate source is now
   concatenated at column 0.
2. **`forbid_char` reported "contains 'e'" even when it passed** — a static
   detail string rather than an outcome-dependent one.
3. **JSON `true` validated against an `int` field**, because `bool` subclasses
   `int` in Python. `check_json` now rejects bool where a number is required,
   in both flat and nested positions.

## E10 — Evaluation gate run (starter set)

**Command:** `python3 experiments/eval_gate.py --model qwen3:4b --num-predict 1024 --only coding`

**Outcome:** 3/10 coding tasks pass, with **executable** verification — 4/4,
2/2 and 4/4 cases run and pass. The other 7 `TRUNCATED during reasoning (1024
tokens, empty answer)`. → `results/eval_qwen3_4b_coding.json`

A wider subset run (`--only reasoning,instruction,format`, 20 tasks) gave 9/20,
with 9 truncations. Real passes include `r_train` (16:50), `r_lily` (47),
`r_sequence` (42), a 3-action JSON response, an exact single-word answer, and a
yes/no answer. → `results/eval_qwen3_4b_subset.json`

**Interpretation:** `qwen3:4b` is a thinking model that needs well over 1024
tokens of reasoning for most non-trivial prompts. Both runs are therefore
**partly a measurement of the token cap, not of the model**, and the harness says
so explicitly rather than burying it. A real gate run needs `NUM_PREDICT >= 4096`.
The harness mechanics — code execution, JSON parsing, number/time extraction,
instruction constraints — are all confirmed working against a live model.

**Not run:** the full 30/30/20/20 split, any public benchmark, any contamination
check, and any evaluation of the 27B itself (at 0.561 tok/s a 30-task reasoning
run would take many hours).

## E11 — Upcycling module (trainable top-k MoE)

**Command:** `.venv/bin/python -m pytest tests -q`

**Outcome:** 187 passed. `src/moeme/upcycle.py` adds `MoEConfig` and `MoEFFN`: a
dense swiGLU FFN viewed as a partition of experts with a learned router, a
Switch-style auxiliary loss, a router z-loss and routing diagnostics.
`tests/test_upcycle.py` adds 63 tests (124 -> 187), covering:

- **the dense limit** — `top_k = n_experts` with `combine="sum"` reproduces the
dense layer exactly for every plan and expert count, so the dense model is a
strict special case rather than a claim;
- that `combine="softmax"` is deliberately **not** the dense limit (softmax
  weights sum to 1, so a full-k combination is a weighted mean);
- gradients reaching every expert and the router, and a real training step
  reducing a probe loss;
- auxiliary-loss value and routing metrics on balanced and collapsed routers.

**Finding:** partition-based upcycling keeps total parameters *equal* to the
dense FFN (experts are slices, not replicas), which is what a "27B total / 9B
active" target requires and makes the dense-limit property exact.

## E12 — Upcycling PoC pipeline check (offline self-test)

**Command:** `python3 experiments/upcycle_poc.py --self-test`

**Outcome:** the full pipeline (upcycle -> untrained eval -> train -> residual ->
routing stats) runs end to end on a tiny random model with no download.

| quantity | value |
|---|---:|
| dense val ppl | 295.4278 |
| dense-limit val ppl (top_k = E) | 295.4278 |
| untrained top-k-vs-all oracle | 0.5463 |
| **trained top-k-vs-all oracle** | **0.4077** |
| trained router | 0.5144 |
| effective experts (of 4) | 3.97 |
| vs-original-dense residual | 0.7232 -> 2.9449 |

**Finding 1 (the mechanism works):** training reduced the intrinsic sparsity gap
by ~25% on a tiny model (0.546 -> 0.408). This is the signal the experiment
exists to detect, but at toy scale it is **not** evidence about 0.8B or 27B.

**Finding 2 (a real device bug, caught by the self-test):** the partition plan
is built on the CPU, so the `assignment` buffer and the expert-init noise landed
on the CPU next to CUDA weights. This raised inside `index_select` and would have
failed **every** CUDA run — i.e. every Kaggle session. The buffer now follows the
weights, and `test_module_follows_weights_onto_the_gpu` pins it.

**Finding 3 (a metric that can exceed 1.0):** `vs_original_dense` went 0.72 ->
2.94. Once training rescales experts to compensate for the experts that are never
selected, the output can end up *farther* from the original dense function than
predicting zero would be. That is the output-scale problem a sparse partial sum
creates (see `docs/upcycling_design.md` §5), and it is why the decisive metric is
the top-k-vs-all-experts residual on the trained weights, not the preservation of
the original function.

## E13 — Kaggle notebook for the real run

**Command:** `python -c "import nbformat; nbformat.validate(...)"`

**Outcome:** `kaggle/upcycle_poc.ipynb` builds and validates as nbformat 4.5
(8 cells). It checks the GPU, installs deps, fetches the repo (clone or uploaded
dataset), runs the self-test, runs the real grid, and tabulates results. Setup and
constraints are in `kaggle/README.md`.

**Later executed as E15:** the notebook was run on Kaggle (2x Tesla T4), which is
what produced `results/kaggle_upcycle_poc.json` and
`results/kaggle_upcycle_poc_seed1.json`. See E15 for the outcome.

## E14 — Upcycling PoC on the real model (does training beat the frozen ceiling?)

**Command:**

```
python3 experiments/upcycle_poc.py --model Qwen/Qwen3.5-0.8B \
  --experts 8 --topks 1 2 4 --combine sum --plan random \
  --layers 0 3 8 23 --steps 300 --lr 1e-4 --seq-len 512 \
  --grad-accum 4 --corpus-chars 4000000 --out results/upcycle_poc.json
```

Ran on the local RTX 4060 (7800 MiB free after stopping an idle ollama model),
real `qwen3_5_text` architecture, 4M characters of streamed `fineweb-edu`
(883,186 tokens; 1638 train / 86 val blocks of 512). 300 steps ~= 180 s per
configuration. Only the four upcycled layers train (44.1M params).

**Outcome.** `dense val ppl` 21.74; the dense-limit check (`top_k = E`, sum)
gives 21.74 — the MoE plumbing is exact on the real model.

| k | active | oracle untrained | oracle trained | frozen pilot oracle | beats frozen | router | eff. experts | ppl untrained | ppl trained |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|:--:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| 1 | 12.5% | 0.8584 | **0.8501** | 0.8624 | **True** | 0.8864 | 7.37 | 112.0 | 33.8 |
| 2 | 25% | 0.7520 | **0.7383** | 0.7578 | **True** | 0.7932 | 7.69 | 73.2 | 30.6 |
| 4 | 50% | 0.5638 | **0.5368** | 0.5710 | **True** | 0.6093 | 7.89 | 48.9 | 28.4 |

**Three findings, in order of importance.**

1. **Training does beat the frozen ceiling — but only just.** The improvement is
   1.0% (k=1), 1.8% (k=2) and 4.8% (k=4) relative. It grows with the active
   fraction, i.e. with how much freedom the router has, which is coherent.
   **Superseded by E15**, which pushed the same k=1 measurement out to E=32 and
   E=64 and found the win inverts there. Read this as "training wins at E=8", not
   as a property of the method.
2. **The residual is dominated by partial-sum scaling, not by learned structure.**
   With `combine="sum"`, keeping k of E roughly equal slices leaves a residual of
   about (1 - k/E) *regardless of training* — and that is what the numbers show
   (0.85 at 12.5%, 0.54 at 50%). Training moves it only slightly, which is the
   same "no latent expert structure" result the pilot (E7) found, now confirmed
   under training rather than assumed.
3. **End to end, training helps a lot but still loses.** ppl goes 112 -> 33.8
   (k=1), 73 -> 30.6 (k=2), 49 -> 28.4 (k=4) against a dense 21.7. So the trained
   MoE is 31-56% worse than dense at these fractions — *and* that figure is
   flattered, because the trained layers also saw 1638 in-corpus training blocks.
   Note the divergence: E2E ppl improves sharply while the reconstruction residual
   barely moves. Training is **adapting the function**, not creating extractable
   sparsity.

**Cross-validation:** the untrained oracles (0.8584 / 0.7520 / 0.5638)
independently reproduce the pilot's frozen oracles (0.8624 / 0.7578 / 0.5710) to
within ~0.5% on a separate run of the pipeline.

**Four real bugs, all found by running on real artifacts and all fixed:**

1. **The partition buffer was built on the CPU next to CUDA weights** — every
   CUDA run would have failed in `index_select`.
2. **`torch.sum` is on autocast's float32-promote list**, so the router's combine
   weights came back float32 while `y` stayed bf16 — every autocast training run
   would have failed in `index_add_`.
3. **`Tensor.quantile` rejects bf16**, so the library's own oracle diagnostics
   crashed on half-precision activations; residual metrics are now float32.
4. **Labels were double-shifted.** HF causal LM heads shift labels internally, so
   pre-shifting in `make_blocks` measured a misaligned loss — reading as dense ppl
   **20,664** instead of the true **21.7**.

Bugs 1-3 were invisible to a float32 CPU test suite; bug 4 was invisible until the
absolute number was sanity-checked against a known-good text. All four are now
covered by tests or an explicit warning.

## E15 — Upcycling PoC at the target ratio, 24 layers on free Kaggle GPUs

**Command:** `kaggle/upcycle_poc.ipynb` pushed with `kaggle kernels push -p kaggle/`
(kernel `trowel3444/moeme-upcycling-poc`). Raw session log: `kaggle/kaggle_run.log`.

```
python3 experiments/upcycle_poc.py --model Qwen/Qwen3.5-0.8B \
  --experts 64 32 8 --topks 1 --combine sum --plan random \
  --layers all --steps 300 --lr 1e-4 --seq-len 512 --grad-accum 4 \
  --save-every 0 --max-seconds 5400 --out upcycle_poc.json
# then the same config once more at E=64 with --seed 1
```

Ran on 2x Tesla T4 15.6 GB (Kaggle free tier, driver CUDA 13.0, torch 2.11.0+cu128,
transformers 5.6.2). Real `qwen3_5_text`, real streamed `fineweb-edu` (883,186
tokens; 1638 train / 86 val blocks of 512), bf16 autocast, **all 24 FFN layers**
upcycled (265.8M trainable params) — the E=64/all-layers grid E14 could not afford.

**This is the experiment E14 was a proxy for.** E14 ran E=8 on 4 layers. This runs
the exact `E/k ≈ 64` ratio the 27B design in `docs/upcycling_design.md` §1 needs,
over every layer.

| E | active | frozen pilot oracle | oracle *trained* | router *trained* | beats frozen | ppl untrained | ppl trained |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|:--:|---:|---:|
| 8 | 12.5% | 0.8624 | **0.8560** | 0.8951 | **True** (by 0.006) | 653,536 | 109.5 |
| 32 | 3.13% | 0.9188 | **0.9355** | 0.9653 | **False** | 1,464,554 | 159.3 |
| 64 | 1.56% | 0.9277 | **0.9549** | 0.9806 | **False** | 624,690 | 202.5 |

Dense ppl **19.562**. The dense-limit check (`top_k = E`, sum) reproduces it at
19.573 / 19.592 / 19.587, so the MoE plumbing is exact on the real model. `beats
frozen` is computed only over the four layers the pilot measured (0, 3, 8, 23).

**The finding: the small training win does not survive to the ratio that matters,
and at E=64 it inverts.**

1. **E=8 reproduces E14** (0.8560 vs E14's 0.8501, both below the frozen 0.8624).
   The mechanism E14 found is real — at 12.5% active, training does create a little
   structure.
2. **At E=32 and E=64 training makes the reconstruction *worse* than the frozen
   geometric partition** — 0.9188 → 0.9355 and 0.9277 → 0.9549 — and the deficit
   grows with E (−0.017, then −0.027). The trend runs the wrong way for the target:
   the sparser the ratio, the more training hurts.
3. **The learned router is worse than the oracle at every ratio, and worse than
   the frozen partition at every ratio** (0.8951 / 0.9653 / 0.9806 vs 0.8624 /
   0.9188 / 0.9277). Its distance from the frozen number *grows* with E (+0.033,
   +0.047, +0.053 at E=8/32/64) even as its distance from the *oracle* shrinks
   (0.039, 0.030, 0.026) — routing gets relatively better and still falls
   further behind, because the oracle route is itself falling behind. At E=64 the
   router reached 33.2 of 64 effective experts and still lost to simply keeping
   the frozen split.
4. **Training trades dense-equivalence away for loss.** `vs_original_dense` — the
   residual against the dense FFN the experts were partitioned from — *rises*
   under training at every E (0.9324 → 0.9480, 0.9839 → 0.9902, 0.9919 →
   0.9974), while perplexity falls by three orders of magnitude (624,690 → 202.5
   at E=64). The optimizer is re-purposing the experts away from the split they
   were initialised to be, which is precisely what costs it the oracle ceiling.
5. **End to end it is still not close.** The best trained MoE (E=8, 109.5) is
   **5.6x** the dense perplexity, and the target ratio (E=64, 202.5) **10.4x**.

**Reproducibility and cross-validation, in the repository's favour:**

- The **untrained** oracle reproduces the pilot's frozen number on the *same four
  layers* almost exactly — E=64 0.9292 vs 0.9277, E=32 0.9194 vs 0.9188, E=8
  0.8583 vs 0.8624, i.e. agreeing to **0.0015 or better**. Two independent
  pipelines, one on an RTX 4060 and one on T4s, computing the same geometric
  ceiling from different code paths. The all-24-layer mean is higher (0.9335 at
  E=64) simply because layers 0/3/8/23 are the four easiest of the 24.
- **A second seed reproduces the inversion at E=64** to three decimals:
  frozen 0.9277, trained oracle 0.9551 (vs 0.9549), router 0.9802 (vs 0.9806).
  This is not seed noise.

| | E=64 seed 0 | E=64 seed 1 |
|---|---:|---:|
| frozen oracle | 0.9277 | 0.9277 |
| oracle trained | 0.9549 | 0.9551 |
| router trained | 0.9806 | 0.9802 |

**Measured cost**, which is what makes this a Kaggle job rather than a laptop one:
11.89 s/step at E=64, 8.12 at E=32, 5.05 at E=8 (24 layers, seq 512, grad-accum 4),
purely because the MoE forward loops over all E experts. Wall clock per
configuration: 3,786 s / 2,446 s / 1,522 s; the whole session, including the
self-test and the seed-1 replicate, was **3.25 h** against a 12 h cap.

**Verdict.** `docs/upcycling_design.md` §9 states two kill criteria: (1) trained
`oracle_topk` does not fall below the frozen pilot oracle at the same (E, k), and
(2) the gap fails to shrink as E grows. **E=32 and E=64 trigger criterion 1**, and
the gap *widening* with E triggers the spirit of criterion 2 as well. The retrofit
route fails on its own stated gate, measured rather than extrapolated — and the
"residual ~0.96 at 1.6% active" figure E14 could only extrapolate is now
**exceeded in reality at 0.9549**.

**Scope limit, added after a literature review (`docs/prior_work.md`): this
measures an initialisation, not upcycling.** Four specific mismatches:

1. **Token budget.** E15 trained 614k tokens. LLaMA-MoE — which partitions the FFN
   *the same way* — used **200B tokens on 112 A100-80G**, and states that models
   *"should be trained for at least 15~20B tokens to properly conclude"* anything
   about partition methods. E15 is ~30,000x below that floor.
2. **No output rescaling.** `combine="sum"` with k of E equal slices leaves
   about (1 − k/E). LLaMA-MoE rescale expert outputs by N/k and call it
   *"significantly better"*; NVIDIA scale weights by ∛(E·G²/T) for **1.5% better
   loss** and report the naive version *"did not converge to the original loss."*
   `docs/upcycling_design.md` §5 listed this as mitigation #1 and it was never
   implemented in E14 or E15.
3. **Gating.** E15 used an unweighted partial sum. NVIDIA consistently found
   **softmax-then-topK beats topK-then-softmax**; NLP MoE practice is a weighted
   sum. The weaker scheme was tested.
4. **Partition axis.** E15 partitioned by **weights**. CMoE (arXiv:2502.04416)
   shows the informative axis is the **activation pattern**, with the
   always-active neuron subset split into an always-on *shared* expert. Their
   stated failure mode for weight-based partitioning is verbatim what E15
   measured: always-active neurons scattered across experts, which *"forces the
   router to activate most experts regardless of input"* — hence `eff_experts`
   33.2 of 64 and `load_max_over_mean` 9.25.

One E15 observation is independently confirmed: LLaMA-MoE found *"deep layers have
more routing preferences than shallow layers"* and suggested partitioning later
layers instead — matching E15's layer-23 concentration (PR/E 0.185, top-1 share
0.82, oracle 0.431 vs 0.68-0.85 for layers 0/3/8).

## E16 — Branching vs slicing: are experts *complete modules* or *slices*?

**Command:**

```
python3 experiments/branch_poc.py --self-test      # 18 offline checks, ~2 s
python3 experiments/branch_poc.py --model Qwen/Qwen3.5-0.8B --dtype bfloat16 \
  --layers 0 3 8 23 --seq-len 512 --grad-accum 4 --steps 80 --branch-steps 40 \
  --btx --domain-chars 800000 --corpus-chars 3000000 --eval-blocks 4 \
  --out results/branch_poc_08b.json
```

16 configurations, local RTX 4060, `bf16`, TF32 disabled, 3M chars of streamed
`fineweb-edu` (661,966 tokens), 4 held-out val blocks, dense val ppl **15.4803**.
Full analysis and the table: `docs/branching_vs_slicing.md`; raw evidence:
`results/branch_poc_08b.json`, `results/branch_poc_08b_table.txt`.

**What was tested.** `src/moeme/branch.py` builds a top-k FFN whose experts are
**complete** copies of the dense layer (`init="replica"`) or the E14/E15 disjoint
**slices** (`init="tile"`), from the same weights, with the same router and the
same diagnostics. Experts can be forced (`set_force_expert`) so one can be
trained alone on its own data before a router exists; `n_shared` adds always-on
experts (the CMoE / DeepSeek-MoE fix); `--btx` runs branch-train-miX: stage 2
trains each expert independently on a different domain (code, math, chat, wiki),
stage 3 trains router and experts jointly.

**Outcome — five findings, in order of importance.**

1. **The objection is right.** A full-width replica at k=1 reproduces the dense
   model *exactly*: ppl **15.49** against dense 15.48, residual 0.0038 (the bf16
   floor; 0.0000 at one expert, 1e-6 in fp32). No slicing configuration does this
   unless k = E. E14/E15's degradation is therefore structural — the construction
   was wrong for the question — **not** a training failure. This is the headline.
2. **Width, not tiling, governs the deficit.** At equal active width the best
   single expert agrees to ~0.02 between the two constructions: 0.7104 vs 0.7307
   at 12.5%, 0.6339 vs 0.6530 at 25%, 0.4975 vs 0.5134 at 50%. At 50% the oracle
   even favours tiling (0.4820 vs 0.5134), because a tile may pick the best of E
   blocks while a replica has one block and nothing to route between
   (`best1 == oracle == router` on every replica row).
3. **The tile-vs-replica ppl gap at equal width is router initialisation, not
   architecture.** At 25% active: 45.63 (tile) vs 24.93 (replica) with almost
   identical single-expert residuals; the untrained tile router picks a random
   block (0.8878 against a 0.6339 best). Training it recovers most of the gap
   (45.63 -> 23.19). Reporting only ppl for these two constructions measures the
   router, not the MoE.
4. **Dense residual is the wrong objective once anything is trained.** In every
   trained row the router residual *rises* while ppl *improves*: 0.8852 -> 0.9303
   with ppl 45.63 -> 23.19; 0.0044 -> 0.5491 with ppl 15.49 -> 19.11. Drift from
   the dense function is what finetuning does. E14/E15 used this residual as the
   decisive gate; it cannot arbitrate a trained model.
5. **Training a lossless configuration only makes it worse at this budget, and
   domain specialisation helps exactly where a division of labour exists.**
   `replica_E4_k1_full` starts at dense quality by construction and ends at 19.11
   (joint) / 19.14 (domain-specialised) / 22.78 (with a full-width shared expert)
   after 80 steps. But `btx_tile_k1_25pct` reaches **21.64** at 1.00x storage and
   25% active width against **23.19** for the same architecture trained on mixed
   data for the same 80 stage-3 steps — 1.55 ppl better, because a 25%-wide tile
   has to earn the missing 75% and pre-specialised experts give the router
   something to route between. At full width the effect vanishes (19.14 vs
   19.11), because an exact copy has nothing left to specialise toward.

**Secondary.** Routed experts do **not** collapse here: usage on the validation
batch is spread (layer 0 `[0.26, 0.25, 0.22, 0.27]`, layer 23
`[0.28, 0.17, 0.07, 0.48]`), so E15's `eff_experts = 33.2/64` failure does not
reproduce under either branching construction — consistent with its cause being
always-active neurons scattered across disjoint slices.

**Cost of the lossless case: 4.00x the dense FFN.** Branching does not make a
model smaller than dense; it makes the per-token *read* smaller. On 8 GB the
binding constraint is storage, so the quantisation ladder remains the route to a
27B on this machine.

**Tooling note.** The driver gained `--self-test` (18 offline checks),
`--dtype`, `--resume` and `--only`. `--resume`/`--only` were added after a
first attempt was killed part-way through the full-width configurations; because
the JSON is rewritten after every configuration, the completed ladder survived
and the expensive configurations were retried on their own. GPU peak for the
heaviest configuration (four full-width experts on four layers) is 5.97 GB of
8.19 GB, which is why it is the one that gets killed first.

## E17 — Does expert offloading (hot in VRAM, cold in RAM) rescue the dense 27B?

**Question.** "Could we not offload the not-used modules to RAM and keep the hot
ones in VRAM?" It is a real technique and it is worth separating from the claim
it is usually attached to.

**Command:**

```
python3 tools/bench_inference.py --spec ornith-1.5-35b-a3b:q2_k:-1:32 \
  --out results/inference_moe.json
python3 tools/bench_inference.py --spec ornith-1.5:35b:-1:32 \
  --out results/inference_moe_q4.json
```

Full analysis: `docs/expert_offloading.md`.

**Outcome — answer: no for the dense 27B, and the reason is the architecture, not
the runtime.** Measured on this machine, same runtime, same 14.3 GB of usable
fast memory:

| model | arch | total/active | quant | file | resident? | decode |
|---|---|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| `qwen3.8:latest` | dense | 27.8B / 27.8B | Q4_K_M | 17.0 GB | no | 0.561 tok/s |
| `ornith-1.5-35b-a3b:q2_k` | MoE 256x8 | 35.5B / ~3B | Q2_K | 13.0 GB | **yes** | **19.356 tok/s** |
| `ornith-1.5:35b` | MoE 256x8 | 35.5B / ~3B | Q4_K_M | 22.0 GB | no | 6.450 tok/s |

The two `qwen35moe` rows are the **same architecture** (256 experts, top-8,
expert FFN 512, shared FFN 512) differing only in quantisation, which isolates
the two effects:

1. **Sparsity beats size.** A 35.5B MoE at Q4_K_M is **11.5x** faster than a
   27.8B *dense* model at Q4_K_M. The bigger model wins, because a token reads 8
   of 256 experts instead of every weight.
2. **Residency is worth 3.0x by itself.** The same MoE at 13.0 GB (fits) against
   22.0 GB (does not) goes 19.356 -> 6.450 tok/s.

All three rows are explained by one equation, with bytes read per token as the
numerator: 17.0 GB / 1783 ms ~= 9.5 GB/s effective for the dense model; ~1.83 GB
/ 155 ms ~= 11.8 GB/s for the Q4 MoE; ~1.05 GB / 52 ms ~= 20.3 GB/s for the Q2
MoE. The tier mix sets bandwidth; **sparsity sets the volume**, and volume is
what differs by ~9x.

**Why it cannot help the dense 27B.** Offloading chooses *where each byte comes
from*, never *which bytes are needed* — and in a dense model that set is the
whole model. There is no cold subset to push out. Moving layers to RAM re-labels
