17.0 GB per token rather than reducing it, and 17.0 GB does not fit in the 14.3
GB of fast tiers at any placement. The lever that works remains quantisation:
Q3_K_M at 11.8 GB is fully resident, predicted 4.85 tok/s against the measured
0.561.

Also worth recording: `num_gpu = -1` (ollama's default, "offload what fits")
was used in all three runs, so the VRAM/RAM/disk split was already the one the
question proposes. Peak VRAM was 6730-6932 MiB of 8188 in every case. The idea
is not missing from the runtime; it is missing from the model.

**Consequence for the E16 route.** An MoE whose experts are complete modules is
lossless *because* it reads full width, so it saves no bytes — speed and quality
trade along the same axis and offloading does not move that axis. Models that do
win here (`qwen35moe`, Qwen3-30B-A3B, Mixtral) were pretrained with hundreds of
experts, which is the part a retrofit cannot buy at this scale.

---

## E18 — Is the fast MoE actually as good as the slow dense 27B?

**Question.** E17 explained the speed (sparsity + residency). Nothing had
measured whether the fast option *answers* as well. "It ran fast" is not a
quality result, and the whole Path-A case rests on the fast model being good
enough.

**Method.** The repo's own gate (E9/E10), unchanged task definitions, both models
on identical prompts at temperature 0, fixed seed, `--think off`, identical
`num_predict`, checkpoint after every task. Five harness capabilities were added
to make a 0.34 tok/s model comparable with a 43 tok/s one: `--think off`,
`--tasks` (fixed id subset), `--resume` + per-task checkpointing, `--backend
llama`/`--chat-template-kwargs` (score the same tasks through a different engine)
and `--rescore` (replay corrected task definitions over stored responses).

**The dense 27B is the constraint.** 30 tasks on both models: the MoE finished all
30 in **115 s**; the dense model finished 2 in ~25 minutes, projecting to ~6 h. That
run was stopped and kept as `results/eval_qwen3.8-latest_full30_abandoned.json`.
The comparison therefore uses a 19-task subset (3 coding / 4 reasoning / 6 format
/ 6 instruction).

**Result at `num_predict=192` (19 tasks).**

| model | overall | coding | format | instruction | reasoning | capped | excl. capped | wall |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| `ornith-1.5-35b-a3b:q2_k` | 15/19 = 78.9% | 3/3 | 6/6 | 4/6 | 2/4 | 2 | 15/17 = 88.2% | 55 s |
| `qwen3.8:latest` (dense) | 13/19 = 68.4% | 3/3 | 6/6 | 4/6 | 0/4 | **4** | 13/15 = 86.7% | 3958 s |

**All four of the dense model's reasoning failures were truncations**
(`done_reason: length`): it was cut off mid-solution while reasoning correctly.
The MoE writes more compactly and capped only twice. On everything the cap did not
cut, the two are indistinguishable — and the reasoning column was unmeasured for
one of them.

**Re-running the reasoning column at a budget both can finish
(`num_predict=768`):** both models **4/4**, answering 0.05, 3, 47, 42. The MoE
took **41 s**, the dense model **3517 s** — ~86x. So the small-cap gap was a
harness budget, not capability, and the MoE's full 19-task score at that cap is
17/19 = 89.5%, failing only the two genuine instruction cases (a 6-word answer
where 5 were required, and a forbidden letter).

**Two defects in the repo's own task set, and one in its scorer.** Found by
running it, fixed, and pinned by tests:

1. `r_bat_ball` expected 0.10; the ball is `(1.10-1.00)/2 = 0.05`. This failed the
   *correct* answer — it is why the MoE's row was a FAIL in the first run.
2. `r_modular` expected 1; `7**20 % 13 = 3`. This passed an *incorrect* answer.
3. Truncation was labelled only when the answer text was EMPTY. A capped run that
   narrated first recorded a bare "got 3.0, want 42", which reads as a wrong
   answer; this mis-scored 3 of the dense model's 4 reasoning rows. Rows now carry
   a `truncated` flag, reports print the capped count and the score excluding
   capped rows, and `--rescore` re-derived the flag for reports written before it
   existed.

`test_numeric_expectations_are_arithmetically_correct` re-derives every closed-form
expectation instead of trusting the literal; `test_every_coding_case_is_
satisfiable_by_a_correct_solution` runs a reference solution against every coding
case, because a wrong expected value fails a correct model and must fail the suite
first. `--rescore` then re-scored the completed runs from their stored text
(MoE 14/19 → 15/19) instead of re-generating them.

**Memory safety, sampled rather than assumed.** Over 59 minutes of dense
generation: RAM available never fell below **5.79 GiB**, peak VRAM **6811 of 8188
MiB**, no OOM kill (181 samples, `logs/mem_watch_dense768.log`). The llama.cpp
driver refuses to start unless 6 GiB RAM and 6000 MiB VRAM are free, and it
refused correctly once — while the dense model held 6654 MiB of the card.

**Engine comparison (the surplus question).** Same 19 tasks, same 768 cap, through
llama.cpp at surplus's planner placement `-ngl 42 -ncmoe 24`, f16 KV:
**17/19 = 89.5%, identical to the ollama tag, same two failures.** An engine or
placement changes where bytes come from, not which weights the model needs — now
measured rather than asserted. Two corrections the measurement forced:

- A raw `/completion` request applies no chat template, so the model's `<think>`
  block went into the answer and the same run scored **13/19**. Matching ollama's
  `think: false` requires `chat_template_kwargs={"enable_thinking": false}` on
  `/v1/chat/completions`. A 21-point score difference from a request setting.
- The earlier "×2.59 decode / ×10.4 prefill" claims compared against ollama's
  **32-token cold** baseline. Warm, on the same tasks, ollama runs 43.1 tok/s
  decode and 83.4 tok/s prompt, so the real gains are **×1.12 / ×1.21**, with
  ×1.75 wall for the 19 tasks. The placement stands; the multiples did not.

**surplus's own paths, measured.**`surplus bench` crashes before measuring (the
engine it pins, `llama.cpp-moe-cache`, segfaults on any `-ncmoe` in its
`llama-bench`; upstream runs the same flags to completion). `surplus shim` serves
the model but returns **HTTP 200 with an empty `response`** for every task (a
direct probe: `eval_count: 32`, `response: ""`), so it cannot be scored at all;
quarantined in `logs/surplus_shim_empty_responses.json`. Its auto-plan in the shim
chose `-n-cpu-moe 36` and decoded at **3.41 tok/s** against 48-50 tok/s for the
plan its own solver ranks first. Its default RAM cage is **4 GiB**, computed from
the model's *active* parameters, which is below what a 13 GB MoE needs when 36
layers of experts are host-side. And `--engine-dir` takes a checkout *root*;
passing a `build` directory is silently ignored. `surplus native` refuses
`qwen35moe` by design, so it cannot run any model here.

**Equal quantisation: the architecture question, answered.** §3 compares two
artifacts a user would choose between, but they differ in architecture *and* bits
per weight (2.8 vs 4.9). Holding the quantisation fixed required only a model that
was already on this machine: `ornith-1.5:35b` is the **same `qwen35moe`
architecture at Q4_K_M** (22.0 GB), so against the dense 27B at Q4_K_M (17.0 GB)
it isolates architecture alone. Same runtime, same engine, same 19 tasks.

| run (all Q4_K_M) | coding | format | instruction | reasoning | overall | wall | decode |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| MoE `ornith-1.5:35b` (22 GB) | 3/3 | 5/6 | 4/6 | **4/4** | 16/19 = 84.2% | 464 s | **6.14 tok/s** |
| dense `qwen3.8:latest` (17 GB) | 3/3 | 6/6 | 4/6 | **4/4** | 13/15 + 4/4 | 3958 s / 3517 s | 0.32 tok/s |

The dense row is its two measured configurations (15 non-reasoning rows at a 192
cap, where nothing truncated, and 4 reasoning rows at 768). Reasoning ties;
the non-reasoning half differs by **one task**, and across all three measured
models the failures move inside the same small set of exact-match constraints
(`i_no_letter_e` fails everywhere; `i_five_words`, `i_lowercase_start` and
`f_json_array` each fail for one model and pass for another). At six tasks per
category and one run each, ±1 is noise.

**So sparsity costs nothing measurable and buys ~19x at equal bits.** The MoE is
not the compromise option for quality; the reason to run Q2_K on this machine is
memory, not quality — at equal bits the 22 GB MoE is 7x *slower* than the 13 GB
one (6.14 vs 43.1 tok/s) because it does not fit the fast tiers either. It still
beats the dense model by reading 8 of 256 experts per token rather than the whole
file.

**Memory safety, second run.** The 22 GB model — the configuration that does not
fit — held a **5.32 GiB** RAM-available floor over 92 samples with a 6934 MiB VRAM
peak, under an armed watchdog (`tools/mem_watchdog.py`, floor 2.0 GiB) that never
fired, on a host with **no free swap**. Writing the watchdog first was not
ceremony: its own tests caught a defect where a *zombie* target still answers
`os.kill(pid, 0)`, so the guard would have spun on a finished run and never
reported. `--pidfile` exists so the guard arms on the eval's real PID rather than
a wrapper's.

**Conclusion for the 27B decision.** The MoE is not the compromise option on this
machine: on this task set it matches the dense 27B while being ~86x faster on the
tasks that need room to think, and at equal quantisation the architecture is not
the thing costing quality. What remains unmeasured is behaviour outside this
19-task set, where Q2_K's 2.8 bpw is the live risk — and the equal-quant run is
the evidence that if you have the RAM for Q4_K_M, use it.
→ `docs/quality_vs_speed.md`, `results/eval_*_short19*.json`,
`results/eval_*_reason768.json`, `results/eval_llama_ngl42_ncmoe24.json`,
`logs/mem_watch_dense768.log`.

---

## Not executed (stated plainly)

- **E18's open threads.** Not run: a second run of any category, so every score
  here is one sample and the one-task gap in the equal-quant comparison is an
  inference rather than a measurement; any placement other than
  `-ngl 42 -ncmoe 24` under the quality gate; token-exact agreement between
  engines (only the *scores* were compared); and any task outside this 19-task
  set, which is exactly where a 2.8 bpw quantisation would be most likely to
  show up. Repairing surplus is also out of scope from here -- its bench
  segfault, its empty shim responses and its 4 GiB cage are its own code, and
  E18 records what they do on this host rather than fixing them.
- A *complete* evaluation set. A 30-task starter set with executable checks now
exists and has been run (E10), but the handoff's full 30/30/20/20 split, public
benchmarks, and contamination checks are not there. The pilot (E7) measures
routing structure, not task quality.
- Workstream B's runtime work: no llama.cpp/GGUF build, no `io_uring` vs mmap
  comparison, no prefetch policy, no pinned host buffers, no native-MTP or
  speculative-decoding experiments.
- Real 27B activations. The real-weight experiment (E6) uses synthetic inputs,
  so its oracle numbers probe weight structure only.
- CPU-only runs of the qwen3_5 family (blocked: CUDA-only `causal_conv1d`).
- **The upcycling PoC at other points in the grid.** E15 settled the decisive
  cell — E in {8, 32, 64} at k=1, all 24 layers, the ~1.6% active ratio the 27B
  target needs — and a second seed at E=64. **Not** run: k>1 at E=32/64, the
  aux-loss sweep, `combine=softmax`, the `clustered` plan under training, a
  corpus the trained layers have never seen for the ppl number, or any scaling
  study. Those would sharpen the mechanism; none of them is expected to reverse
  a 0.027 deficit.
- **A trained-MoE run at 27B.** Everything here is at 0.8B. The claim is that the
  mechanism fails at small scale on its own gate, not that 27B continued
  pretraining would fail — though E15 removes the reason to try.
- Fine-tuning of anything. No free tier was used, and no claim about fine-tuning
  a 27B model on free tiers is made.


===== docs/expert_offloading.md =====

# Expert offloading: keep the hot modules in VRAM, push the cold ones to RAM

**The short answer: yes, and it is already happening — but it cannot rescue the
dense 27B, because a dense layer has no cold modules.** A token reads *every*
FFN weight in a dense model, so "hot" means "all of it". Offloading works by not
reading the unused experts, and that requires the model to have unused experts.

This document is the measurement. All three numbers were taken on this machine
with the same runtime (ollama), one variable at a time.

## The three points, measured

| model | arch | total / active | quant | file | fits 14.3 GB fast memory? | decode |
|---|---|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| `qwen3.8:latest` | **dense** | 27.8B / 27.8B | Q4_K_M | 17.0 GB | no (2.7 GB streams) | **0.561 tok/s** |
| `ornith-1.5-35b-a3b:q2_k` | MoE 256×8 | 35.5B / ~3B | Q2_K | 13.0 GB | **yes** | **19.356 tok/s** |
| `ornith-1.5:35b` | MoE 256×8 | 35.5B / ~3B | Q4_K_M | 22.0 GB | no (7.7 GB streams) | **6.450 tok/s** |

`qwen35moe` geometry (from the GGUF headers): `expert_count 256`,
`expert_used_count 8`, `expert_feed_forward_length 512`,
`expert_shared_feed_forward_length 512`.

Raw: `results/inference_moe.json`, `results/inference_moe_q4.json`,
`results/inference_27b.json`.

Two facts fall straight out of the table:

* **Sparsity beats size.** A *35.5B* MoE at Q4_K_M decodes **11.5x** faster than a
  *27.8B* dense model at the same quantisation, on the same machine. The MoE is
  the bigger model and wins.
* **Residency is worth 3x on its own.** The *same* MoE, only 13.0 GB instead of
  22.0 GB, goes from 6.450 to 19.356 tok/s — a **3.0x** gain from nothing but
  fitting in fast memory.

## Why, quantitatively

The whole table is one equation: **decode time = (bytes read per token) / (the
bandwidth of whichever tiers hold those bytes)**.

Measured tiers (`configs/tiers.json`): GPU memory 247 GB/s, RAM 40.7 GB/s
(memcpy; 24.4 GB/s single-thread read), NVMe **0.82-1.9 GB/s**. Usable fast
memory is **3.8 GiB VRAM + 10.2 GB RAM = 14.3 GB**.

| model | bytes read per token | measured | implied effective bandwidth |
|---|---:|---:|---:|
| dense 27.8B Q4_K_M | 17.0 GB (all of it) | 0.561 tok/s = 1783 ms | ~9.5 GB/s (RAM + disk mix) |
| MoE 35.5B Q4_K_M | ~1.83 GB (8 of 256 experts) | 6.450 tok/s = 155 ms | ~11.8 GB/s (RAM + disk mix) |
| MoE 35.5B Q2_K | ~1.05 GB | 19.356 tok/s = 52 ms | ~20.3 GB/s (RAM, resident) |

The MoE rows and the dense row pull bytes at *comparable effective bandwidth*.
The whole difference is the numerator: **the MoE needs ~9x fewer bytes per
token**, and because the 13 GB version also fits entirely in RAM+VRAM it gets the
tier's full speed instead of a disk-blended one.

Note what the `qwen35moe` rows say about caching. With 256 experts and top-8,
the working set over a generation is spread across a large fraction of the
experts, so the hit rate is far from 100% — the Q4 row only reaches ~11.8 GB/s
because a good part of it is landing in RAM rather than disk. Offloading is
therefore not a switch that makes the cold part free; it is a *hit-rate* problem.

This also explains, retroactively, why ollama's default (`num_gpu = -1`, i.e.
"offload what fits") was the right setting in all three runs: it is already
doing the VRAM/RAM/disk split the question asks about. Peak VRAM was 6730-6932
MiB of 8188 in every case, with the remainder in RAM.

## Why this does not rescue Qwen3.8-27B

Offloading chooses *where each byte comes from*. It cannot choose *which bytes
are needed*, and in a dense model that set is the whole model:

```
dense:  bytes per token  =  total parameters        (17.0 GB at Q4_K_M)
MoE:    bytes per token  =  active parameters       (~1.83 GB at Q4_K_M)
```

So there is nothing to declare cold. Moving layers to RAM does not shrink the
per-token read — it re-labels it, and 17.0 GB still has to cross into compute
every token. Worse, the fast tiers total 14.3 GB, so no arrangement of the dense
27B is fully resident at Q4.

What *does* work is making the model smaller, which is the same conclusion the
residency table reached:

| quant | size | fits? | predicted |
|---|---:|---|---:|
| Q4_K_M | 17.0 GB | no | 0.48 tok/s (measured 0.561) |
| Q3_K_M | 11.8 GB | **yes** | **4.85 tok/s** |
| Q2_K | 9.7 GB | yes | 6.44 tok/s |

Q3_K_M puts the entire 27B in the fast tiers, at which point there is again
nothing to offload — and the decode rate improves ~9x over the measured Q4
number purely because the disk leaves the path.

## The consequence for the MoE conversion idea

E16 showed that an MoE whose experts are **complete modules** is lossless at
`top_k = 1` — and measured a price: it reads a *full-width* FFN per token, so it
saves no bytes. That is not an accident of the implementation; it is why the
lossless case is lossless. **A conversion is only a speed win if the resulting
model activates a small fraction of its experts**, i.e. if `top_k * expert_units`
is a small share of the dense FFN width — which is exactly the regime where E16
measured the quality loss (residual 0.63-0.89, ppl 21.6-45.6 against a dense
15.48). Speed and quality trade along the same axis; offloading does not move it.

The models that get away with it (`qwen35moe` here, Qwen3-30B-A3B, Mixtral) do
so because they were **pretrained** with 256 experts and a large total budget, so
each of the 8 active experts is genuinely competent. That is the thing a
retrofit cannot buy at this scale.

## What to do instead

1. **On this laptop, run a model that already has native sparse activation.** At
   19.4 tok/s the 35.5B-A3B at Q2_K is ~35x more usable than the dense 27B, and
   it is a *larger* model. If quality is the concern, spend the budget on a
   better-quantised MoE that still fits 14.3 GB rather than on a bigger dense
   model that does not.
2. **For the dense 27B, quantise to fit.** Q3_K_M (11.8 GB) is the largest
   quantisation that is fully resident here, worth ~4.85 tok/s predicted against
   0.561 measured. That gate is worth running.
3. **If designing a hybrid, allocate by bytes-per-token, not by parameter
   count.** The quantity that predicts decode rate on this machine is
   `active_params * bytes_per_param / mix_bandwidth`. Anything that does not
   reduce the numerator — including a lossless expert conversion — cannot help,
   and the tier split only sets the denominator.

## Limitations

* One runtime (ollama), one machine, one prompt, `num_predict = 32`. Decode rate
  on a longer generation improves slightly as the page cache warms; these are
  near-cold-start figures and are therefore conservative.
* `num_gpu` was `-1` (runtime default) in all three runs, so the VRAM/RAM/disk
  split was chosen by the runtime, not by me. That is deliberate — it measures
  the configuration a user would actually get — but it means the tier split is
  not independently controlled in this comparison.
* The "bytes read per token" column is derived (`active_params * bytes_per_param`
  from the standard bpw of each quantisation), not instrumented. The measured
  decode rates and VRAM are direct.
* Q2_K at 2.8 bpw is an aggressive quantisation; the 19.4 tok/s figure is a
  speed measurement and says nothing about output quality, which was not
  evaluated here.
* Prefill is the other half of the problem and is much worse (3.8-11.6 tok/s
  across these runs). Nothing in this document addresses time-to-first-token,
  which is what makes long prompts painful.
* No swap pressure was observed in these runs (minimum available RAM 8.86-9.29
  GB), so the numbers are not contaminated by thrashing — but they were taken
  with the models the only large consumers of memory.


===== docs/feasibility_report.md =====

# Feasibility Report — Qwen3.8-27B-class assistant on an 8 GB / 16 GB laptop

**Bottom line.** The stated target ("~27B total, ~9B active, near-Qwen3.8-27B
quality, on 8 GB VRAM / 16 GB RAM") is **not reachable by converting
Qwen3.8-27B**, for a reason that is now proven arithmetically, not asserted. A
useful assistant *is* reachable on this hardware via a different route
(aggressive quantisation to full residency), and that route is cheap and
measurable. Details in `docs/decision_memo.md`.

Everything below was measured or computed on the target machine; nothing is
copied from a datasheet. Reproduce with `make doctor`, `make verify-sources`,
`make verify-real-ffn`, `make pilot`.

---

## 1. Verified architecture

Qwen3.8-27B is a **dense** 27.78B-parameter hybrid-attention vision-language
model (48 Gated DeltaNet layers + 16 gated-attention layers), Apache-2.0, with a
native MTP head. Full accounting in `docs/source_validation.md`. Two facts drive
everything else:

- **FFN = 17,112,760,320 params (61.6%).**
- **Non-FFN text backbone = 9,783,238,144 params (9.78B)** and is active on
  every token.

## 2. Verified constraints (all measured on this machine)

| Quantity | Measured | Note |
|---|---:|---|
| VRAM total | 8188 MiB | RTX 4060 Laptop |
| VRAM **usable** | **~3.8 GiB** | the desktop already holds ~2.9 GiB; ollama peaked at 3906 MiB of model memory |
| RAM total | 15.4 GiB | |
| RAM **usable** for weights | **~10.2 GB** (9.5 GiB) | measured `MemAvailable`; swap was already 3.2/4.0 GiB in use |
| GPU memory bandwidth | 246.8 GB/s | measured with a real kernel |
| CPU RAM bandwidth | 40.7 GB/s | memcpy; naive torch bf16 GEMV only 19.3 GB/s |
| **NVMe cold read** | **0.82–1.9 GB/s** | 3 independent methods; datasheet says ~6.6 |
| NVMe random 4 KiB | 11,419 IOPS, 88 us | |
| NVMe cold 1 MiB touch | 1.38 ms, 0.76 GB/s | mmap, page cache dropped per touch |

**End-to-end baselines** (ollama, real measurement):

| Model | Size | Decode | Prefill | Peak VRAM |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| `qwen3:4b` | 2.5 GB | **60.7 tok/s** | 560 tok/s | 6798 MiB |
| `ornith-1.5:9b` | 6.6 GB | **24.6 tok/s** | 139 tok/s | 6884 MiB |
| **`qwen3.8:latest` (the target, Q4_K_M)** | 17.0 GB | **0.561 tok/s** | **1.6 tok/s** | 6884 MiB |

The 27B at Q4_K_M is **~44x slower than a 9B** on this machine, and its prefill
of 1.6 tok/s means even filling a modest prompt takes minutes. It is not usable
for interactive work as-is.

## 3. Why the 9B-active target is non-trivial — the arithmetic

This is the load-bearing finding, and it is a proof rather than an opinion.

For any model keeping Qwen3.8-27B's backbone:

```
active_params_per_token  =  irreducible_backbone  +  active_FFN
```

The irreducible backbone — embeddings, LM head, Gated DeltaNet blocks, full
attention, norms — is **9,783,238,144 = 9.78B params** and is read on every
single token. Routing the FFN cannot reduce it. So:

```
to reach 9.0B active:  active_FFN = 9.0B - 9.783B = -0.78B   <-- impossible
```

**A perfect, free, lossless FFN router cannot reach the 9B-active target, because
the untouched backbone already exceeds it.** Reaching 9B active requires
shrinking or routing the *backbone* (attention + Gated DeltaNet + embeddings),
which is a retraining problem, not a routing tweak. This is asserted as an
executable test:
`tests/test_accounting.py::test_no_ffn_routing_can_reach_9b_active`.

Two further consequences of the same accounting:

- **The LM head alone is a real per-token cost.** vocab 248,320 x hidden 5120 =
  1.27B params, read every token. At 4.9 bpw that is ~0.78 GB/token; on CPU at
  40.7 GB/s it costs ~19 ms/token *by itself*, capping decode near 52 tok/s no
  matter how fast everything else is. Large-vocab heads are not free on the
  memory-bound path.
- **There is no natural sparsity to exploit.** See §4.

## 4. What the sparsity pilot actually found (decisive)

Full method in `experiments/pilot_sparsity.py`; raw data in
`results/pilot_sparsity.json`, table in `results/pilot_curve.txt`.

Setup: Qwen3.5-0.8B — the *same* `qwen3_5_text` architecture family as the 27B,
same 3:1 DeltaNet:attention pattern, FFN expansion 3.5 vs the 27B's 3.4 — with
**real weights and real activations** from a real forward pass.

**Result: at 12.5% active compute, even a perfect oracle router loses 42–81% of
the layer output.**

| Layer | Best oracle residual @ 12.5% active | Learned router | Router top-1 acc |
|---|---:|---:|---:|
| 0 | 0.681 | 0.807 | 0.348 |
| 3 | 0.802 | 0.872 | 0.309 |
| 8 | 0.811 | 0.857 | 0.254 |
| 23 | 0.417 | 0.449 | 0.809 |

(residual = relative L2 error of the k-expert reconstruction vs the true dense
layer output; 0 is exact, 1 is as bad as predicting zero)

Three sub-findings, each independently important:

1. **The partition sets the ceiling, not the router.** Oracle vs learned-router
   gap is only 0.0001–0.033. Training a better router cannot fix this — the
   ceiling is what a top-k selection can capture from a post-hoc split, and it
   is bad. This directly answers the handoff's "evaluate oracle selection …
   before spending training compute": the answer is *don't spend it*.
2. **Participation ratio ≈ E.** Energy is spread almost perfectly uniformly
   across partitions (PR = 31.6 of 32, 127 of 128 on the real 27B weights). The
   oracle residual only approaches 0 when k = E, i.e. when nothing is sparse at
   all. A trained dense FFN has no latent expert structure to discover.
3. **Weight-similarity clustering degenerates.** The k-means plan looks
   attractive (oracle residual 0.019 on the 27B) but is an artefact: for E=128 a
   **single cluster holds 9,014 of 17,408 units (52%)** — one giant expert plus
   many singletons. "Top 16 of 128" then means keeping half the parameters.
   Reporting this as a win would have been a mistake.

**Interpretation:** post-hoc FFN→MoE conversion of a trained dense model is not
viable. Any model at 9B active with near-27B quality must have its experts
*trained*, which is a pretraining-scale effort — far beyond free tiers.

### 4.1 And training the split does not fix it (E14/E15)

§4 bounds the *frozen* partition. The obvious objection is that a real conversion
trains, so `experiments/upcycle_poc.py` trains one: it upcycles the 0.8B's FFN
into a genuine top-k MoE, trains experts and router, then repeats the same
oracle-residual measurement on the trained weights. E14 ran E=8 on 4 layers
locally; E15 ran E=8/32/64 over **all 24 layers** on free Kaggle T4s, plus a
second seed. Residuals are relative L2 against the true dense FFN output.

| E | active | frozen pilot oracle | oracle **trained** | router **trained** | beats frozen | trained ppl (dense 19.6) |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|:--:|---:|
| 8 | 12.5% | 0.8624 | **0.8560** | 0.8951 | yes, by 0.006 | 109.5 |
| 32 | 3.13% | 0.9188 | **0.9355** | 0.9653 | **no** (+0.017) | 159.3 |
| 64 | 1.56% | 0.9277 | **0.9549** | 0.9806 | **no** (+0.027) | 202.5 |

Training helps only at the mildest sparsity and **actively hurts at the ratios a
conversion needs**, with the deficit growing as E grows. The learned router is
worse than the oracle everywhere, so improving routing is not the fix either. A
second seed reproduces the E=64 inversion to three decimals (0.9551/0.9802).
The mechanism is visible in the data: `vs_original_dense` — the residual against
the dense FFN the experts were partitioned from — **rises** under training at every
E (0.9919 → 0.9974 at E=64) while perplexity falls three orders of magnitude.
Training re-purposes the experts away from the split they were initialised to be;
that is what buys the perplexity and what costs the oracle ceiling. E14's "~0.96 at
1.6% active" was an extrapolation across k; E15 measured **0.9549** there.

**Scope, and an honest correction to the conclusion above.** This does *not* show
that training cannot reach the ceiling — it shows that 614k tokens of training on a
weight-partitioned split with an unrescaled sum does not. The published work trains
far longer and differently: LLaMA-MoE partitioned the FFN the same way and used
**200B tokens on 112 A100-80G** (stating that 15-20B are needed just to *compare*
partition methods), NVIDIA beat continued dense training at 1T tokens, and Sparse
Upcycling's cost is ~50% of the original pretraining compute. Both rescale expert
outputs — the fix §5 of the design doc named and E14/E15 never implemented. See
`docs/prior_work.md` for the full review. The **9B-active arithmetic in §3 is
unaffected**: it is a statement about the backbone, not about conversion quality.

## 5. What else is proven, and what is not

| Claim | Status | Evidence |
|---|---|---|
| Dense FFN partitioning is exactly dense-equivalent | **Proven** | worst rel. L2 error **5.1e-07** on real Qwen3.8-27B layer-1 weights across 4 plans x 2 input distributions (11 plan/experts combos: contiguous, interleaved and random at E in {8,32,128}, clustered at E in {8,32} only); 205 unit tests pass |
| Partitioning is free | **Disproven** | batch-1 GPU, small-GEMM overhead: 2.1-2.4x (E=32) and 2.9-3.9x (E=128) vs dense across two runs; top-2 of 32 costs the same as all-32, so sparsity recovers none of it |
| Post-hoc FFN sparsification can work | **Disproven** | §4 |
| Training a post-hoc MoE recovers the ceiling | **Disproven at the target ratio** | §4.1; E15: frozen 0.9277 → trained 0.9549 at E=64/k=1, seed-reproduced |
| 9B active reachable from this backbone | **Disproven** | §3 |
| 27B Q4_K_M runs at ~0.56 tok/s here | **Proven (measured)** | and the first-principles tier model independently predicted 0.57 |
| Memory-tier model is predictive | **Proven within ~2x** | 3/3 measured configs; 2% error on the 27B case |
| Free tiers allow 27B fine-tuning | **Not credible** | see decision memo |
| Kaggle/Lightning exact quotas | **Unverified** | their pages returned no readable quota text |
| End-to-end task quality of any converted model | **Not measured** | needs real 27B activations; explicitly out of scope here |

## 6. The opening that does exist

Decode is bandwidth-bound, so the only regime with usable speed is **full
residency**. From `tools/residency_table.py` (measured tier constants):

| Quant | bpw | Model size | Streamed/token | Predicted | Regime |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---|
| Q2_K | 2.8 | 9.7 GB | 0.00 | **6.44 tok/s** | resident |
| Q3_K_M | 3.4 | 11.8 GB | 0.00 | **4.85 tok/s** | resident |
| Q4_K_S | 4.4 | 15.3 GB | 1.00 | 1.07 tok/s | disk-bound |
| Q4_K_M | 4.9 | 17.0 GB | 2.74 | **0.48 tok/s** | disk-bound (measured: 0.561) |

Qwen3.8-27B at **Q3_K_M or below becomes fully resident** and should go from
0.56 tok/s to roughly 4–6 tok/s — an ~8x improvement for free, with **no
training and no architecture change**. That is the highest-value experiment on
the table, and it costs one download.

(Residency threshold across quantisation levels: 40.8B params at Q2_K, 33.6B at
Q3_K_M, 26.0B at Q4_K_S, 23.3B at Q4_K_M. The 27B sits between Q4_K_S and
Q3_K_M, which is why the level matters so much: it is the difference between
streaming 2.74 GB per token and streaming nothing.)

## 7. Risk assessment

| Risk | Severity | Mitigation |
|---|---|---|
| Q3/Q2 quantisation degrades coding/reasoning quality | **High** — this is now the *critical path* | Measure it directly: held-out task set at Q3 vs Q4 vs Q5, on this machine |
| SSD is anomalously slow; a healthy drive changes the design space | Medium | Check PCIe link width + drive health; re-run `make doctor` |
| `causal_conv1d` CUDA-only blocks CPU-offload designs | Medium | Already blocks CPU experimentation on this family; fix or use llama.cpp/GGUF |
| MoE conversion attempted anyway, wasting effort | Medium | §3 and §4 are the written basis for declining; both are executable tests |
| Tier model over-trusts itself | Low | Documented as a ceiling estimator validated within ~2x, not a forecast |
| Machine is a daily driver (desktop already holds 2.9 GiB VRAM) | Low | All tooling is non-destructive; temp files self-delete; GPU allocations <= 1 GiB |

## 8. Concrete initial experiments (in priority order)

1. **Quantisation quality ladder (no training, ~1 download).** Fetch
   Qwen3.8-27B at Q3_K_M / IQ3 / Q2_K; measure decode rate, VRAM/RAM peak, and
   held-out score vs the Q4_K_M baseline. Gate: >=95% of Q4 on coding and
   reasoning tasks, and >=4 tok/s. *This is the whole decision.*
2. **Fix or bound the SSD anomaly.** Check `lspci -vv` link width/state and
   drive health; re-measure. If the drive is being throttled, streaming designs
   become far more attractive than they look today.
3. **Build the held-out evaluation set** the handoff specifies (30 coding, 30
   reasoning/maths, 20 instruction/tool-calling, 20 chat) with executable
   checks, so step 1's gate is real rather than a vibe.
4. **Only if 1 fails:** evaluate a *trained* small MoE. Budget-check first —
   §3 and §4 imply this is a pretraining project, not an afternoon.

## 9. Limitations of this report

- The pilot ran at 0.8B-scale because that is what fits the free VRAM and
  because the 27B's Gated DeltaNet path cannot run on CPU here. The *structure*
  findings (uniform energy, partition-bound ceiling) are the kind that scale
  with width, but they were not re-measured at 27B.
- No real 27B activations were captured; the real-weight experiment uses
  synthetic inputs, so its oracle numbers probe *weight* structure only.
- The calibration corpus is 31.7 k characters of self-authored and repository
  code. It is reproducible and uncontaminated, but it is not a benchmark.
- Only CUDA was tested. No Vulkan/ROCm, no llama.cpp build, no `io_uring` work —
  the runtime-exploration items in the handoff's workstream B are *not* done.
- Memory-tier predictions are additive and single-stream; real runtimes overlap
  CPU and GPU work, which is why the model over-predicts some configurations by
  up to ~1.7x.


===== docs/prior_work.md =====

# Prior work: dense → MoE conversion, and what it says about E15

**Why this document exists.** E15 concluded that "the retrofit route is dead on its
own stated gate." That conclusion is **too strong**, and this is the literature
that shows why. The repo's own experiment was not a fair test of upcycling: it
trained for 614k tokens, used the one gating scheme the literature identifies as
wrong, and never implemented the output-rescaling fix that both the paper trail
and this repo's own design doc say is essential.

Everything below is cited. Read `docs/experiment_log.md` E15 first for what was
actually measured.

---

## 1. The result the handoff was remembered for: upcycling works, modestly

| Work | Method | Continued-pretrain budget | Outcome |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Sparse Upcycling** (Komatsuzaki et al., 2022, [2212.05055](https://arxiv.org/abs/2212.05055)) | Duplicate the dense FFN into E experts, add a router | **~50% of the original dense pretraining cost** | "significantly outperform their dense counterparts on SuperGLUE and ImageNet"; also beat from-scratch MoE given 100% of the dense pretraining budget |
| **LLaMA-MoE** (Zhu et al., EMNLP 2024, [2406.16554](https://arxiv.org/abs/2406.16554)) | **Partition** the FFN into E experts (this repo's method) + continual pretrain | **200B tokens**, 112 × A100-80G | LLaMA-MoE-3.5B (4/16): avg **57.7** vs Sheared-LLaMA-2.7B 56.4, OpenLLaMA-3B 55.6 |
| **NVIDIA upcycling** (He et al., 2024, [2410.07524](https://arxiv.org/abs/2410.07524)) | Virtual-group init + weight scaling | **1T tokens** | Nemotron-4 15B: MMLU **67.6%** upcycled vs **65.3%** continued dense on the *same* 1T tokens |
| **Drop-Upcycling** (Nakamura et al., ICLR 2025, [2502.19261](https://arxiv.org/abs/2502.19261)) | Upcycle + statistically re-initialise part of the weights | Hundreds of billions+ | 5.9B-active ≈ **13B dense** at ~1/4 the training FLOPs |

So "someone did it and it was kind of ok" is exactly right. The wins are real but
**modest**: LLaMA-MoE beats dense models of *similar active parameters*, not the
original 7B dense model it came from (their final loss is explicitly higher than
LLaMA-2-7B's). NVIDIA's headline is **+2.3 MMLU points** over continued dense
training. Sparse Upcycling's is a SuperGLUE gain for ~half a pretraining budget.
Nobody gets a free lunch; they get a real but bounded one, and they pay for it.

**The cost, in one line:** Sparse Upcycling puts it at **+10% to +60% of the cost
of training the original dense network**. For a 27B-class model that is 10⁴–10⁵
GPU-hours. LLaMA-MoE's own 3.5B-active run is ≈112 A100s × ~3 days ≈ **10⁴
A100-hours** (~$20k). Not a laptop job, but two orders of magnitude cheaper than
pretraining a new 27B — which is the actual argument for upcycling.

## 2. The cheap version exists, and it is closer to this repo than anything else

**CMoE — "Analytical FFN-to-MoE Restructuring via Activation Pattern Analysis"**
(Pei et al., Huawei + CUHK, [2502.04416](https://arxiv.org/abs/2502.04416)) is the
direct answer to "can this be done *without* hundreds of billions of tokens."
It restructures a dense FFN into a MoE using **only a small calibration set**,
with **no router training at all**:

- Neuron activation frequency is **bimodal** on LLaMA-2-7B: most neurons activate
  conditionally (rate ≈ 0.07), while a subset activates on **nearly every input**
  (rate → 1.0).
- The method puts the always-active neurons into an always-on **shared expert**
  and clusters the conditional ones into routed experts **by activation pattern**,
  not by weight similarity. The router is then built analytically from
  representative-neuron statistics.
- Result: **up to 1.17× speedup** with "minutes of processing and 2k-sample
  fine-tuning."

Their critique of prior work is a precise description of what this repo did:

> existing methods either rely on **weight-based clustering** that treats all
> neurons uniformly regardless of their activation behavior

and their prediction of the failure mode is exactly the symptom E15 recorded:

> high-frequency neurons may be scattered across different routed experts. This
> forces the router to activate most experts regardless of input, undermining the
> sparsity that MoE relies on for efficiency.

E15 measured `eff_experts = 33.2 of 64` with `load_max_over_mean` up to **9.25**.
That is not a mysterious training failure — it is the predicted consequence of
partitioning by weights while ignoring activation frequency.

## 3. What this repo did that the literature says is wrong

Four concrete mismatches, in order of how much they matter:

1. **The token budget.** E15 trained 300 steps × 4 × 512 = **614k tokens**.
   LLaMA-MoE states: *"models should be trained for at least 15–20B tokens to
   properly conclude"* which partition method is better — they used **200B**.
   E15 is **~30,000× below** even the "enough to form an opinion" floor. This is
   the single biggest problem: E15 is not a measurement of upcycling, it is a
   measurement of an *initialisation*.
2. **No output rescaling.** With `combine="sum"`, keeping k of E equal slices
   leaves ≈(1 − k/E) of the output. Both fixes in the literature target exactly
   this: LLaMA-MoE rescale expert outputs by N/k and report it *"provides
   significantly better performance"*; NVIDIA scale expert weights by
   ∛(E·G²/T) for **1.5% better loss**, and note that without it the naive
   fine-grained result *"did not converge to the original loss."* This repo's
   `docs/upcycling_design.md` §5 listed "scale expert init by E/k" as mitigation
   #1 and **never implemented it** in E14 or E15.
3. **The gating scheme.** E15 used `combine="sum"` (an unweighted partial sum).
   NVIDIA found **softmax-then-topK beats topK-then-softmax** consistently, and
   NLP MoE practice is a *weighted* sum with probabilities. The repo tested a
   configuration the literature says is the weaker one.
4. **Partitioning by weights, not activations.** Contiguous / interleaved /
   random / weight-k-means. Per CMoE, the informative axis is the activation
   pattern, with always-active neurons separated out. The repo's finding that
   `clustered` (weight k-means) degenerates into one giant expert is consistent
   with CMoE's point that weight-space clustering is the wrong tool — and E15
   then trained only on `plan=random`.

**One thing E15 got right, independently confirmed.** E15 noted that layer 23
behaves completely differently from layers 0/3/8 — participation ratio 0.185 vs
0.6–0.9, top-1 energy share 0.82, oracle residual 0.431 vs 0.68–0.85. LLaMA-MoE
found the same thing from the routing side:

> deep layers have more routing preferences than shallow layers … Based on this
> finding, **expert partition on the latter layers' FFNs may bring further
> improvements.**

Two independent pipelines, same conclusion: sparsity lives in the late layers.
That is now a supported lead rather than a hunch from one model.

## 4. What this does *not* change

- **The 9B-active arithmetic still holds.** The non-FFN backbone is 9,783,238,144
  params read every token. No amount of upcycling quality touches it, so
  "~27B total, ~9B active" remains unreachable *from this backbone*. Upcycling
  changes how good a converted model can be, not what active-parameter floor is
  reachable.
- **The cost is still 10⁴–10⁵ GPU-hours.** A free tier (≈30 GPU-hours/week, and
  T4s rather than A100s) is still ~50 years. Better recipes do not make it free.
- **MoE-isn't-automatically-faster-at-batch-1** was overstated (see §5).
- **The resident-quantisation path is unaffected** and remains the cheapest real
  win. It is not in competition with upcycling: one is a download, the other is
  a training campaign.

## 5. Correction: the batch-1 claim was too strong

E15's `.`/`real_ffn.json` microbenchmark showed top-2 of 32 timing the same as
all-32, and this was reported as "MoE is a datacenter-batch technique, not a
laptop-latency technique." That reading is wrong, and the measurement itself
explains why.

Dense reads 535 MB of layer-1 weights in 2.443 ms = **219 GB/s**, ≈89% of this
machine's measured 246.8 GB/s — so the dense case *is* bandwidth-bound. Top-2
should therefore read ~33 MB (≈0.15 ms) and instead took 5.93 ms. The gap is
~5.8 ms of **dispatch overhead from naive per-expert `index_select` + 32 small
GEMMs**, not a property of MoE. NVIDIA's own guidance says the opposite of what
was claimed: *"At batch size 1 decoding is bound by available memory rather than
compute, and this is where MoE performs well since it reads fewer weight bytes."*

So the honest statement is: **at batch 1 MoE should save bytes read, and this
repo's unoptimised dispatch destroyed that saving.** A real implementation
(grouped/fused GEMM) is required before any claim about MoE latency on this
hardware. The open question for the laptop is different and narrower — whether 64
scattered expert reads per token can be served under a 0.8–1.9 GB/s SSD without
destroying the win, which is a *locality* problem, not a FLOPs problem.

Related: **MoEfication** (Zhang et al., ACL Findings 2022,
[2110.01786](https://arxiv.org/abs/2110.01786)) "can conditionally use **10% to
30% of FFN parameters while maintaining over 95% original performance**", with a
**2x speedup at 25% of FFN parameters** — *without* retraining. But it works on
**ReLU** FFNs, where *"most inputs only activate a tiny ratio of neurons"*. LLaMA-MoE
note plainly that *"the Swish-based FFN does not bring natural sparsity as ReLU in
T5 and BERT."* CMoE's activation histograms show SwiGLU is not sparsity-free
either, just weaker — which is why the shared/routed split matters there and
weight-clustering does not work here. This is the sharpest available explanation
for why E15's oracle residual could not beat 0.86 at 12.5% active: a SwiGLU FFN
has far less to exploit than the ReLU models every post-hoc MoE success was built
on.

## 6. Revised verdict

**E15's conclusion should be read as: "this initialisation, this gating, and this
budget do not beat the frozen ceiling."** Not as "upcycling cannot work." The
literature says it can, at a price this project cannot pay, and the specific
failures E15 recorded have published, named causes.

The cheapest genuinely-untested experiment is **CMoE-style restructuring**: an
activation-pattern partition with a shared expert and an analytical router needs
only a calibration set and minutes of compute, and it can be run on the 0.8B model
*locally* with the machinery already in `src/moeme/ffn.py`. It targets the one
thing E15 left unexplained — the router collapse.

## 7. Sources

- Komatsuzaki et al., *Sparse Upcycling*, [arXiv:2212.05055](https://arxiv.org/abs/2212.05055)
- Zhu et al., *LLaMA-MoE*, EMNLP 2024, [arXiv:2406.16554](https://arxiv.org/abs/2406.16554)
- He et al., *Upcycling Large Language Models into Mixture of Experts*, [arXiv:2410.07524](https://arxiv.org/abs/2410.07524)
- Nakamura et al., *Drop-Upcycling*, ICLR 2025, [arXiv:2502.19261](https://arxiv.org/abs/2502.19261)
- Pei et al., *Analytical FFN-to-MoE Restructuring via Activation Pattern Analysis* (CMoE), [arXiv:2502.04416](https://arxiv.org/abs/2502.04416)
- Zhang et al., *MoEfication*, ACL Findings 2022, [arXiv:2110.01786](https://arxiv.org/abs/2110.01786)
- Liew et al., *Scaling Laws for Upcycling MoE Language Models*, ICML 2025
- NVIDIA, *Dense vs. MoE Models: Active Parameters, Throughput, and When to Choose Each* (developer blog, Sep 2026)

**Limitations of this review.** Not every source was read in full: LLaMA-MoE,
NVIDIA and CMoE were read as full HTML text (LLaMA-MoE and NVIDIA truncated at the
tail), while Sparse Upcycling, Drop-Upcycling, MoEfication, the upcycling scaling
laws and the NVIDIA developer blog were read at **abstract level only**, with the
"+10% to +60%" figure for Sparse Upcycling coming from a search snippet of the
paper body rather than text I read. Everything cited is a primary source or the
publisher's own abstract; nothing is a replication. The GPU-hour figures in §1 are
my arithmetic from the papers' stated token counts and GPU counts, not quotes. Web search was available for this
review (unlike the rest of the repository, which was built without it), so this is
the one document in `docs/` whose claims rest on secondary sources rather than
artefacts measured here.


===== docs/quality_vs_speed.md =====

# Does the fast MoE actually answer as well as the slow dense 27B?

**Short answer: on this task set, at any budget either model can finish, the two
are indistinguishable — and the MoE gets there in ~1/86th of the wall time. The
apparent gap at a small token cap was an artifact of the cap: it cut the dense
model off mid-solution and the harness then recorded the last number it saw as
its answer.** That defect is now fixed and reported, and the reasoning tasks were
re-run at a budget the dense model can actually finish.

**And at equal quantisation the architecture is not the compromise.** With both
models at Q4_K_M (§4), the sparse 22 GB MoE and the dense 17 GB model tie on
reasoning (4/4 each) and differ by one brittle task elsewhere (12/15 vs 13/15) —
while the sparse model decodes **~19× faster**. Sparsity costs nothing measurable
here; what it does not do is make a model fit, which is why the Q2_K MoE remains
the one to run on this machine.

**And surplus's placement is worth having — but not through surplus's own bench
or shim on this machine.** The plan its solver picks (`-ngl 42 -ncmoe 24`) is the
best placement tested, and driving the same 19 tasks through llama.cpp at that
placement reproduced the ollama run's score exactly (17/19) with ×1.75 the wall
speed. Its `bench` crashes, its `shim` returns empty responses, its auto-plan in
the shim is ~15× slower than its own best plan, and its default 4 GiB RAM cage is
smaller than this model needs. Details in §5.

This document follows the repo's rule: every number re-prints from `results/*.json`
via `tools/summarise_results.py`, using the task set and harness already in the
repo (`experiments/eval_gate.py`), not a new one invented for this question.

## 1. Method, and the changes that made it possible

The gate is the repo's existing task set: 30 tasks, scored mechanically, not by a
judge model.

| category | n | how it is checked |
|---|---:|---|
| coding | 10 | the generated function is **executed** against fixed cases in a subprocess |
| reasoning | 8 | the final number is extracted and compared (`ANSWER: <value>`) |
| format | 6 | output must parse as JSON and match a required schema |
| instruction | 6 | exact-match constraints (counts, casing, exact tokens, forbidden letter) |

Both models run identical prompts at `temperature 0`, a fixed seed, and the same
`num_predict`. Five changes were needed, and all are additive:

- `--think off` — suppresses the separate reasoning field for thinking models.
  Without it a qwen3.x model spends an unbounded number of tokens thinking, and
  the dense 27B cannot be evaluated in a sitting.
- `--tasks a,b,c` — run a fixed id subset, so a model decoding at 0.34 tok/s is
  compared with one at 47 tok/s on the *same* prompts.
- `--resume` plus a checkpoint after **every** task (the CPU-only control run
  documented in `docs/expert_offloading.md` was OOM-killed; losing hours of a
  slow run to one dropped connection is not acceptable, and `--out` is always a
  valid partial report).
- `--backend llama` / `--chat-template-kwargs` — run the identical task set
  through llama.cpp instead of the ollama tag, which is what turns "is the other
  engine better" into a measurement (§5).
- `--rescore` — replay corrected task definitions over *stored* responses, so a
  multi-hour run made under a wrong expectation is re-scored, not re-run.

## 2. The constraint that sets the experiment size

Measured decode on this machine, same runtime, same weights:

| model | file | decode | a 192-token answer costs |
|---|---:|---:|---:|
| `ornith-1.5-35b-a3b:q2_k` (35.5B MoE, Q2_K) | 13.0 GB | **36.7 tok/s** | 5 s |
| `qwen3.8:latest` (27.8B dense, Q4_K_M) | 17.0 GB | **0.34–0.39 tok/s** | **8–9 min** |

That ~100× gap decides what is measurable. The first attempt ran all 30 tasks on
both models. The MoE finished all 30 in **115 s**; the dense model finished 2 in
~25 minutes, projecting to ~6 hours. That run was stopped and its two recorded
tasks are kept as evidence in
`results/eval_qwen3.8-latest_full30_abandoned.json`.

So the comparison uses a **19-task subset** (3 coding, 4 reasoning, 6 format,
6 instruction), run identically on both models.

## 3. Results

### 3.1 MoE, full 30-task set (`num_predict=384`)

| model | overall | coding | format | instruction | reasoning |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| `ornith-1.5-35b-a3b:q2_k` | **26/30 = 86.7%** | 10/10 | 6/6 | 4/6 | 6/8 |

### 3.2 Both models, 19 tasks, `num_predict=192`

| model | overall | coding | format | instruction | reasoning | capped rows | excluding capped | wall |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| MoE `ornith-1.5-35b-a3b:q2_k` | **15/19 = 78.9%** | 3/3 | 6/6 | 4/6 | 2/4 | 2 | 15/17 = 88.2% | 55 s |
| dense `qwen3.8:latest` | 13/19 = 68.4% | 3/3 | 6/6 | 4/6 | 0/4 | **4** | 13/15 = 86.7% | 3958 s |

Two things make the headline numbers misleading on their own:

- **Every one of the dense model's four reasoning failures was a truncation.**
  `done_reason` was `length` on all four: it was cut off mid-solution while
  reasoning correctly. Its `r_lily` response ends "…Therefore", on its way to 47;
  the scorer then extracted a number from the working and recorded a wrong
  answer. The MoE, which writes more compactly, capped only twice.
- **The reason the extraction misfires is worth separating.** A capped run that
  never reaches its answer marker has no answer to score, so it stays a failure;
  rows now carry `truncated` and both scores are printed. That is what §3.3 fixes
  by re-running rather than re-framing.

So the only defensible reading of §3.2 is: on everything the cap did not cut,
**the two models are statistically indistinguishable** (MoE 15/17, dense 13/15,
same two instruction failures), and the reasoning column is unmeasured for one
of them.

### 3.3 The reasoning column, re-run at a budget both can finish (`num_predict=768`)

The four reasoning tasks only, identical prompts, identical cap:

| model | reasoning @768 | wall | decode (median) |
|---|---:|---:|---:|
| MoE `ornith-1.5-35b-a3b:q2_k` | **4/4** | 41 s | 43.4 tok/s |
| dense `qwen3.8:latest` | **4/4** | 3517 s | 0.339 tok/s |

Both models answered `0.05`, `3`, `47`, `42` — i.e. **the 192-cap figures were
entirely a harness budget**: the dense model's 0/4 and the MoE's 2/4 both came
from rows the cap cut off mid-working. At a budget both models can finish, there
is no quality gap to find on these tasks: 86× the wall time, same answers.

The MoE on the full 19 tasks at the same 768 cap scores **17/19 = 89.5%**
(coding 3/3, format 6/6, instruction 4/6, reasoning 4/4), the only failures being
two genuine instruction cases (`i_five_words` returned 6 words, `i_no_letter_e`
used a word containing "e"). Those two fail for the dense model at every cap too.

### 3.4 Two defects in the repo's own task set, and one in its scorer

Running the set is what exposed these; all three are fixed and pinned by tests.

1. **`r_bat_ball` expected 0.10.** The ball is `(1.10 - 1.00) / 2 = 0.05`, so the
   task marked the *correct* answer wrong. Both models answered 0.05. This is
   what made the MoE's `r_bat_ball` row a FAIL in the first run.
2. **`r_modular` expected 1.** `7**20 % 13 = 3`; the old expectation marked the
   *incorrect* answer (1) as a pass. The two defects mis-scored in opposite
   directions.
3. **Truncation was only labelled for *empty* answers.** A capped model that
   narrates before answering scored a bare "got 3.0, want 42", which reads as a
   wrong answer. This is the defect that mis-scored 3 of the dense model's 4
   reasoning rows in §3.2.

`test_numeric_expectations_are_arithmetically_correct` now re-derives every
numeric expectation with a closed form instead of trusting the literal, and
`test_every_coding_case_is_satisfiable_by_a_correct_solution` runs a reference
solution against every coding case — a wrong expected value fails a correct model,
so it must fail the suite first. `--rescore` applied the corrected definitions to
the already-completed runs (`r_bat_ball` 14/19 → 15/19 for the MoE) instead of
re-running them.

## 4. Equal quantisation: is the *architecture* a quality compromise? — no

§3 compared two artifacts a user would actually choose between, but they differ in
two ways at once:

| artifact | architecture | quantisation |
|---|---|---|
| `ornith-1.5-35b-a3b:q2_k` | MoE, 256 experts, top-8 | Q2_K (2.8 bpw) |
| `qwen3.8:latest` | dense | Q4_K_M (4.9 bpw) |

So a gap would be architecture **plus** ~1.7× in bits per weight. Holding the
quantisation fixed removes that, and the partner model was already on this
machine: **`ornith-1.5:35b` is the same `qwen35moe` architecture at Q4_K_M**
(22.0 GB), so against the dense 27B at Q4_K_M (17.0 GB) it isolates architecture
alone. Same runtime, same engine, same 19 tasks, `--think off`.

| run (all Q4_K_M, ~4.9 bpw) | coding | format | instruction | reasoning | overall | decode |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| **MoE** `ornith-1.5:35b` (22 GB) | 3/3 | 5/6 | 4/6 | **4/4** | 16/19 = 84.2% | **6.14 tok/s** |
| **dense** `qwen3.8:latest` (17 GB) | 3/3 | 6/6 | 4/6 | **4/4** | 13/15 non-reasoning + 4/4 reasoning | 0.32 tok/s |

The dense row is assembled from its two measured configurations: its 15
non-reasoning rows at a 192 cap (all completed — the cap did not bind, so they are
comparable to the MoE's) and its 4 reasoning rows at 768 (0/4 at 192 was pure
truncation, §3.3).

**Verdict: sparsity costs nothing measurable here and buys ~19×.** Reasoning ties
at 4/4, and the 15 non-reasoning tasks differ by **one task** (12/15 vs 13/15).
That one task is not a signal: across all three measured models the failures move
around inside the same small set of brittle exact-match constraints —
`i_no_letter_e` fails everywhere, and `i_five_words` / `i_lowercase_start` /
`f_json_array` each fail for one model and pass for another. At six tasks per
category and one run each, ±1 task is noise, not capability.

Two consequences worth separating:

- **The architecture question is answered**: a pretrained sparse model is not the
  compromise option against a dense one at the same bits. Its 4/4 on reasoning is
  the same 4/4, not a deficit.
- **The practical recommendation does not change, and the reason is memory, not
  quality.** At equal bits the 22 GB MoE is 7× *slower* than the 13 GB MoE
  (6.14 vs 43.1 tok/s) because it does not fit the fast tiers either — it just
  reads far fewer bytes per token than the dense model does. On this machine the
  13 GB Q2_K MoE is still the one to run: it matches the dense 27B at 4.9 bpw on
  this task set while fitting, which is why it is 43 tok/s and not 6.

The residual caveat that this section does **not** remove is Q2_K itself: on work
outside this 19-task set, 2.8 bpw is the risk, and the equal-quant run is evidence
that if you have the RAM for Q4_K_M you should use it. The next subsection measures
how big that risk actually is, because a pass/fail gate cannot.

### 4.1 Does a smaller quantisation make the model dumber? — yes, about 10%, and the gate cannot see it

"I prefer Q4" is not the question §4 answers. §4 shows the **architecture** is not
the compromise; it says nothing about whether the *quantisation* is. Those are two
different knobs — the equal-quant run holds bits fixed and moves the architecture,
and the comparison a user actually faces ("13 GB or 22 GB of the same thing?") holds
the architecture fixed and moves the bits.

The gate's answer to that second question is a tie, and the tie is genuine but
uninformative. Scoring the same `qwen35moe` 35B at Q2_K and at Q4_K_M on the
identical 19 tasks at an identical 768 cap, same runtime, `--think off`, with
**nothing capped on either side**:

| 35B MoE, same 19 tasks, 768 cap | **Q2_K** (13 GB) | Q4_K_M (22 GB) |
|---|---:|---:|
| overall | **17/19** = 89.5% | 16/19 = 84.2% |
| coding / format / instruction / reasoning | 3/3, 6/6, 4/6, 4/4 | 3/3, 5/6, 4/6, 4/4 |
| wall, all 19 tasks | **62.7 s** | 463.5 s |
| decode | 43.1 tok/s | 6.14 tok/s |

Q4_K_M scores one task *worse*, which is inside the ±1-task noise band §4 already
established. But these two runs are not the same model giving the same answers.
Comparing them token by token, **13 of 19 responses are not the same text at all** —
only 6 are byte-identical, the median divergence is at the *first* character, and 9
of the 13 part company within 5 characters. Three tasks flip verdict, two of them in
Q2_K's favour. Q2_K does not reproduce Q4_K_M and then get graded on it; it produces
different text that happens to pass the same checks. At six tasks per category, a
gate made of exact-match assertions on easy prompts is a statement about the
assertions.

**Perplexity is the instrument that can see the difference.**
`experiments/quant_perplexity.py` builds one fixed corpus from this repo's own prose
and code, records its sha256, runs `llama-perplexity` once per quantisation with
every other flag held fixed, and stores each run's final estimate, its cumulative
series, its command line, its wall time and its guard verdict next to it. On a
matched pair — same model, same tokens, `-c 512 --chunks 8`:

| matched pair, identical token stream | PPL | vs Q4_K_M |
|---|---:|---:|
| `ornith-1.5:9b` Q4_K_M | 7.0029 ± 0.409 | — |
| `ornith-1.5:9b` Q2_K | 7.7128 ± 0.468 | **+0.71 PPL (+10.1%)** |

So the answer to "does Q2 make the model stupid?" is **no — but it is not free.** It
costs about **10% in next-token log-likelihood** on the text this machine is actually
pointed at, and that cost is completely invisible to the 19-task gate. Ten percent in
log-likelihood is the kind of gap that shows up on work harder than the gate's
prompts, which is exactly the residual risk flagged above. It is a real degradation,
and it is not a cliff.

A caveat that comes with doing this honestly: the corpus is repo-local, so it moves
as the repo does — two perplexity runs are only comparable when their recorded
**sha256 matches**. Every report this probe writes carries it, alongside the corpus
byte count and the source-file list, precisely because a PPL pair without the corpus
identity is not a measurement.

The 35B MoE's *own* matched pair cannot be measured on this host: the 21 GB Q4_K_M
artifact needs a partial expert offload even to load under 15 GiB, and the eval-style
batched pass over an 8-of-256 MoE touches nearly every expert, so it does not fit
that way either (below). The 9B pair is the same probe, same corpus builder, same
flags — it answers the quantisation question on a pair that fits.

**The practical reading, which is what changed.**

- **On this machine the 13 GB Q2_K MoE is still the one to run — now for two
  independent reasons.** It fits *and* the 22 GB Q4_K_M costs **7.4× the wall time**
  (463.5 s vs 62.7 s for the same 19 tasks) without scoring better. The 10% PPL gain
  is real, and it is not worth 7× here.
- **"I prefer Q4" is a defensible position, and this repo should stop implying
  otherwise.** Q4_K_M is the *closer* model; the perplexity pair is the measurement
  that says so. If you have the RAM, use it. The recommendation for Q2_K is a
  **memory** recommendation, not a claim that the two quants are equivalent.
- **When the gate says "no measurable difference", read it as "this gate cannot
  measure it".** The token-by-token comparison makes the point concrete: 13 of 19
  outputs differ while the score moves by one task in the *opposite* direction to the
  perplexity. Use the gate for capability and the probe for fidelity.

## 5. Can surplus do better?

Surplus (the local Rust engine in `~/Documents/surplus`) is three things: a
planner/solver that decides GGUF placement and drives llama.cpp, an
Ollama-compatible shim, and an experimental in-process native decoder. They get
different answers, and the honest summary is that **its placement is better and
its wrapper is worse — on this machine, right now.**

### 5.1 Its plan is measurably better than the runtime default

For `Ornith-1.5-35B-A3B-Q2_K.gguf` surplus's planner chose
`-ngl 42 --n-cpu-moe 24`, expert-granular rather than layer-granular, which is the
"hot modules in VRAM, cold ones in RAM" split the runtime does not choose by
itself. Measured with `llama-bench`, same weights, everything else fixed:

| placement | prompt tok/s | decode tok/s |
|---|---:|---:|
| `-ngl 42 -ncmoe 24` (surplus's pick) | **121.5** | **50.1** |
| `-ngl 99 -ncmoe 48` | 80.1 | 36.5 |
| `-ngl 99 -ncmoe 56` | 49.7 | 33.1 |
| ollama default (`num_gpu = -1`), 32-token **cold** baseline | 11.6 | 19.4 |

The prediction behind it is a ranking aid, not a forecast: 61.52 predicted
against 50.1 measured is **23% optimistic**, 57.17 (14% optimistic) at the
planner's default 2048 context.

**Read that last row with care — §5.2 shows it is a cold-start number, not what a
session gets.** `llama-bench` is a fresh process per placement, and ollama's
11.6/19.4 comes from a 32-token single-prompt benchmark. Against a warm session on
the same tasks, ollama's own rates are 83.4 tok/s prompt and 43.1 tok/s decode,
which puts this placement at ×1.21 and ×1.12 rather than ×10 and ×2.6. The
placement is still the better one; the multiples in this table are not the ones a
user experiences.

### 5.2 The same placement, scoring the same tasks — quality is unchanged, speed is real

`llama-bench` generates text but never checks it, so §5.1 says nothing about
quality. Re-running the identical 19 tasks through llama.cpp at
`-ngl 42 -ncmoe 24` (`make eval-llama`, f16 KV):

| run | overall | coding | format | instruction | reasoning | decode (median) | prompt (median) | wall |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| ollama tag, 768 | **17/19 = 89.5%** | 3/3 | 6/6 | 4/6 | 4/4 | 43.1 tok/s | 83.4 tok/s | 63 s |
| llama.cpp @ `-ngl 42 -ncmoe 24`, 768 | **17/19 = 89.5%** | 3/3 | 6/6 | 4/6 | 4/4 | **48.4 tok/s** | **101.2 tok/s** | **36 s** |

Identical score, identical failures, on the same weights. That is the expected
result, and it is worth stating plainly: **an engine or placement changes where
bytes come from, never which weights the model needs.** What it buys here is
speed: ×1.12 decode, ×1.21 prompt processing, and ×1.75 wall time for the same 19
tasks.

Peak VRAM at this placement was **6292 MiB** of 8188 (against 6654 MiB for the
ollama tag's own split), load 16.0 s, peak server RSS 8.79 GiB.

**This section corrects the draft it replaces.** Two earlier claims do not
survive a same-conditions measurement, and both were artefacts of the baseline
rather than of the placement:

- *"×2.59 ollama's decode"* compared 50.1 tok/s (a fresh `llama-bench` process)
  against ollama's 19.36 tok/s **32-token cold** figure. Warm, on the same tasks,
  ollama decodes at 43.1 tok/s and the gain is **×1.12**.
- *"×10.4 on prompt processing"* compared 121.5 tok/s against ollama's 11.6 cold
  figure. Warm, on the same tasks, ollama prompts at 83.4 tok/s and the gain is
  **×1.21**.

So the placement's real, reproducible advantage on this machine is ~10-20%, plus
a ×1.75 wall-time difference on this task set. That is worth adopting — and it is
an order of magnitude smaller than the cold-baseline comparison suggested. Any
"×N faster" claim in this repo should name the baseline it beat.

### 5.3 An engine comparison must match the template, not just the weights

Before that, the same llama.cpp run at the same placement scored **13/19**. The
cause was not the engine: `/completion` is a raw continuation and applies no chat
template, so the model wrote its `<think>` block into the answer and a
strict-instruction task received `\n\n<think>\nThe user wants me to reply with…`
instead of `CONFIRMED`. Instruction tasks went 4/6 → 1/6.

Ollama's `think: false` suppresses that; the equivalent here is
`chat_template_kwargs={"enable_thinking": false}` on `/v1/chat/completions`, which
is what the harness now sends. The uncorrected run is kept as evidence in
`results/eval_llama_ngl42_ncmoe24_CHAT_TEMPLATE_THINKING_ON.json` (its model
label says so, so no head-to-head table can quote it as engine quality). **That is a 21-point
score difference produced by a request setting rather than by the model** — the
same class of error as the truncation defect in §3.4, and the reason the harness
now reports which engine, template kwargs and cap produced a number. Finding it
took a direct probe (all four request variants on `/completion` still emitted
`<think>` on this build; the chat route with `enable_thinking: false` returned a
bare `yes`).

### 5.4 Its measurement path cannot confirm any of that

`surplus bench <model>` **crashes before it measures anything**:

```
surplus: [backend_error] llama-bench failed (signal: 11 (SIGSEGV) (core dumped))
```

Isolated by calling the binaries directly: the engine surplus pins for this model
(`llama.cpp-moe-cache`) segfaults whenever `-ncmoe` is passed to *llama-bench*
(exit 139 at `-ncmoe 24`, OOM-killed at `-ncmoe 40`), while the same placement on
the upstream build runs to completion and produces the 50.1 tok/s row. Two more
findings from the same probe:

- `--load-mode` is not a parameter of the pinned fork's `llama-bench`, so
  surplus's `engine::supports()` probe is the only reason that flag is absent from
  the failing command line.
- The fork's *server* does accept `-ncmoe` (it is `llama-bench` in that fork that
  crashes), so the pin is not fatal for serving — only for tuning.

This is the actionable result for surplus: `surplus optimize` cannot tune MoE
placements while `bench` dies on the engine it selects, so its own caveat that
"predicted rank is not a measured speed guarantee" currently has no way to be
discharged for MoE. The plan was confirmed by running `llama-bench` upstream by
hand instead.

### 5.5 Its shim serves the model but returns empty answers

`surplus shim` is the one interface that could be compared without translation,
because it speaks Ollama's own `/api/*` routes on port 11435. Started against this
artifact (pinned to the upstream checkout, which is required — see below):

```
surplus: memory cage 4G — the server cannot exceed it; the desktop stays safe
surplus: backend: …/llama.cpp-upstream/build/bin/llama-server … -ngl 42 -c 8192
         --n-cpu-moe 36 --cache-type-k q8_0 --cache-type-v q8_0 …
```

Three separate problems, each measured:

1. **Every response is empty.** 13 tasks ran; all 13 came back with
   `done_reason: stop` and `response: ""`. A direct probe of `/api/generate`
   confirms it is not the harness: HTTP 200, `eval_count: 32`, `response: ""`.
   The server had generated 227 tokens for one task. A client that trusts
   Ollama's API gets nothing back.
2. **The plan it picks unprompted is ~15× slower than its own best plan.** The
   shim chose `-n-cpu-moe 36` (from the stored per-model config, at ctx 8192) and
   decoded at **3.41 tok/s**, against 51 tok/s for the `-ncmoe 24` plan its solver
   ranks first and 46.6 tok/s for ollama. Pushing more expert layers to the CPU
   than the machine needs is the difference between usable and not.
3. **The default RAM cage is far below what this model needs.** The cage budget is
   computed from *active* parameters (~3B × 0.37 B/param ≈ 1.1 GiB) plus KV and
   slack → **4 GiB**. With `--n-cpu-moe 36`, the expert tensors of 36 layers are
   host-side; that is closer to 7 GB of touchable data, and the first shim run had
   its backend killed mid-run (HTTP 500 Bad Gateway) under the cage. Restricting
   the working set of a 13 GB MoE to 4 GiB is not a safety margin, it is a
   throughput cap and a failure mode.

One interface note worth recording because it fails silently: `--engine-dir` is a
**checkout root** (surplus resolves `<root>/build/bin/llama-server`). Passing the
`build` directory logs `pinned engine … has no llama-server — ignoring the pin` and
falls back to the fork, which is how the first attempt ended up on the crashing
engine without saying so in the result.

The empty-response run is quarantined in
`logs/surplus_shim_empty_responses.json`, outside `results/`, so no summary can
read it as a score.

### 5.6 The native decoder cannot run this model at all, by design

`surplus native` supports standard `qwen3moe` and **explicitly rejects**
`qwen35moe` — every model in this repo — naming the missing pieces (gated
delta-net linear attention, shared expert, sections RoPE). Its own
`docs/QWEN35_NATIVE.md` scopes that port as multi-day work and states the expected
outcome for a 17 GB model on the scalar CPU path: seconds per token. For the
standard-`qwen3moe` case it *can* run, `docs/NATIVE_ENGINE.md` records ~26 s vs
~4 s against llama.cpp CUDA. So the native path is a correctness and ownership
project, not a speed play, and it is not a candidate here.

### 5.7 What "better" can and cannot mean

An engine changes **where bytes come from and how fast they arrive**. It does not
change **which** weights the model needs, so it cannot change the quality of a
given checkpoint — and §5.2 now *measures* that rather than assuming it: two
different engines, two placements, same 17/19 and the same two failures.

Two caveats keep that from being a purity claim:

- Surplus's plan uses `q8_0` KV (its own README calls this "a precision tradeoff,
  not evidence of unchanged output quality"), so a surplus run is not
  output-identical to an f16-KV run. The measured cross-engine agreement in §5.2
  is at f16 KV, driven through llama.cpp directly.
- Different kernels also differ in floating-point summation order, so token-exact
  agreement across engines is not expected in general.

Practically: surplus's contribution to this question today is its **placement** —
adopt `-ngl 42 --n-cpu-moe 24` in llama.cpp terms. Its planner is worth using;
its bench cannot verify the plan, and its shim cannot yet be scored.

## 6. Limitations

- **One prompt set, 19 tasks, one run each.** The checks are mechanical, but 6
  instruction tasks cannot support a percentage with much confidence, and a single
  run of a 4-task reasoning category cannot separate a real gap from sampling
  noise. Read the categories, not the headline.
- **Thinking is disabled** (`--think off`) for both models, because the dense
  model's thinking budget makes it unmeasurable otherwise. This measures
  non-reasoning output quality, not the models' best achievable answers.
- **The dense 27B comparison is the expensive half of this document.** Its
  4-task run at 768 took **59 minutes**; the full 30-task version was abandoned
  after ~25 minutes for 2 tasks. Every dense number here is the largest one that
  fits a sitting, not the one that would be ideal.
- **Memory safety was sampled, not assumed.** Over 59 minutes of dense
  generation, RAM available never fell below **5.79 GiB** and peak VRAM was
  **6811 of 8188 MiB** (181 samples, `logs/mem_watch_dense768.log`). The 22 GB
  Q4_K_M MoE run — the case that does not fit — held a **5.32 GiB** RAM floor over
  92 samples with a **6934 MiB** VRAM peak, under an armed watchdog
  (`tools/mem_watchdog.py`, floor 2.0 GiB) that never had to fire; this host had
  **no free swap** throughout. The llama.cpp runs go through a preflight that
  refuses to start unless 6 GiB RAM and 6000 MiB VRAM are free — it refused
  correctly once, while the dense model held the card.
- **Every cell is one run.** The equal-quant comparison in §4 differs by a single
  task on the non-reasoning half, and the failures there move around inside the
  same handful of exact-match constraints between models — so treat a one-task gap
  in a six-task category as noise. Two runs per model would turn that from an
  inference into a measurement.
- **§4's two sides ran at different caps for the non-reasoning tasks** (768 for
  the MoE, 192 for the dense model). The cap did not bind for either — all 15 rows
  finished on their own in both runs — so the comparison holds, but it is not the
  same configuration string.
- **The Q4_K_M MoE does not fit either** (22 GB against 14.3 GB of fast tiers).
  Its 6.14 tok/s comes from reading 8 of 256 experts per token, not from
  residency, which is the clearest evidence in this repo that sparsity and
  residency are separate levers.
- **Q2_K at 2.8 bpw is aggressive.** This measures one checkpoint at one
  quantisation; it says nothing about `qwen35moe` at Q4_K_M or higher, which is
  the more relevant question for anyone with more memory.
- **The placement sweep is three points plus the runtime default**, measured with
  `llama-bench` (`-p 128 -n 64 -r 2`, threads fixed, `q8_0` KV) rather than
  through a chat runtime, so its prompt numbers are prompt-processing throughput,
  not time-to-first-token in an interactive session.
- **The engine comparison covers one model, one placement and one engine pair.**
  llama.cpp at `-ngl 42 -ncmoe 24` matched ollama on this set; that is not a claim
  about other placements, other models, or about any engine's output being
  token-identical.
- **surplus was not fixable from here.** Its bench, its shim and its cage are its
  own code; this document records what they do on this host, not how to repair
  them. The shim failure in particular (empty `response`, exit 200) is a defect on
  surplus's side to confirm with its maintainer.

Raw evidence: `results/eval_ornith-1.5-35b-a3b-q2_k.json`,
`results/eval_ornith-1.5-35b-a3b-q2_k_short19.json`,
`results/eval_ornith-1.5-35b-a3b-q2_k_short19_768.json`,
`results/eval_ornith-1.5-35b-a3b-q2_k_reason768.json`,
`results/eval_qwen3.8-latest_short19.json`,
`results/eval_qwen3.8-latest_reason768.json`,
`results/eval_qwen3.8-latest_full30_abandoned.json`,
`results/eval_llama_ngl42_ncmoe24.json`,
`results/eval_llama_ngl42_ncmoe24_CHAT_TEMPLATE_THINKING_ON.json`,
`results/eval_ornith-1.5-35b_short19_768.json` (the equal-quantisation run),
`results/moe_placement.json`, `logs/mem_watch_dense768.log`,
`logs/mem_watch_q4_35b.log`, `logs/surplus_shim_empty_responses.json`, and the
`logs/surplus_*.log` files.


===== docs/source_validation.md =====

# Source Validation Notes

**Method.** Everything below was verified against *primary* artefacts: the
Hugging Face model API, official `config.json` files, the official model cards,
and the safetensors weight headers read directly over HTTP range requests
(`tools/verify_model_geometry.py`). The web-search tool was unavailable for the
whole session, so **no secondary sources were used** — which is the stronger
path anyway, since parameter counts read from the real weight headers cannot be
wrong in the way a blog post can.

Every number here is reproducible: `make verify-sources`.

---

## 1. Nomenclature: what actually exists

The handoff referred to "Qwen3.8-27B", "Qwen3.8-Next", and
"DeepSeek V4.1 Flash/Pro" as things to verify rather than assume. They were all
checked. **All three exist**, but one of them is not what the handoff assumed.

| Name | Status | Evidence |
|---|---|---|
| `Qwen/Qwen3.8-27B` | **Real**, official Qwen org, Apache-2.0 | HF API: 6.77M downloads; `config.json`; model card |
| `Qwen/Qwen3.8-Flash-Next` | **Real**, official Qwen org | HF API; `config.json` — **180.0B params, not 27B** |
| `deepseek-ai/DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash` | **Real**, official DeepSeek org | HF API: 1.36M downloads |
| `Qwen/Qwen3-Next-80B-A3B-*` | Real, prior generation | HF API |
| "Qwen3.8-Next" as a ~27B model | **Not verified — no such artefact found** | `Qwen3.8-Flash-Next` is 180B |

### The two framing corrections that matter

**(a) Qwen3.8-27B is DENSE, not a Mixture-of-Experts.** Its `config.json` has no
`num_experts` / `num_experts_per_tok` fields anywhere. All 27.78B parameters are
active on every token. There is no "9B active" variant to discover or extract —
the requested 9B-active property would have to be *created*.

**(b) The family's MoE model is 180B, not 27B.** Verified from its weight
headers: `Qwen/Qwen3.8-Flash-Next` = **179,999,981,459 params**, of which
121.4B is MoE expert FFN and 51.2B is an n-gram memory embedding (20M n-gram
vocab x 2560). It cannot run on 8 GB VRAM in any quantisation.

This means the handoff's target — "~27B total, ~9B active, near-Qwen3.8-27B
quality" — describes a model that **does not exist** in this family. It would
have to be trained.

---

## 2. Exact geometry of Qwen3.8-27B (from the weight headers)

Read from the 1199 safetensors tensor headers, not estimated.

| Component | Parameters | Share |
|---|---:|---:|
| **FFN** (gate + up + down; 17408 intermediate) | **17,112,760,320** | 61.60% |
| Gated DeltaNet block (linear attention) | 5,562,044,928 | 20.02% |
| Full attention block | 1,677,721,600 | 6.04% |
| Token embedding (248,320 x 5120, untied) | 1,271,398,400 | 4.58% |
| LM head (untied) | 1,271,398,400 | 4.58% |
| Vision tower (27 blocks, hidden 1152) | 460,730,096 | 1.66% |
| MTP head | 424,699,392 | 1.53% |
| RMSNorms | 674,816 | 0.002% |
| **Total** | **27,781,427,952** | 100% |

- **Text tower alone: 26,895,998,464** (total minus vision tower and MTP head).
- Per-layer FFN is uniform: **267,386,880** in every one of the 64 layers.
- `hidden_size` 5120, `intermediate_size` 17408, 64 layers, vocab 248,320.
- 48 Gated DeltaNet layers + 16 gated full-attention layers, in a strict
  3:1 repeating pattern (`full_attention_interval` = 4).
- `q_proj` is **fused query+gate** (5120 -> 12288); `k_proj`/`v_proj` are
  4 KV heads x 256.
- **Native MTP is present in the weights** (`mtp.*`: `fc`, one full-attention
  transformer layer, `pre_fc_norm_embedding`, `pre_fc_norm_hidden`, 424.7M
  params). The model card states it was "trained with multiple steps".
- Context 262,144 native, extensible to 1,000,000. License Apache-2.0.
- Weights are BF16, 55,562,855,904 bytes.

## 3. Verification of the previous chat's parameter split

The handoff contained second-hand claims of "~17.1B FFN params and ~9.8B other
params" that it asked to be independently verified. **Both are correct**, and
the scope is now pinned down exactly:

| Claim | Verified value | Verdict |
|---|---:|---|
