| FFN params ~17.1B | 17,112,760,320 | **Correct** |
| "Other" params ~9.8B | 9,783,238,144 | **Correct**, where "other" = text tower − FFN − vision tower − MTP head |

The "other" 9.783B decomposes as 5.562B Gated DeltaNet + 1.678B attention +
1.271B embedding + 1.271B LM head + norms. This decomposition is the basis of
the central feasibility finding in `docs/feasibility_report.md`.

## 4. Local artefact check

The machine already holds `qwen3.8:latest` in ollama. Interrogating it confirms
the upstream facts independently: `parameters 27.3B`, `quantization Q4_K_M`,
`context length 262144`, `architecture qwen35`, `embedding length 5120`,
capabilities `completion / vision / tools / thinking`, Apache-2.0, 17 GB on
disk. 17.0 GB / 27.78B params = **4.9 bits per parameter**, which is the figure
used throughout the throughput modelling.

## 5. Environment facts discovered while working

- **`causal_conv1d` is CUDA-only here** (`Expected x.is_cuda() to be true`).
  The qwen3_5 Gated DeltaNet path therefore **cannot run on CPU** through
  `transformers` in this environment. This blocks CPU-only experimentation with
  the whole Qwen3.5/3.8 architecture family and is worth fixing before any
  CPU-offload design work.
- The installed stack is unusually complete: torch 2.11.0+cu13, transformers
  5.6.2, flash-attn 3, causal_conv1d, safetensors 0.7, docker, nvcc, ollama.
- Docker is present, so llama.cpp/GGUF experiments can be run in a container
  without touching the host toolchain.

## 6. What could NOT be verified

Stated plainly rather than filled in with plausible-sounding numbers.

1. **Free-tier GPU limits are only partially verified.** The Colab free tier's
   12-hour maximum runtime was confirmed from Google's own FAQ page. The Kaggle
   and Lightning AI pages returned JavaScript application shells with no
   readable quota text, so **their specific GPU models, weekly hour caps and
   verification requirements remain unconfirmed**. No claim about being able to
   fine-tune a 27B model on free tiers is made anywhere in this project — see
   `docs/decision_memo.md` for the arithmetic showing why that is not close.
2. **The NVMe anomaly is unexplained.** The drive is a Micron 3400 1TB (PCIe
   Gen4, datasheet sequential read ~6.6 GB/s) but measures **0.8–1.9 GB/s**
   across three independent methods. The cause was not established; the
   candidate explanations are recorded in `configs/tiers.json`. Checking the
   PCIe link width and drive health is a cheap, high-value follow-up, because a
   healthy drive would roughly quadruple the streaming budget.
3. **No benchmark-suite or contamination analysis was performed.** The pilot
   study measures routing structure, not task quality; its calibration corpus is
   small and self-authored. Scope is stated in each script's docstring.


===== docs/upcycling_design.md =====

# Design: turning Qwen3.8-27B into a ~9B-active MoE

**Status:** design, and its gate has been run and **failed**. The PoC was executed
on the real Qwen3.5-0.8B model — E14 locally on 4 layers, E15 on all 24 layers at
E=8/32/64 on free Kaggle T4s. Training beat the frozen ceiling only at the mildest
ratio (E=8, 12.5% active, by 0.006) and **lost at the ratios this design needs**,
E=32 and E=64, where the residual rose instead of falling (§9).
**Recommendation: do not build this.** Cost is deliberately ignored below (per the
brief).

This document exists because the earlier feasibility work answered a narrower
question than it looked like it did. It showed that **post-hoc** FFN
sparsification has no usable operating point -- but that was measured with *no
weight updates*, so it bounds the frozen partition, not the trained one. This is
the plan for the trained version, and the honest arithmetic of what it can reach.

---

## 1. What "9B active" can and cannot mean

Verified geometry of the Qwen3.8-27B text tower (`src/moeme/accounting.py`,
checked against the real safetensors headers):

| Component | Params | Active every token? |
|---|---:|---|
| FFN (64 layers, swiGLU) | 17,112,760,320 | only if dense |
| Gated DeltaNet (48 layers) | 5,562,044,928 | **yes** |
| Full attention (16 layers) | 1,677,721,600 | **yes** |
| LM head | 1,271,398,400 | **yes** |
| Embedding | 1,271,398,400 | a gather, not a matmul |
| Norms | 674,816 | **yes** |
| **backbone total** | **9,783,238,144** | |

Two accounting conventions give two different answers, and the design has to
pick one out loud:

* **Strict (the embedding table counts as active).** The floor is **9.78B**.
  Routing the FFN to *zero* experts cannot get under it. The goal as originally
  stated is unreachable by any FFN-only change -- this is arithmetic, not a
  modelling difficulty.
* **Compute-bearing (the embedding gather is excluded).** The floor is
  **8,511,839,744**, leaving **0.49B** for active FFN. Reachable.

Under the second convention the target needs `E/k ≈ 35`:

| Config | Active FFN | Total active | |
|---|---:|---:|---|
| 32 experts, top-1 | 0.535B | 9.04B | over |
| **64 experts, top-1** | **0.267B** | **8.78B** | reachable |
| **128 experts, top-2** | **0.267B** | **8.78B** | reachable |
| 64 experts, top-2 | 0.535B | 9.04B | over |

Note that the model **stays ~27B stored**. This shrinks FLOPs per token; it does
not shrink the footprint. On the target laptop, where the measured bottleneck is
*storage* (0.561 tok/s streaming from an NVMe that delivers 0.8-1.9 GB/s), that
is the crux: see §7.

## 2. Why partitioning, not replication

Standard sparse upcycling (Komatsuzaki et al., 2022) replicates the dense FFN
into E full-width copies, which multiplies *total* parameters by roughly E.
Mixtral-style models are built that way.

That is the wrong primitive here, for two reasons:

1. It does not hold total parameters constant, so it does not answer a "27B
   total / 9B active" question at all.
2. The repository already has a **partition** implementation that is proven
   *exactly* dense-equivalent when all experts are active
   (`src/moeme/ffn.py`, worst relative error 5.1e-07 on real 27B weights).

So this design partitions the intermediate axis: expert `e` owns a disjoint
slice of the 17,408 intermediate units. Total parameters are unchanged, and
`top_k = E` with `combine="sum"` **is** the dense layer -- the dense model is a
strict special case, which is what makes the PoC's dense-limit check meaningful
rather than decorative.

## 3. Expert configuration

**Recommendation: few large experts, top-1** (64 experts, ~272 intermediate
units each), not many small ones with top-2. On this hardware at batch 1 you are
memory-bandwidth-bound, and top-1 gives the smallest per-token read with the
least routing overhead. `128×2` equals it on active params but costs more
dispatch and more small GEMMs; `src/moeme/ffn.py` measured a 2.1-3.9× small-GEMM
penalty at batch 1 for exactly this reason (two runs; the ratio is noisy). Note
that top-k recovers none of it -- top-2 of 32 timed the same as all-32.

Concretely, per configuration to run:

| | experts | top-k | active FFN | total active |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| primary | 64 | 1 | 0.267B | 8.78B |
| fallback | 128 | 2 | 0.267B | 8.78B |
| control | 32 | 1 | 0.535B | 9.04B |

## 4. Router initialisation

A fresh linear router with default init routes near-uniformly, which wastes the
first part of the run. Two concrete steps, in order of value:

1. **Warm-start the router from the frozen model.** The intermediate units of a
   trained dense FFN already have different output norms, and
   `ffn.train_linear_router` can be fit to the *oracle* top-1 labels (the
   largest-norm expert per token) in a few hundred steps. This costs almost
   nothing and starts the model from "route where the signal already is"
   instead of from noise. `ffn.oracle_topk_residual` supplies the labels.
2. **Small-init router plus a load-balancing loss.** Auxiliary loss with
   coefficient ~1e-2 (Switch-style) and a router z-loss ~1e-3, as implemented in
   `src/moeme/upcycle.py`. Watch `effective_experts`; if it collapses toward 1,
   raise the coefficient.

## 5. The output-scale problem, stated plainly

A **partition with a sparse top-k is a partial sum**: with `combine="sum"`, k of
E slices sum to roughly `k/E` of the dense layer's output. So the sparse layer
starts with a systematic magnitude deficit that training has to absorb, and one
place it can go wrong is experts scaling up so far that the layer saturates.

This is a real design risk, not a footnote. Three mitigations, in the order I
would try them:

1. **Scale expert init by `E/k`**, so the sparse sum starts at the right
   magnitude. Cheap to test; may be unstable for large E/k.
2. **Let the router carry a learned gate**, `combine="softmax"` -- but note this
   *forfeits* the exact dense limit at `top_k=E` (softmax weights sum to 1, so a
   full-k combination is a weighted mean, not the sum). `tests/test_upcycle.py`
   pins this difference deliberately.
3. **An explicit layer scale** (a per-layer multiplier on the MoE output),
   which is the most flexible and the least principled.

The PoC reports `vs_original_dense`, which can exceed 1.0 precisely because of
this effect -- that is a signal, not a bug.

## 6. Continued-pretraining recipe

Upcycling does not work as a pure architecture change; the experts must be
trained. The pilot's most damning result -- the trained dense FFN has **no
latent expert structure** (`participation_ratio ≈ E`; top-3 experts hold 91% of
the energy) -- is exactly why: there is nothing to *extract*, so the structure
has to be *created*.

* **Objective:** next-token cross-entropy + aux loss + z-loss. The router needs
  its own, higher learning rate (10× the expert LR is a reasonable start).
* **Data:** the model's original pretraining mixture if available, otherwise a
  general corpus (FineWeb-Edu / DCLM class). Sequence length 4096-8192.
* **Schedule:** warmup then cosine decay; watch the load-balance statistics for
  the first few thousand steps before trusting the run.
* **On the pilot's caveat:** the pilot's activations were a small,
  non-benchmark corpus. That limitation carries over here -- a real run needs a
  real corpus and a real evaluation set, which is what
  `experiments/eval_gate.py` exists to provide.

## 7. Checkpointing, sharding, and why this is not a laptop job

* **Training** shards experts across devices with expert parallelism and an
  all-to-all dispatch. Checkpoint frequently: the recipe below is a multi-day
  job on rented hardware.
* **Inference at batch 1** is the regime this whole study is about, and there
  the win is different from the training win: only the *selected* expert's
  weights must be read per token. If the active set is resident, throughput
  scales with `active_bytes`, not total bytes. That is the same mechanism as the
  resident-quantisation recommendation, applied to a sparser model.
* **Locality matters more than the textbook design.** A router free to pick any
  of 64 experts defeats prefetching. A hashed or otherwise locality-constrained
  router trades a little quality for a large bandwidth win -- worth prototyping.

## 8. Cost

Compute is approximately `6 × N_active × D` FLOPs. With `N_active ≈ 8.8B`:

| Continued-pretrain budget | FLOPs | ≈ A100-hours | ≈ cost at $2/hr |
|---|---:|---:|---:|
| 50B tokens (partial recovery) | 2.6e21 | ~4,900 | ~$10k |
| 500B tokens | 2.6e22 | ~49,000 | ~$100k |
| 2T tokens (~15% of a full pretrain) | 1.1e23 | ~196,000 | ~$390k |

These assume ~150 TFLOP/s effective per A100 and will move with model,
parallelism efficiency, and how much quality you insist on. The point is the
order of magnitude: **the real job is 10⁴-10⁵ GPU-hours.** A free tier is ~30
GPU-hours/week, so this is not a quota problem you can engineer around.

For contrast, the measured recommendation in `docs/decision_memo.md` -- quantise
below ~3.4 bpw so the model is fully resident -- was measured at **~8×** with
**zero** training. Any MoE retrofit has to beat that, not merely exist.

## 9. The gate: what would make me abandon this

The PoC decides it on free hardware. Kill criteria:

1. **At 0.8B, trained `oracle_topk` does not fall below the frozen pilot oracle**
   for the same (E, k). If training cannot create structure at small scale, there
   is no reason to believe 27B's continued pretraining will.
2. **The gap fails to shrink as E grows.** The mechanism should show up more
   strongly with more experts; if it does not, it is noise.
3. **`effective_experts` collapses** and no aux-loss coefficient fixes it.

If any of those hold, the retrofit route is dead and the answer is the one the
earlier work already found: **quantise resident** (free, measured ~8×), and if a
sparse model is genuinely wanted, **pretrain it MoE natively** rather than
converting this bottleneck.

### What the PoC actually found

**E14 — local RTX 4060, 4 layers, E=8 (the mild-sparsity control).**

| k | active | oracle untrained | oracle trained | frozen pilot | ppl untrained | ppl trained |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| 1 | 12.5% | 0.8584 | **0.8501** | 0.8624 | 112.0 | 33.8 |
| 2 | 25% | 0.7520 | **0.7383** | 0.7578 | 73.2 | 30.6 |
| 4 | 50% | 0.5638 | **0.5368** | 0.5710 | 48.9 | 28.4 |

Dense ppl 21.7. `beats_frozen_oracle = True` at every fraction, by 1.0-4.8%.

**E15 — free Kaggle T4s, all 24 layers, k=1, out to the ratio this design needs.**
This is the run that decides it, because §1 requires `E/k ≈ 35` and k=1 is the
config §3 recommends.

| E | active | frozen pilot oracle | oracle trained | router trained | beats frozen | ppl trained |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|:--:|---:|
| 8 | 12.5% | 0.8624 | **0.8560** | 0.8951 | yes, by 0.006 | 109.5 |
| 32 | 3.13% | 0.9188 | **0.9355** | 0.9653 | **no** (+0.017) | 159.3 |
| 64 | 1.56% | 0.9277 | **0.9549** | 0.9806 | **no** (+0.027) | 202.5 |

Dense ppl 19.562 (dense-limit check reproduces it at 19.573/19.592/19.587, so the
plumbing is exact). Seed 1 at E=64 gives oracle 0.9551 and router 0.9802 against
seed 0's 0.9549/0.9806 — the inversion is not noise.

**The gate fails as tested.** Criterion 1 holds at E=32 and E=64: trained `oracle_topk`
*rises* above the frozen pilot oracle, and does so by more as E grows — criterion 2
also points the wrong way. The router never gets close to the oracle, and the
oracle itself loses to simply keeping the frozen geometric split. The residual is
dominated by partial-sum scaling, exactly as §5 predicted: with `combine="sum"`,
keeping k of E roughly equal slices leaves about (1 − k/E) of the output no matter
how long you train. Training **adapts the function** rather than creating
extractable sparsity — `vs_original_dense`, the residual against the dense FFN the
experts were partitioned from, actually **rises** at every E (0.9324 → 0.9480 at
E=8; 0.9919 → 0.9974 at E=64) while perplexity falls three orders of magnitude.
E14's "residual ~0.96 at 1.6% active" was an extrapolation across k; E15 **measured
0.9549** there.

End to end, the best trained MoE (E=8, 109.5 ppl) is 5.6x dense and the target
ratio (E=64, 202.5) is 10.4x dense.

**Recommendation for *this project* stands: quantise resident; do not retrofit.**
The remaining looseness is the aux loss (33.2 of 64 effective experts,
`load_max_over_mean` up to 9.25 at E=64) and `combine="softmax"` — but neither can
explain a deficit that the *oracle* route also has, since the oracle needs no
router at all.

**However, E15 is not a fair test of the method this document designs, and the
recommendation above is about budget, not about feasibility.** `docs/prior_work.md`
reviews what the field actually did: upcycling works, at ~50% of the original
pretraining compute (Sparse Upcycling); LLaMA-MoE partitioned the FFN exactly as
§2 does and needed **200B tokens** where E15 used **614k**; NVIDIA got +2.3 MMLU
over continued dense training on 1T tokens. E15 also used `combine="sum"` without
the N/k rescaling §5 calls mitigation #1, and partitioned by weights where CMoE
shows the axis should be the **activation pattern** with a shared expert — which is
the published explanation for E15's router collapse. Treat §9's gate as satisfied
for *an untrained-partition initialisation*, not for upcycling.

## 10. Limitations of this design

* The expert counts assume the *compute-bearing* definition of active params;
  under strict accounting the goal is impossible and that should be said plainly
  to anyone who asks for "9B active".
* The 0.8B PoC trains on a small corpus (16.4 KB/step x 300 steps) and, in E15,
  all 24 layers of a 0.8B model. It tests the mechanism, not a finished
  conversion, and it cannot speak to whether recovery *improves* with scale —
  though it does remove the reason to assume it would, since the deficit grows
  with E rather than shrinking.
* Only `plan="random"`, `combine="sum"` was trained at the decisive ratios. The
  `clustered` and `interleaved` plans are frozen-only, and `combine="softmax"` was
  not trained at all.
* Cost figures are FLOP-based estimates with an assumed efficiency, not quotes.
* No benchmark suite or contamination analysis is included; quality claims would
  need `experiments/eval_gate.py` run at scale.

    "expert_parameters",
    "iter_moe_modules",
    "reconstruction_residual",
    "router_parameters",
    "set_top_k",
    "upcycle_model",
    "BranchConfig",
    "BranchMoEFFN",
]

__version__ = "0.1.0"


===== src/moeme/accounting.py =====

"""
moeme.accounting -- parameter/FLOP accounting for the Qwen3.8-27B geometry.

Purpose: make the central feasibility claim *executable and testable* rather
than an assertion in prose. The claim is that Qwen3.8-27B's non-FFN text
backbone is already ~9.78B parameters and is active on every token, so a
"9B active per token" target cannot be reached without retraining the backbone
itself -- routing the FFN is not sufficient.

All shapes below are taken from the verified artefact
(https://huggingface.co/Qwen/Qwen3.8-27B config.json, plus the safetensors
headers read by tools/verify_model_geometry.py). The test suite asserts that
the numbers computed here reproduce the measured weight-side totals.
"""

from __future__ import annotations

from dataclasses import dataclass


@dataclass(frozen=True)
class Qwen38_27B:
    """Verified geometry of the text tower of Qwen/Qwen3.8-27B (dense)."""

    hidden: int = 5120
    intermediate: int = 17408
    num_layers: int = 64
    vocab: int = 248320
    # 16 x (3 GatedDeltaNet layers + 1 full-attention layer)
    full_attention_layers: int = 16
    linear_attention_layers: int = 48
    # full attention (fused q+gate projection)
    attn_q_dim: int = 24 * 256          # heads x head_dim, doubled by the fused gate
    attn_kv_dim: int = 4 * 256
    attn_o_dim: int = 24 * 256
    # gated delta net
    gdn_qk_dim: int = 16 * 128
    gdn_v_dim: int = 48 * 128
    # MTP head and vision tower are present in the released checkpoint
    mtp_params_measured: int = 424_699_392
    vision_params_measured: int = 460_730_096

    # ---- measured reference values, for assertions ----
    measured_total: int = 27_781_427_952
    measured_ffn: int = 17_112_760_320
    measured_linear_attention: int = 5_562_044_928
    measured_attention: int = 1_677_721_600
    measured_embedding: int = 1_271_398_400
    measured_lm_head: int = 1_271_398_400

    # ----- per-component accounting -----
    def ffn_params(self) -> int:
        # swiGLU: gate, up, down
        per_layer = 3 * self.hidden * self.intermediate
        return per_layer * self.num_layers

    def attention_params(self) -> int:
        # q_proj is fused q+gate => 2x the query width
        per_layer = (
            self.hidden * (2 * self.attn_q_dim)
            + 2 * self.hidden * self.attn_kv_dim
            + self.attn_q_dim * self.hidden
        )
        return per_layer * self.full_attention_layers

    def linear_attention_params(self) -> int:
        # in_proj_qkv, in_proj_z, out_proj + small in_proj_a/b, conv1d, A_log, dt_bias
        per_layer = (
            self.hidden * (2 * self.gdn_qk_dim + self.gdn_v_dim)   # in_proj_qkv
            + self.hidden * self.gdn_v_dim                          # in_proj_z
            + self.gdn_v_dim * self.hidden                          # out_proj
            + 2 * self.hidden * self.gdn_num_heads                  # in_proj_a, in_proj_b
            + self.gdn_conv_dim * 4                                 # conv1d, kernel 4
            + 2 * self.gdn_num_heads                                # A_log, dt_bias (1 scalar/head)
        )
        return per_layer * self.linear_attention_layers

    def norms_params(self) -> int:
        """2 RMSNorms per layer (10,240 each) + the DeltaNet norms + final norm."""
        return self.measured_norms

    measured_norms: int = 674_816

    gdn_num_heads: int = 48

    @property
    def gdn_conv_dim(self) -> int:
        return 2 * self.gdn_qk_dim + self.gdn_v_dim

    def embedding_params(self) -> int:
        return self.vocab * self.hidden

    def lm_head_params(self) -> int:
        return 0 if self.tie_embeddings else self.vocab * self.hidden

    tie_embeddings: bool = False

    # ----- the decision-relevant totals -----
    def irreducible_active_params(self) -> int:
        """Params active on *every* token even if the whole FFN were free.

        This is the floor for the active-parameter count of any model that keeps
        the Qwen3.8-27B backbone untouched. Routing the FFN cannot go below it,
        so it is the hard limit on how small "active per token" can get.
        """
        return (
            self.embedding_params()
            + self.lm_head_params()
            + self.attention_params()
            + self.linear_attention_params()
            + self.norms_params()
        )

    def text_backbone_params(self) -> int:
        """Text tower only: FFN + attention + embeddings + norms.

        Equals the released text-tower parameter count (verified against the
        safetensors headers: 26,895,998,464).
        """
        return (
            self.irreducible_active_params() + self.ffn_params()
        )

    def total_with_aux(self) -> int:
        return self.text_backbone_params() + self.mtp_params_measured \
            + self.vision_params_measured

    def report(self) -> dict:
        irr = self.irreducible_active_params()
        ffn = self.ffn_params()
        text = self.text_backbone_params()
        return {
            "ffn_params": ffn,
            "attention_params": self.attention_params(),
            "linear_attention_params": self.linear_attention_params(),
            "embedding_params": self.embedding_params(),
            "lm_head_params": self.lm_head_params(),
            "irreducible_active_params": irr,
            "text_backbone_params": text,
            "total_with_aux": self.total_with_aux(),
            "ffn_share_of_text": ffn / text,
            "irreducible_over_9b": irr / 9e9,
            "measured_total": self.measured_total,
            "measured_ffn": self.measured_ffn,
        }


# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
# memory-tier sizing
# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------

def bytes_for_params(params: float, bits_per_param: float) -> float:
    """Bytes to store `params` at a given weight precision."""
    return params * bits_per_param / 8.0


class MemoryTier:
    """A residency tier with a measured bandwidth (bytes/second)."""

    def __init__(self, name: str, capacity_bytes: float, bandwidth_bps: float):
        self.name = name
        self.capacity_bytes = capacity_bytes
        self.bandwidth_bps = bandwidth_bps

    def seconds_for(self, nbytes: float) -> float:
        return nbytes / self.bandwidth_bps if self.bandwidth_bps > 0 else float("inf")


def predicted_decode_tok_s(
    *,
    active_params: float,
    bits_per_param: float,
    gpu: MemoryTier,
    ram: MemoryTier,
    disk: MemoryTier,
    kv_bytes: float = 0.0,
) -> dict:
    """Decode throughput ceiling for a memory-tier layout.

    Decode is bandwidth-bound: each token must read every active weight once.
    We fill tiers in preference order (GPU -> RAM -> disk) and take the slowest
    tier as the limiting factor, because tiers are traversed serially within a
    token. Overlap can improve on this; it cannot beat the slowest tier.
    """
    need = bytes_for_params(active_params, bits_per_param)
    remaining = need
    plan = {}
    for tier in (gpu, ram, disk):
        cap = max(0.0, tier.capacity_bytes - (kv_bytes if tier is gpu else 0.0))
        take = min(remaining, cap)
        if take > 0:
            plan[tier.name] = {"bytes": take, "seconds": tier.seconds_for(take)}
        remaining -= take
        if remaining <= 0:
            break
    total_s = sum(v["seconds"] for v in plan.values())
    return {
        "active_bytes": need,
        "placement": plan,
        "unplaceable_bytes": max(0.0, remaining),
        "seconds_per_token": total_s,
        "tok_s": (1.0 / total_s) if total_s > 0 else float("inf"),
        "bottleneck": max(plan, key=lambda k: plan[k]["seconds"]) if plan else None,
    }


===== src/moeme/branch.py =====

"""
moeme.branch -- the *branching* alternative to partitioning a dense FFN.

Why this module exists
----------------------
`moeme.upcycle` (and the pilot before it) answers "can a **disjoint slice** of a
trained dense FFN stand in for the whole thing?" and finds that it cannot: a
top-k partial sum leaves ~(1 - k/E) of the layer output, and the residual only
approaches zero as k -> E. That is a statement about slicing a sum, not about
MoE.

The obvious objection -- and it is the right objection -- is that real MoE
models (Mixtral, Switch, BTX) do not slice anything: their experts are
*complete* feed-forward networks. Activating one gives a whole layer's maths,
not a fraction of it, so there is no partial-sum deficit to begin with.

This module implements that alternative so the two can be measured against each
other on the same model, activations and metric:

    expert = a complete swiGLU FFN, initialised from the dense one, routed

The parameter that matters is **how much intermediate width the active set
covers**, `top_k * expert_units / intermediate`. The module makes it possible to
vary that while holding the *shape* of the construction (tiled vs replicated)
fixed, which is what distinguishes "slicing hurts" from "reading less hurts".

Two constructions, one width knob
---------------------------------
* `init="tile"`      expert j is initialised from the j-th disjoint block of
                     intermediate units. At `expert_units = I / E` this is
                     exactly the pilot's partition, and `sum` over all experts is
                     the dense layer. Total parameters stay at 1x the dense FFN.
* `init="replica"`   every expert is initialised from the *same* block. At
                     `expert_units = I` every expert is a complete copy of the
                     dense FFN, so `top_k = 1` reproduces the dense layer
                     exactly and the residual is 0 -- at the price of storing E
                     complete FFNs (E x the FFN parameters).

That contrast is the whole point: the lossless case is lossless *because it
reads full width*, and it is not sparse. Nothing here claims otherwise; the
module exists to let the claim be measured rather than argued.

Shared experts
--------------
`n_shared` always-on experts are added to every token's output. This is the
DeepSeek-MoE / CMoE idea: the units that are active for *most* inputs (and so
cannot be routed away) belong in an always-on block, leaving the router to
choose only among genuinely input-dependent experts. `unit_order` lets the
caller put high-activation units first, which is how that block is chosen in
practice.

A warning about `combine="sum"`, which is also `upcycle.MoEFFN`'s default
-----------------------------------------------------------------------
With `sum` the combination weights are a constant 1.0, so the router's only
influence on the output is the *discrete* expert choice -- and a discrete choice
carries no gradient. The router is then reached **only** by the load-balancing
auxiliary loss, never by the language-model objective. Measured on a probe: 6e-9
versus 2.8e-3 for `softmax`, i.e. ~5 orders of magnitude less signal.

So a `sum`-mode router is optimised for *balance*, never for *quality*, and a
router-underperforms-oracle result from a `sum` run says nothing about what a
properly differentiable router could do. `softmax` is the mode to train a router
in; `sum` is the mode to demonstrate exact dense equivalence in (it is the only
mode that keeps `top_k == E` an exact decomposition).

Layout matches Hugging Face: gate/up are (intermediate, hidden), down is
(hidden, intermediate), so `F.linear(x, W)` is the HF forward.
"""

from __future__ import annotations

from dataclasses import dataclass, replace

import torch
import torch.nn as nn
import torch.nn.functional as F
from torch import Tensor

COMBINE_MODES = ("sum", "mean", "softmax")
INIT_MODES = ("tile", "replica")


@dataclass(frozen=True)
class BranchConfig:
    """Configuration for one branched MoE layer."""

    n_experts: int = 4
    top_k: int = 1
    #: intermediate units each routed expert owns. None => the full width.
    expert_units: int | None = None
    init: str = "replica"
    n_shared: int = 0
    #: intermediate units each shared expert owns. None => `expert_units`.
    shared_units: int | None = None
    #: permutation of the intermediate axis; high-priority units first. Used to
    #: place the always-active units in the shared expert.
    unit_order: tuple[int, ...] | None = None
    combine: str = "sum"
    aux_loss_coef: float = 1e-2
    z_loss_coef: float = 0.0
    router_hidden: int | None = None
    init_noise: float = 0.0     # relative std added to the expert initialisation
    seed: int = 0

    def __post_init__(self) -> None:
        if self.n_experts < 1:
            raise ValueError("n_experts must be >= 1")
        if not 1 <= self.top_k <= self.n_experts:
            raise ValueError(
                f"top_k={self.top_k} must be in [1, n_experts={self.n_experts}]"
            )
        if self.combine not in COMBINE_MODES:
            raise ValueError(f"combine={self.combine!r} not in {COMBINE_MODES}")
        if self.init not in INIT_MODES:
            raise ValueError(f"init={self.init!r} not in {INIT_MODES}")
        if self.n_shared < 0:
            raise ValueError("n_shared must be >= 0")
        if self.init_noise < 0:
            raise ValueError("init_noise must be >= 0")

    def with_(self, **kw) -> "BranchConfig":
        return replace(self, **kw)


def _residual(reference: Tensor, got: Tensor) -> float:
    """Relative L2 error, the same metric the pilot and `upcycle` use."""
    ref = reference.detach().float()
    return float((ref - got.detach().float()).norm() / ref.norm().clamp_min(1e-12))


class BranchMoEFFN(nn.Module):
    """`n_experts` complete swiGLU FFNs with a top-k router, plus shared experts.

    Duck-types the parts of `MoEFFN`'s interface the experiment harness uses:
    `expert_outputs`, `router_scores`, `n_experts`, `last_stats`,
    `total_aux_loss()` and a `router` module.
    """

    def __init__(self, gate: Tensor, up: Tensor, down: Tensor,
                 config: BranchConfig):
        super().__init__()
        I, H = int(gate.shape[0]), int(gate.shape[1])
        if tuple(up.shape) != (I, H) or tuple(down.shape) != (H, I):
            raise ValueError(
                f"expected gate/up (intermediate, hidden) and down "
                f"(hidden, intermediate); got {tuple(gate.shape)}, "
                f"{tuple(up.shape)}, {tuple(down.shape)}"
            )
        self.config = config
        self.intermediate = I
        self.hidden = H
        self.E = config.n_experts

        U = I if config.expert_units is None else int(config.expert_units)
        if not 1 <= U <= I:
            raise ValueError(f"expert_units={U} must be in [1, intermediate={I}]")
        if config.init == "tile" and config.n_experts * U != I:
            raise ValueError(
                f"init='tile' needs n_experts*expert_units == intermediate, "
                f"got {config.n_experts}*{U} != {I}"
            )
        self.U = U

        if config.unit_order is not None:
            order = torch.as_tensor(config.unit_order, dtype=torch.long)
            if order.numel() != I or \
                    not torch.equal(torch.sort(order).values,
                                    torch.arange(I)):
                raise ValueError("unit_order must be a permutation of all "
                                 "intermediate units")
        else:
            order = torch.arange(I)
        self.order = order

        S = U if config.shared_units is None else int(config.shared_units)
        self.S = S
        if config.n_shared and not 1 <= S <= I:
            raise ValueError(f"shared_units={S} must be in [1, intermediate={I}]")

        # The dense layer's own weights, kept as the exact reference.
        self.register_buffer("dense_gate", gate.detach().clone(), persistent=False)
        self.register_buffer("dense_up", up.detach().clone(), persistent=False)
        self.register_buffer("dense_down", down.detach().clone(), persistent=False)

        def make_slices(n: int, width: int, tiled: bool) -> list[Tensor]:
            out = []
            for j in range(n):
                a = j * width if tiled else 0
                out.append(order[a:a + width])
            return out

        self.expert_slices = make_slices(self.E, U, config.init == "tile")
        self.shared_slices = make_slices(config.n_shared, S, tiled=False)

        # Stacked expert weights: (n_experts, units, hidden) / (n_experts, hidden, units)
        self.gate = nn.Parameter(self._stack(gate, self.expert_slices))
        self.up = nn.Parameter(self._stack(up, self.expert_slices))
        self.down = nn.Parameter(self._stack_down(down, self.expert_slices))
        if config.n_shared:
            self.s_gate = nn.Parameter(self._stack(gate, self.shared_slices))
            self.s_up = nn.Parameter(self._stack(up, self.shared_slices))
            self.s_down = nn.Parameter(self._stack_down(down, self.shared_slices))

        if config.init_noise:
            g = torch.Generator().manual_seed(config.seed)
            for p in (self.gate, self.up, self.down):
                p.data.add_(torch.randn(p.shape, generator=g) * config.init_noise
                            * p.data.std())
            if config.n_shared:
                for p in (self.s_gate, self.s_up, self.s_down):
                    p.data.add_(torch.randn(p.shape, generator=g)
                                * config.init_noise * p.data.std())

        if config.router_hidden:
            self.router: nn.Module = nn.Sequential(
                nn.Linear(H, config.router_hidden),
                nn.SiLU(),
                nn.Linear(config.router_hidden, self.E),
            )
        else:
            self.router = nn.Linear(H, self.E)
        self.router = self.router.to(device=gate.device, dtype=torch.float32)

        # Set to an expert index to bypass the router and send every token to
        # that expert at weight 1.0. This is the mechanism for **independent**
        # expert training (branch-train-miX stage 2): an expert can be trained
        # on its own data alone, before a router exists to choose between
        # experts. `None` means normal routed behaviour.
        self.force_expert: int | None = None

        self.last_aux_loss: Tensor = torch.zeros((), dtype=torch.float32)
        self.last_z_loss: Tensor = torch.zeros((), dtype=torch.float32)
        self.last_stats: dict = {}

    def set_force_expert(self, e: int | None) -> None:
        """Route every token to expert `e` (or restore routing with `None`)."""
        if e is not None and not 0 <= int(e) < self.E:
            raise ValueError(f"force_expert={e} out of range for E={self.E}")
        self.force_expert = None if e is None else int(e)

    # ---- construction ----------------------------------------------------

    @staticmethod
    def _stack(w: Tensor, slices: list[Tensor]) -> Tensor:
        return torch.stack([w[s].detach().clone() for s in slices])

    @staticmethod
    def _stack_down(w: Tensor, slices: list[Tensor]) -> Tensor:
        return torch.stack([w[:, s].detach().clone() for s in slices])

    @classmethod
    def from_hf_mlp(cls, mlp: nn.Module, config: BranchConfig) -> "BranchMoEFFN":
        missing = [n for n in ("gate_proj", "up_proj", "down_proj")
                   if not hasattr(mlp, n)]
        if missing:
            raise ValueError(f"mlp is missing {missing}; not a swiGLU MLP")
        return cls(mlp.gate_proj.weight.detach(),
                   mlp.up_proj.weight.detach(),
                   mlp.down_proj.weight.detach(), config)

    # ---- geometry --------------------------------------------------------

    @property
    def n_experts(self) -> int:
        return self.E

    @property
    def top_k(self) -> int:
        return min(self.config.top_k, self.E)

    @property
    def active_width_fraction(self) -> float:
        """Share of the intermediate axis the active routed set covers."""
        return self.top_k * self.U / self.intermediate

    @property
    def total_width_fraction(self) -> float:
        """Share of the dense FFN this layer *stores* (routed experts only)."""
        return self.E * self.U / self.intermediate

    @property
    def active_params(self) -> int:
        """Params read per token: shared experts + the top-k routed experts."""
        per_expert = 3 * self.U * self.hidden
        return (self.config.n_shared * 3 * self.S * self.hidden
                + self.top_k * per_expert)

    @property
    def stored_params(self) -> int:
        per_expert = 3 * self.U * self.hidden
        return (self.config.n_shared * 3 * self.S * self.hidden
                + self.E * per_expert
                + sum(p.numel() for p in self.router.parameters()))

    # ---- forward ---------------------------------------------------------

    def _expert_forward(self, x2: Tensor, gate: Tensor, up: Tensor,
                        down: Tensor, j: int) -> Tensor:
        h = F.silu(F.linear(x2, gate[j])) * F.linear(x2, up[j])
        return F.linear(h, down[j])

    def dense_forward(self, x: Tensor) -> Tensor:
        """The dense swiGLU layer this module was built from (exact reference)."""
        shape = x.shape
        x2 = x.reshape(-1, shape[-1])
        h = F.silu(F.linear(x2, self.dense_gate)) * F.linear(x2, self.dense_up)
        return F.linear(h, self.dense_down).reshape(shape)

    def router_scores(self, x: Tensor) -> Tensor:
        x2 = x.reshape(-1, x.shape[-1])
        return self.router(x2.float())

    def _shared_output(self, x2: Tensor) -> Tensor:
        y = torch.zeros_like(x2)
        for j in range(self.config.n_shared):
            y = y + self._expert_forward(x2, self.s_gate, self.s_up, self.s_down, j)
        return y

    def expert_outputs(self, x: Tensor) -> Tensor:
        """Every routed expert's output: (E, T, hidden). Routed experts only."""
        x2 = x.reshape(-1, x.shape[-1])
        return torch.stack([self._expert_forward(x2, self.gate, self.up,
                                                 self.down, j)
                            for j in range(self.E)])

    def _combine_weights(self, top_val: Tensor, combine: str,
                         dtype: torch.dtype) -> Tensor:
        k = top_val.shape[1]
        if combine == "sum":
            return torch.ones_like(top_val, dtype=dtype)
        if combine == "mean":
            return torch.full_like(top_val, 1.0 / k, dtype=dtype)
        if combine == "softmax":
            return torch.softmax(top_val, dim=1).to(dtype)
        raise ValueError(f"combine={combine!r}")

    def forward(self, x: Tensor) -> Tensor:
        shape = x.shape
        x2 = x.reshape(-1, shape[-1])
        if self.force_expert is not None:
            return self._forced_forward(x2, shape)
        logits = self.router(x2.float())
        k = self.top_k
        top_val, top_idx = torch.topk(logits, k, dim=1)

        y = self._shared_output(x2) if self.config.n_shared \
            else torch.zeros_like(x2)

        w_sel = self._combine_weights(top_val, self.config.combine, x2.dtype)
        for e in range(self.E):
            mask = top_idx == e
            tok = mask.any(dim=1)
            if not bool(tok.any()):
                continue
            ye = self._expert_forward(x2[tok], self.gate, self.up, self.down, e)
            w = (w_sel * mask.to(w_sel.dtype)).sum(dim=1).to(y.dtype)
            rows = torch.nonzero(tok, as_tuple=False).flatten()
            y.index_add_(0, rows, (ye * w[tok].unsqueeze(1)).to(y.dtype))

        self.last_aux_loss, self.last_z_loss = self._losses(logits, top_idx)
        with torch.no_grad():
            self.last_stats = self.routing_metrics(logits.detach(),
                                                   top_idx).as_dict()
        return y.reshape(shape)

    def _forced_forward(self, x2: Tensor, shape) -> Tensor:
        """Every token to one expert, weight 1.0, no router in the path.

        The router still runs (under `no_grad`) purely so the diagnostics stay
        populated, but it does not touch the output and no balancing loss is
        charged -- stage 2 exists to let experts *diverge*, and a load-balancing
        penalty would be pushing the wrong way.
        """
        e = self.force_expert
        y = self._shared_output(x2) if self.config.n_shared \
            else torch.zeros_like(x2)
        y = y + self._expert_forward(x2, self.gate, self.up, self.down,
                                     e).to(y.dtype)
        self.last_aux_loss = torch.zeros((), dtype=torch.float32)
        self.last_z_loss = torch.zeros((), dtype=torch.float32)
        with torch.no_grad():
            logits = self.router(x2.float())
            top = torch.full((x2.shape[0], 1), int(e), dtype=torch.long,
                             device=logits.device)
            stats = self.routing_metrics(logits, top).as_dict()
        stats["forced_expert"] = int(e)
        self.last_stats = stats
        return y.reshape(shape)

    # ---- losses / diagnostics -------------------------------------------

    def _losses(self, logits: Tensor, top_idx: Tensor) -> tuple[Tensor, Tensor]:
        T = logits.shape[0]
        E = self.E
        probs = torch.softmax(logits, dim=1)
        onehot = F.one_hot(top_idx, num_classes=E).to(probs.dtype)
        f = onehot.sum(dim=(0, 1)) / float(max(T * top_idx.shape[1], 1))
        P = probs.mean(dim=0)
        aux = E * (f * P).sum()
        z = torch.logsumexp(logits, dim=1)
        return aux, (z ** 2).mean()

    def total_aux_loss(self) -> Tensor:
        return (self.config.aux_loss_coef * self.last_aux_loss
                + self.config.z_loss_coef * self.last_z_loss)

    def aux_loss(self) -> Tensor:
        return self.last_aux_loss

    def routing_metrics(self, logits: Tensor, top_idx: Tensor):
        from .upcycle import RoutingMetrics
        T = logits.shape[0]
        E = self.E
        onehot = F.one_hot(top_idx, num_classes=E).float()
        usage = onehot.sum(dim=(0, 1)) / float(max(T * top_idx.shape[1], 1))
        u = usage.clamp_min(0)
        s = float(u.sum())
        p = (u / s if s > 0 else u).clamp_min(1e-12)
        eff = float(1.0 / (p ** 2).sum())
        mx = float(u.max() / u.mean().clamp_min(1e-12))
        ent = float(-(p * p.log()).sum() / torch.log(torch.tensor(float(E))))
        probs = torch.softmax(logits, dim=1)
        r_ent = float(-(probs * probs.clamp_min(1e-12).log()).sum(-1).mean()
                      / torch.log(torch.tensor(float(E))))
        return RoutingMetrics(usage.tolist(), eff, mx, ent, r_ent, int(T))

    # ---- measurement -----------------------------------------------------

    @torch.no_grad()
    def residual_report(self, x: Tensor, top_k: int | None = None) -> dict:
        """Faithfulness of the sparse layer to the dense function it replaced.

        All residuals are against the *original dense* output, which is the only
        reference that means the same thing for a tile and a replica. The
        harness's `oracle_topk_residual` compares against the sum of all
        experts, which equals the dense layer for a tile but is E x the dense
        layer for a replica, so it is not usable here.
        """
        # The experts may be bf16/fp16 while the captured activations are
        # float32; a matmul across the two dtypes raises. Match the weight
        # dtype for the forwards and promote to float32 only for the metric.
        x2 = x.reshape(-1, x.shape[-1]).to(self.gate.dtype)
        k = self.top_k if top_k is None else min(top_k, self.E)
        ref = self.dense_forward(x2).detach().float()
        outs = self.expert_outputs(x2).detach().float()       # (E, T, H)
        # Per-token error is used ONLY to rank which experts to keep; every
        # residual reported below is then the same global norm ratio, so the
        # numbers are directly comparable with each other and with the pilot's.
        # Mixing conventions here would make a tile-vs-replica table show
        # definitional differences as if they were architectural.
        #
        # Every `residual_*` below measures the **routed path only**, with the
        # always-on shared experts excluded. Shared experts contribute the same
        # additive term to all four, so including them would move the numbers
        # by an amount that has nothing to do with routing; their own
        # contribution is reported separately as `residual_shared_only`.
        err = (outs - ref.unsqueeze(0)).norm(dim=-1) / \
            ref.norm(dim=-1).clamp_min(1e-12).unsqueeze(0)
        order = err.argsort(dim=0)                           # (E, T)

        def take(n: int) -> Tensor:
            idx = order[:n].unsqueeze(-1).expand(-1, -1, outs.shape[-1])
            return outs.gather(0, idx).sum(0)

        report = {
            "residual_best_single_expert": _residual(ref, take(1)),
            "residual_oracle_topk": _residual(ref, take(k)),
            "residual_router": _residual(ref, self._routed_forward(x2)),
            "residual_all_experts_sum": _residual(ref, outs.sum(0)),
            "active_width_fraction": self.active_width_fraction,
            "stored_width_fraction": self.total_width_fraction,
        }
        if self.config.n_shared:
            report["residual_shared_only"] = _residual(
                ref, self._shared_output(x2).detach().float())
        return report

    def _routed_forward(self, x2: Tensor) -> Tensor:
        """`forward` without the always-on shared experts: the routed path."""
        logits = self.router(x2.float())
        top_val, top_idx = torch.topk(logits, self.top_k, dim=1)
        w_sel = self._combine_weights(top_val, self.config.combine, x2.dtype)
        y = torch.zeros_like(x2)
        for e in range(self.E):
            mask = top_idx == e
            tok = mask.any(dim=1)
            if not bool(tok.any()):
                continue
            ye = self._expert_forward(x2[tok], self.gate, self.up, self.down, e)
            w = (w_sel * mask.to(w_sel.dtype)).sum(dim=1).to(y.dtype)
            rows = torch.nonzero(tok, as_tuple=False).flatten()
            y.index_add_(0, rows, (ye * w[tok].unsqueeze(1)).to(y.dtype))
        return y


===== src/moeme/ffn.py =====

"""
moeme.ffn -- dense-equivalent FFN partitioning.

The core idea
-------------
A swiGLU FFN computes

    h = silu(x @ Wg^T) * (x @ Wu^T)        # h has shape (..., intermediate)
    y = h @ Wd^T                           # (..., hidden)

The intermediate axis is *separable*: if we split the intermediate index set
into disjoint groups g_0 .. g_{E-1}, then

    y = sum_e  ( silu(x @ Wg[g_e]^T) * (x @ Wu[g_e]^T) ) @ Wd[:, g_e]^T

holds **exactly**, because the down-projection is linear and sums over
intermediate units independently. That makes an "all experts active" partition
a mathematically exact decomposition of the dense layer -- the property this
module exists to exploit and to test.

An MoE drops the sum to a top-k subset, which is where approximation (and
quality loss) enters. Nothing here claims that dropping partitions preserves
quality: `oracle_topk_residual` measures the *best case* for a given k, which
is the number that must be checked before spending training compute.

Layout conventions match Hugging Face:
    Wg, Wu : (intermediate, hidden)
    Wd     : (hidden, intermediate)
so `nn.functional.linear(x, W)` gives the HF forward.
"""

from __future__ import annotations

from dataclasses import dataclass

import torch
import torch.nn.functional as F
from torch import Tensor


# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
# partition plans
# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------

@dataclass(frozen=True)
class PartitionPlan:
    """Assignment of every intermediate unit to exactly one expert."""

    assignment: Tensor  # int64, shape (intermediate,) -> expert id in [0, n_experts)
    n_experts: int

    def __post_init__(self) -> None:
        if self.assignment.ndim != 1:
            raise ValueError("assignment must be 1-D over the intermediate axis")
        ids = torch.unique(self.assignment)
        if ids.numel() != self.n_experts:
            raise ValueError(
                f"plan declares {self.n_experts} experts but uses {ids.numel()}"
            )
        if int(ids.min()) != 0 or int(ids.max()) != self.n_experts - 1:
            raise ValueError("expert ids must be contiguous and 0-based")

    @property
    def intermediate(self) -> int:
        return int(self.assignment.numel())

    def expert_indices(self, device: torch.device | None = None) -> list[Tensor]:
        """Intermediate indices owned by each expert (ragged sizes allowed)."""
        a = self.assignment if device is None else self.assignment.to(device)
        return [
            torch.nonzero(a == e, as_tuple=False).flatten()
            for e in range(self.n_experts)
        ]

    def sizes(self) -> list[int]:
        return [int(i.numel()) for i in self.expert_indices()]

    def covers_all(self) -> bool:
        idx = torch.cat(self.expert_indices())
        return int(idx.numel()) == self.intermediate and \
            bool(torch.equal(torch.sort(idx).values,
                             torch.arange(self.intermediate, device=idx.device)))


def _balanced_sizes(intermediate: int, n_experts: int) -> list[int]:
    """Split `intermediate` into n_experts sizes differing by at most 1."""
    if n_experts > intermediate:
        raise ValueError("cannot have more experts than intermediate units")
    base, rem = divmod(intermediate, n_experts)
    return [base + (1 if e < rem else 0) for e in range(n_experts)]


def contiguous_plan(intermediate: int, n_experts: int) -> PartitionPlan:
    """Consecutive blocks. Cheap, but splits correlated neighbours apart only
    at block edges, which is usually the *worst* case for oracle recovery."""
    assign = torch.empty(intermediate, dtype=torch.long)
    off = 0
    for e, s in enumerate(_balanced_sizes(intermediate, n_experts)):
        assign[off:off + s] = e
        off += s
    return PartitionPlan(assign, n_experts)


def interleaved_plan(intermediate: int, n_experts: int) -> PartitionPlan:
    """Round-robin: expert e owns indices e, e+E, e+2E, ..."""
    assign = torch.arange(intermediate, dtype=torch.long) % n_experts
    return PartitionPlan(assign, n_experts)


def random_plan(intermediate: int, n_experts: int, seed: int = 0) -> PartitionPlan:
    """Deterministic random assignment subject to the balanced sizes."""
    g = torch.Generator().manual_seed(seed)
    assign = torch.empty(intermediate, dtype=torch.long)
    off = 0
    for e, s in enumerate(_balanced_sizes(intermediate, n_experts)):
        assign[off:off + s] = e
        off += s
    assign = assign[torch.randperm(intermediate, generator=g)]
    return PartitionPlan(assign, n_experts)


def clustered_plan(gate: Tensor, up: Tensor, n_experts: int,
                   iters: int = 25, seed: int = 0) -> PartitionPlan:
    """Cluster intermediate rows of [Wg; Wu] with k-means.

    This is the interesting non-trivial plan: if intermediate units that behave
    similarly are grouped together, a top-k selection has a chance of capturing
    correlated mass, so oracle recovery can beat the contiguous baseline. We
    implement it exactly to be able to measure whether that hope is real.
    """
    if gate.shape[0] != up.shape[0]:
        raise ValueError("gate/up must share the intermediate axis")
    X = torch.cat([gate, up], dim=1).float()
    n = X.shape[0]
    if n_experts > n:
        raise ValueError("cannot have more experts than intermediate units")

    # generator must match X's device, otherwise torch.multinomial rejects it
    g = torch.Generator(device=X.device).manual_seed(seed)
    # k-means++ style spread init
    first = int(torch.randint(n, (1,), generator=g, device=X.device).item())
    centres = [X[first]]
    d = torch.cdist(X, centres[0][None]).flatten()
    for _ in range(1, n_experts):
        p = d.clamp_min(0) ** 2
        p = p / p.sum()
        nxt = int(torch.multinomial(p, 1, generator=g).item())
        centres.append(X[nxt])
        d = torch.minimum(d, torch.cdist(X, centres[-1][None]).flatten())
    C = torch.stack(centres)

    assign = torch.zeros(n, dtype=torch.long, device=X.device)
    for _ in range(iters):
        dist = torch.cdist(X, C)
        new_assign = dist.argmin(dim=1)
        if torch.equal(new_assign, assign) and _ > 0:
            assign = new_assign
            break
        assign = new_assign
        for e in range(n_experts):
            m = assign == e
            if bool(m.any()):
                C[e] = X[m].mean(dim=0)
    # repair empty clusters so the plan is valid
    for e in range(n_experts):
        if not bool((assign == e).any()):
            counts = torch.bincount(assign, minlength=n_experts)
            donor = int(counts.argmax())
            assign[int(torch.nonzero(assign == donor)[0])] = e
    # compact ids
    uniq, remap = torch.unique(assign, return_inverse=True)
    if uniq.numel() != n_experts:
        raise RuntimeError("clustering produced fewer distinct clusters than requested")
    return PartitionPlan(remap.to(torch.long), n_experts)


# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
# dense reference + partitioned forward
# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------

@dataclass
class DenseFFN:
    """A plain swiGLU FFN with Hugging Face weight layout."""

    gate: Tensor  # (intermediate, hidden)
    up: Tensor    # (intermediate, hidden)
    down: Tensor  # (hidden, intermediate)

    def __post_init__(self) -> None:
        if self.gate.shape != self.up.shape:
            raise ValueError("gate and up must have the same shape")
        if self.down.shape != (self.gate.shape[1], self.gate.shape[0]):
            raise ValueError(
                f"down must be (hidden, intermediate) = "
                f"{(self.gate.shape[1], self.gate.shape[0])}, got {tuple(self.down.shape)}"
            )

    @property
    def hidden(self) -> int:
        return int(self.gate.shape[1])

    @property
    def intermediate(self) -> int:
        return int(self.gate.shape[0])

    def forward(self, x: Tensor) -> Tensor:
        return self._forward_units(x, slice(None))

    def _forward_units(self, x: Tensor, idx) -> Tensor:
        gg, uu = self.gate[idx], self.up[idx]
        h = F.silu(F.linear(x, gg)) * F.linear(x, uu)
        return F.linear(h, self.down[:, idx])

    def params(self) -> int:
        return int(self.gate.numel() + self.up.numel() + self.down.numel())


class PartitionedFFN:
    """A DenseFFN viewed as `n_experts` experts under a PartitionPlan."""

    def __init__(self, dense: DenseFFN, plan: PartitionPlan):
        if plan.intermediate != dense.intermediate:
            raise ValueError(
                f"plan covers {plan.intermediate} units but FFN has "
                f"{dense.intermediate}"
            )
        self.dense = dense
        self.plan = plan
        # Plans may be built on the CPU (contiguous/interleaved/random) while the
        # weights live on the GPU. Index tensors must follow the weights.
        device = dense.gate.device
        self._indices = [i.to(device) for i in plan.expert_indices()]

    @property
    def n_experts(self) -> int:
        return self.plan.n_experts

    def expert_size(self, e: int) -> int:
        return int(self._indices[e].numel())

    def expert_params(self, e: int) -> int:
        s = self.expert_size(e)
        return 3 * s * self.dense.hidden

    def all_active_forward(self, x: Tensor) -> Tensor:
        """Sum over *every* expert -- must equal DenseFFN.forward to fp tolerance."""
        idx = self._indices[0]
        out = self.dense._forward_units(x, idx)
        for e in range(1, self.n_experts):
            out = out + self.dense._forward_units(x, self._indices[e])
        return out

    def expert_outputs(self, x: Tensor) -> Tensor:
        """Per-expert contributions, shape (n_experts, *x.shape[:-1], hidden).

        Materialising all of them is the *oracle* setting: a real router cannot
        afford this, which is precisely why the oracle number is an upper bound.
        """
        ys = [self.dense._forward_units(x, self._indices[e])
              for e in range(self.n_experts)]
        return torch.stack(ys, dim=0)

    def topk_forward(self, x: Tensor, topk: Tensor, weights: Tensor | None = None) -> Tensor:
        """Sum only the selected experts.

        topk    : (n_tokens, k) expert ids
        weights : (n_tokens, k) optional combination weights, default 1.0
        """
        if topk.ndim != 2:
            raise ValueError("topk must be (n_tokens, k)")
        if int(topk.max()) >= self.n_experts:
            raise ValueError("topk contains an out-of-range expert id")
        outs = self.expert_outputs(x)               # (E, T, hidden)
        E, T, H = outs.shape
        k = topk.shape[1]
        rows = torch.arange(T, device=x.device)
        # outs[e, t, :] for each (t, j) pair -> (T, k, hidden)
        vals = torch.stack([outs[topk[:, j], rows, :] for j in range(k)], dim=1)
        if weights is not None:
            vals = vals * weights.reshape(T, k, 1).to(vals.dtype)
        return vals.sum(dim=1)

    def dense_equivalent_error(self, x: Tensor) -> float:
        """Relative L2 error of the all-active partition vs the dense layer."""
        ref = self.dense.forward(x)
        got = self.all_active_forward(x)
        return float((ref - got).norm() / ref.norm().clamp_min(1e-12))


# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
# oracle + router-free diagnostics
# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------

def oracle_topk_residual(expert_outputs: Tensor, k: int) -> dict:
    """Best possible top-k relative residual.

    expert_outputs : (E, T, hidden) per-expert contributions
    Returns per-token relative L2 residual ||y - y_hat_k|| / ||y|| and summary
    statistics. This is the ceiling any learned router is chasing: if it is bad,
    no router can be good, and the design must change (or be trained).
    """
    E, T, H = expert_outputs.shape
    if not 1 <= k <= E:
        raise ValueError(f"k={k} out of range for {E} experts")
    # diagnostics run in float32 regardless of the activation dtype: bf16/fp16
    # residuals would both lose precision and break `quantile` (which requires
    # float or double)
    eo = expert_outputs.float()
    total = eo.sum(dim=0)                                   # (T, hidden)
    norms = eo.norm(dim=2)                                  # (E, T)
    topk = torch.topk(norms, k, dim=0).indices              # (k, T)
    mask = torch.zeros_like(norms, dtype=torch.bool)
    mask.scatter_(0, topk, True)
    kept = (eo * mask.unsqueeze(-1)).sum(dim=0)
    resid = (total - kept).norm(dim=1) / total.norm(dim=1).clamp_min(1e-12)
    return {
        "k": k,
        "n_experts": E,
        "active_fraction": k / E,
        "mean_rel_residual": float(resid.mean()),
        "median_rel_residual": float(resid.median()),
        "p90_rel_residual": float(resid.quantile(0.9)),
        "max_rel_residual": float(resid.max()),
        "rms_rel_residual": float(resid.pow(2).mean().sqrt()),
    }


def contribution_concentration(expert_outputs: Tensor) -> dict:
    """How concentrated is the per-expert output energy?

    If a trained dense FFN has no natural expert structure, energy is spread
    almost uniformly across partitions and top-k cannot recover it. Measured by
    the participation ratio (1 / sum p_i^2), the effective expert count, and the
    share held by the single largest contributor.
    """
    energy = expert_outputs.float().pow(2).sum(dim=(1, 2))  # (E,)
    p = energy / energy.sum().clamp_min(1e-30)
    pr = 1.0 / p.pow(2).sum().clamp_min(1e-30)
    return {
        "n_experts": int(expert_outputs.shape[0]),
        "participation_ratio": float(pr),
        "top1_energy_share": float(p.max()),
        "entropy_normalised": float(
            -(p * p.clamp_min(1e-30).log()).sum() / torch.log(
                torch.tensor(float(expert_outputs.shape[0]))
            )
        ),
    }


def train_linear_router(features: Tensor, targets: Tensor, n_experts: int,
                        steps: int = 400, lr: float = 0.05,
                        seed: int = 0) -> Tensor:
    """Fit a linear E-way router by cross-entropy on oracle top-1 labels.

    This is a cheap proxy for "what could a learned router extract from the
    hidden state alone, with no expert computation". It deliberately has no
    access to the per-expert outputs, so its accuracy is a lower bound on a
    properly trained (and much larger) router.
    """
    torch.manual_seed(seed)
    W = torch.zeros(features.shape[1], n_experts,
                    device=features.device, dtype=torch.float32,
                    requires_grad=True)
    b = torch.zeros(n_experts, device=features.device, requires_grad=True)
    feats = features.float()
    opt = torch.optim.Adam([W, b], lr=lr)
    for _ in range(steps):
        opt.zero_grad()
        loss = F.cross_entropy(feats @ W + b, targets)
        loss.backward()
        opt.step()
    return torch.cat([W.detach(), b.detach()[None]], dim=0)


def router_topk_residual(expert_outputs: Tensor, router_scores: Tensor,
                         k: int) -> dict:
    """Realistic (non-oracle) top-k residual using router scores.

    router_scores : (T, E) scores computed without seeing expert outputs.
    """
    E, T, H = expert_outputs.shape
    eo = expert_outputs.float()                             # float32 diagnostics
    total = eo.sum(dim=0)
    if router_scores.shape != (T, E):
        raise ValueError(f"router_scores must be (T,{E}), got {tuple(router_scores.shape)}")
    topk = torch.topk(router_scores, k, dim=1).indices       # (T, k)
    mask = torch.zeros(T, E, dtype=torch.bool, device=eo.device)
    mask.scatter_(1, topk, True)
    kept = (eo * mask.t().unsqueeze(-1)).sum(dim=0)
    resid = (total - kept).norm(dim=1) / total.norm(dim=1).clamp_min(1e-12)
    return {
        "k": k,
        "n_experts": E,
        "mean_rel_residual": float(resid.mean()),
        "median_rel_residual": float(resid.median()),
        "p90_rel_residual": float(resid.quantile(0.9)),
    }


===== src/moeme/upcycle.py =====

"""
moeme.upcycle -- turn a dense swiGLU FFN into a *trainable* top-k MoE.

Why this module exists
----------------------
`experiments/pilot_sparsity.py` measured the **no-retrain ceiling**: slicing a
*trained* dense FFN into E experts and keeping k of them loses most of the
layer output (Qwen3.5-0.8B layer 0, E=8, k=1: mean relative residual 0.746).
Nothing in that experiment touches any weight, so it is a ceiling for a
*frozen* partition -- and it says nothing about what gradient descent could do.

This module implements the thing the pilot deliberately did not: a partition
whose expert weights *and* router are trained. The two experiments together
answer the question that matters:

    can training beat the frozen ceiling, i.e. does a sparse partition become
    usable once it is allowed to specialise?

Design decisions, stated plainly (they are choices, not accidents)
------------------------------------------------------------------
* Experts are a **partition** of the intermediate axis (via `moeme.ffn`), not
  replicas. Total parameters therefore stay ~constant, which is what a
  "27B total / 9B active" target requires. It also means `top_k == n_experts`
  is an **exact** decomposition of the dense layer, so the dense model is a
  strict special case of this module and cannot be beaten at `top_k=E`.
* `combine="sum"` is the default. It keeps that exact limit *and* matches the
  semantics of `ffn.oracle_topk_residual` (a partial sum of expert outputs),
  so "trained vs frozen" is an apples-to-apples comparison. The price is that a
  sparse sum is scaled by ~k/E relative to the dense layer; training has to
  learn that scale. `combine="mean"` and `combine="softmax"` are provided and
  documented, but they do *not* preserve the exact limit at top_k=E.
* For the PoC the full weight matrices stay resident as `Parameter`s and the
  partition is a fixed buffer. This is deliberately **not** a memory-efficient
  MoE -- it exists to answer a quality question, not a memory question.
  Sharding/dispatch is a deployment concern, covered in
  `docs/upcycling_design.md`.

Layout matches Hugging Face: gate/up are (intermediate, hidden), down is
(hidden, intermediate), so `F.linear(x, gate)` is the HF forward.
"""

from __future__ import annotations

from dataclasses import dataclass, field

import torch
import torch.nn as nn
import torch.nn.functional as F
from torch import Tensor

from .ffn import (
    DenseFFN,
    PartitionPlan,
    clustered_plan,
    contiguous_plan,
    interleaved_plan,
    random_plan,
)

COMBINE_MODES = ("sum", "mean", "softmax")
PLAN_MODES = ("contiguous", "interleaved", "random", "clustered")


def build_plan(name: str, gate: Tensor, up: Tensor, n_experts: int,
               seed: int = 0) -> PartitionPlan:
    """Dispatch to the partition plans in `moeme.ffn`."""
    inter = int(gate.shape[0])
    if name == "contiguous":
        return contiguous_plan(inter, n_experts)
    if name == "interleaved":
        return interleaved_plan(inter, n_experts)
    if name == "random":
        return random_plan(inter, n_experts, seed=seed)
    if name == "clustered":
        return clustered_plan(gate, up, n_experts, seed=seed)
    raise ValueError(f"unknown plan {name!r}; expected one of {PLAN_MODES}")


@dataclass(frozen=True)
class MoEConfig:
    """Configuration for one upcycled MoE layer."""

    n_experts: int = 8
    top_k: int = 2
    combine: str = "sum"
    aux_loss_coef: float = 1e-2
    z_loss_coef: float = 0.0
    router_hidden: int | None = None   # None => a single linear router layer
    plan: str = "random"
    seed: int = 0
    expert_noise: float = 0.0          # relative std added to expert init

    def __post_init__(self) -> None:
        if self.n_experts < 1:
            raise ValueError("n_experts must be >= 1")
        if not 1 <= self.top_k <= self.n_experts:
            raise ValueError(
                f"top_k={self.top_k} must be in [1, n_experts={self.n_experts}]"
            )
        if self.combine not in COMBINE_MODES:
            raise ValueError(f"combine={self.combine!r} not in {COMBINE_MODES}")
        if self.plan not in PLAN_MODES:
            raise ValueError(f"plan={self.plan!r} not in {PLAN_MODES}")
        if self.expert_noise < 0:
            raise ValueError("expert_noise must be >= 0")

    def with_(self, **kw) -> "MoEConfig":
        from dataclasses import replace
        return replace(self, **kw)


@dataclass
class RoutingMetrics:
    """Load balancing / specialisation diagnostics for one forward pass."""

    usage_fraction: list[float]        # dispatch share per expert, sums to 1
    effective_experts: float           # participation ratio of `usage_fraction`
    load_max_over_mean: float          # 1.0 == perfectly balanced
    usage_entropy_normalised: float    # 1.0 == uniform dispatch
    router_entropy_mean: float         # 1.0 == router is maximally unsure
    n_tokens: int

    def as_dict(self) -> dict:
        return {
            "usage_fraction": self.usage_fraction,
            "effective_experts": self.effective_experts,
            "load_max_over_mean": self.load_max_over_mean,
            "usage_entropy_normalised": self.usage_entropy_normalised,
            "router_entropy_mean": self.router_entropy_mean,
            "n_tokens": self.n_tokens,
        }


# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
# the module
# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------

class MoEFFN(nn.Module):
    """A dense swiGLU FFN viewed as `n_experts` partition experts with a router.

    Drop-in replacement for a Hugging Face `*MLP` module: `forward(x)` takes the
    hidden states and returns the layer output. The auxiliary losses the
    training loop needs are stashed on the module (`last_aux_loss`,
    `last_z_loss`, `last_stats`) because HF decoder layers call `self.mlp(x)`
    with no channel to return them.
    """

    def __init__(self, gate: Tensor, up: Tensor, down: Tensor,
                 config: MoEConfig) -> None:
        super().__init__()
        if gate.shape != up.shape:
            raise ValueError("gate and up must share shape (intermediate, hidden)")
        hidden, inter = int(gate.shape[1]), int(gate.shape[0])
        if tuple(down.shape) != (hidden, inter):
            raise ValueError(
                f"down must be (hidden, intermediate)=({hidden}, {inter}), "
                f"got {tuple(down.shape)}"
            )
        if config.n_experts > inter:
            raise ValueError(
                f"n_experts={config.n_experts} exceeds intermediate={inter}"
            )

        self.config = config
        self.hidden_dim = hidden
        self.intermediate = inter

        plan = build_plan(config.plan, gate, up, config.n_experts, config.seed)
        # the plan is built on the CPU; the module's weights may already be on a
        # GPU, so the index buffer MUST follow the weights or index_select fails
        self.register_buffer("assignment", plan.assignment.to(gate.device).clone())

        if config.expert_noise > 0:
            g = torch.Generator(device="cpu").manual_seed(config.seed + 7919)
            def noisy(t: Tensor) -> Tensor:
                sd = t.float().std().item()
                n = torch.randn(t.shape, generator=g,
                                dtype=torch.float32).to(t.device)
                return (t.float() + n * (sd * config.expert_noise)).to(t.dtype)
            gate, up, down = noisy(gate), noisy(up), noisy(down)

        # Parameters keep the dense layout; experts are views onto row/column
        # subsets of them under `assignment`.
        self.gate = nn.Parameter(gate.detach().clone().contiguous())
        self.up = nn.Parameter(up.detach().clone().contiguous())
        self.down = nn.Parameter(down.detach().clone().contiguous())

        if config.router_hidden:
            self.router: nn.Module = nn.Sequential(
                nn.Linear(hidden, config.router_hidden),
                nn.SiLU(),
                nn.Linear(config.router_hidden, config.n_experts),
            )
        else:
            self.router = nn.Linear(hidden, config.n_experts)
        self.router = self.router.to(device=gate.device, dtype=torch.float32)

        self.last_aux_loss: Tensor = torch.zeros((), dtype=torch.float32)
        self.last_z_loss: Tensor = torch.zeros((), dtype=torch.float32)
        self.last_stats: dict = {}
        self._units_cache: list[Tensor] | None = None
        self._units_device: torch.device | None = None

    # ---- construction helpers -------------------------------------------

    @classmethod
    def from_dense(cls, dense: DenseFFN, config: MoEConfig) -> "MoEFFN":
        return cls(dense.gate, dense.up, dense.down, config)

    @classmethod
    def from_hf_mlp(cls, mlp: nn.Module, config: MoEConfig) -> "MoEFFN":
        """Upcycle an HF `*MLP` module (gate_proj/up_proj/down_proj)."""
        missing = [n for n in ("gate_proj", "up_proj", "down_proj")
                   if not hasattr(mlp, n)]
        if missing:
            raise ValueError(f"mlp is missing {missing}; not a swiGLU MLP")
        return cls(
            mlp.gate_proj.weight.detach(),
            mlp.up_proj.weight.detach(),
            mlp.down_proj.weight.detach(),
            config,
        )

    # ---- geometry --------------------------------------------------------

    @property
    def n_experts(self) -> int:
        return self.config.n_experts

    @property
    def top_k(self) -> int:
        return min(self.config.top_k, self.config.n_experts)

    @property
    def params(self) -> int:
        return (self.gate.numel() + self.up.numel() + self.down.numel()
                + sum(p.numel() for p in self.router.parameters()))

    def expert_units(self, e: int) -> Tensor:
        """Intermediate indices owned by expert `e`, on the weight device."""
        if self._units_cache is None or self._units_device != self.assignment.device:
            a = self.assignment
            self._units_cache = [
                torch.nonzero(a == i, as_tuple=False).flatten()
                for i in range(self.n_experts)
            ]
            self._units_device = self.assignment.device
        return self._units_cache[e]

    def expert_sizes(self) -> list[int]:
        return [int(self.expert_units(e).numel()) for e in range(self.n_experts)]

    # ---- forward ---------------------------------------------------------

    def dense_forward(self, x: Tensor) -> Tensor:
        """The dense swiGLU layer these experts partition (exact reference)."""
        shape = x.shape
        x2 = x.reshape(-1, shape[-1])
        h = F.silu(F.linear(x2, self.gate)) * F.linear(x2, self.up)
        return F.linear(h, self.down).reshape(shape)

    def _router_logits(self, x2: Tensor) -> Tensor:
        # router is kept in float32 for stable softmax/top-k
        return self.router(x2.float())

    def _route(self, x2: Tensor) -> tuple[Tensor, Tensor, Tensor]:
        logits = self._router_logits(x2)                     # (T, E) float32
        k = self.top_k
        top_val, top_idx = torch.topk(logits, k, dim=1)      # (T, k)
        return logits, top_val, top_idx

    def _combine_weights(self, top_val: Tensor, combine: str,
                         x_dtype: torch.dtype) -> Tensor:
        """Combination weights, always cast to the *activation* dtype.

        The router deliberately runs in float32 for stable top-k, so its outputs
        must be cast before they meet bf16/fp16 activations -- otherwise
        `index_add_` raises on a dtype mismatch (found the first time this ran on
        a bf16 model).
        """
        k = top_val.shape[1]
        if combine == "sum":
            return torch.ones_like(top_val, dtype=x_dtype)
        if combine == "mean":
            return torch.full_like(top_val, 1.0 / k, dtype=x_dtype)
        if combine == "softmax":
            return torch.softmax(top_val, dim=1).to(x_dtype)
        raise ValueError(f"combine={combine!r}")

    def _forward_impl(self, x: Tensor, k: int | None = None,
                      combine: str | None = None) -> tuple[Tensor, Tensor, Tensor]:
        """Returns (y, logits, top_idx). `k`/`combine` override the config."""
        shape = x.shape
        x2 = x.reshape(-1, shape[-1])
        logits = self._router_logits(x2)                     # (T, E)
        E = self.n_experts
        k = self.top_k if k is None else min(k, E)
        combine = combine or self.config.combine

        top_val, top_idx = torch.topk(logits, k, dim=1)      # (T, k)
        sel_w = self._combine_weights(top_val, combine, x2.dtype)

        y = torch.zeros_like(x2)
        for e in range(E):
            mask = top_idx == e                              # (T, k)
            tok = mask.any(dim=1)                            # (T,)
            if not bool(tok.any()):
                continue
            units = self.expert_units(e)
            xe = x2[tok]                                     # (Te, H)
            ge = self.gate.index_select(0, units)            # (Ie, H)
            ue = self.up.index_select(0, units)
            he = F.silu(F.linear(xe, ge)) * F.linear(xe, ue)  # (Te, Ie)
            de = self.down.index_select(1, units)            # (H, Ie)
            ye = F.linear(he, de)                            # (Te, H)
            # `.sum()` is on autocast's float32-promote list, so under autocast
            # this reduction comes back float32 even from bf16 inputs. Cast it
            # (and the product) back to the activation dtype or index_add_ raises
            # a dtype mismatch -- which is what happened on the first real run.
            w = (sel_w * mask.to(sel_w.dtype)).sum(dim=1).to(y.dtype)   # (T,)
            rows = torch.nonzero(tok, as_tuple=False).flatten()
            y.index_add_(0, rows, (ye * w[tok].unsqueeze(1)).to(y.dtype))

        return y.reshape(shape), logits, top_idx

    def forward(self, x: Tensor) -> Tensor:
        y, logits, top_idx = self._forward_impl(x)
        self.last_aux_loss, self.last_z_loss = self._losses(logits, top_idx)
        # diagnostics are pure logging: keep them off the autograd graph
        with torch.no_grad():
            self.last_stats = self.routing_metrics_from(
                logits.detach(), top_idx
            ).as_dict()
        return y

    # ---- losses ----------------------------------------------------------

    def _losses(self, logits: Tensor, top_idx: Tensor) -> tuple[Tensor, Tensor]:
        """Switch-style load-balancing aux loss, plus the router z-loss."""
        T = logits.shape[0]
        E = self.n_experts
        probs = torch.softmax(logits, dim=1)                  # (T, E)
        # dispatch fraction f_i: share of (token, slot) assignments on expert i
        onehot = F.one_hot(top_idx, num_classes=E).to(probs.dtype)   # (T, k, E)
        f = onehot.sum(dim=(0, 1)) / float(max(T * top_idx.shape[1], 1))  # (E,)
        P = probs.mean(dim=0)                                 # (E,)
        aux = E * (f * P).sum()
        z = torch.logsumexp(logits, dim=1)
        z_loss = (z ** 2).mean()
        return aux, z_loss

    def aux_loss(self) -> Tensor:
        return self.last_aux_loss

    def total_aux_loss(self) -> Tensor:
        return (self.config.aux_loss_coef * self.last_aux_loss
                + self.config.z_loss_coef * self.last_z_loss)

    # ---- diagnostics -----------------------------------------------------

    @staticmethod
    def routing_metrics_from(logits: Tensor, top_idx: Tensor) -> RoutingMetrics:
        E = int(logits.shape[1])
        T = int(logits.shape[0])
        onehot = F.one_hot(top_idx, num_classes=E).float()
        counts = onehot.sum(dim=(0, 1))                       # (E,)
        total = counts.sum().clamp_min(1.0)
        f = counts / total
        pr = 1.0 / f.pow(2).sum().clamp_min(1e-30)
        ent = -(f * f.clamp_min(1e-30).log()).sum() / torch.log(
            torch.tensor(float(E))
